Biomolecules 3
☆ AI-driven structural informatics reveals diverse glycosylation machinery across double-stranded DNA viruses
Lan Na
Glycosylation is widespread in biology and shapes molecular recognition, yet the diversity and biosynthetic organization of viral glycosyltransferases (GTs) remain poorly understood. Here we integrate structural-alphabet-guided searches and structure similarity networks with genomic-neighbourhood and biosynthetic gene cluster embedding analyses to examine viral GT-A proteins. This approach reveals extensive structural diversity overlooked by sequence-based annotation, including previously uncharacterized viral GT lineages. Most strikingly, we identified a jumbo-phage locus encoding five tandem glycosyltransferases alongside hydroxymethyl-pyrimidine biosynthetic and sulfation-associated enzymes, suggesting a previously unrecognized form of viral DNA modification involving a sulfated pentasaccharide. Beyond these examples, predicted saccharide-associated loci span divergent double-stranded DNA viruses, from giant viruses to tailed phages, and occupy multiple regions of the reference biosynthetic landscape. Together, our findings uncover an underappreciated repertoire of virus-encoded glycosylation machinery and suggest that viral glycan biosynthesis encompasses diverse molecular contexts and potential biological functions.
♻ ☆ Core physical principles of allostery
Eric Rouviere, Olivier Rivoire, Rama Ranganathan
Allostery is a basic property of proteins that represents the functional coupling of distantly positioned amino acids. In different manifestations, this property underlies signal transmission, cooperative binding, regulation, catalysis, and evolvability---the elementary features of molecular processes in living cells. While thermodynamic models have provided excellent abstract phenomenological descriptions and classifications of allostery, comparable descriptions and classifications at a more microscopic level, where the nature of physical interactions is specified, are lacking. Starting from minimal assumptions, we derive the essential physical principles of allostery and show that previously described forms of allostery can be viewed as specific implementations of a combination of two core principles: actuation of a soft mode and switching between two states. Organizing allostery by these principles may provide a simpler and more universal language to describe the physics of allostery in proteins.
♻ ☆ CMADiff: Cross-Modal Aligned Diffusion for Controllable Protein Generation
Changjian Zhou, Xinyue Zhang, Yuexi Qiu, Jiaqing Zhang, Heng-Da Cheng, Jia Song, Wensheng Xiang
AI-assisted protein design has emerged as a critical tool for advancing biotechnology, as deep generative models have demonstrated their reliability in this domain. However, most existing models primarily utilize protein sequence or structural data for training, neglecting the physicochemical properties of proteins.Moreover, they are deficient to control the generation of proteins in intuitive conditions. To address these limitations,we propose CMADiff here, a novel framework that enables controllable protein generation by aligning the physicochemical properties of protein sequences with text-based descriptions through a latent diffusion process. Specifically, CMADiff employs a Conditional Variational Autoencoder (CVAE) to integrate physicochemical features as conditional input, forming a robust latent space that captures biological traits. In this latent space, we apply a conditional diffusion process, which is guided by BioAligner, a contrastive learning-based module that aligns text descriptions with protein features, enabling text-driven control over protein sequence generation. Validated by a series of evaluations including AlphaFold3, the experimental results indicate that CMADiff outperforms protein sequence generation benchmarks and holds strong potential for future applications. The implementation and code are available at https://github.com/HPC-NEAU/PhysChemDiff.

QM 9
☆ A heterogenetic theory of living systems
Alessandro Sarti, Giovanna Citti
The problem of the dynamics of living systems and their mathematical representation essentially unfolds on two levels. Firstly, the principles of autopoiesis and operational closure describe how a living system produces and maintains itself. Secondly, living phenomena are recognised as historical, involving the evolution of dynamics and spaces of possibility that change over time. Currently, no differential model can incorporate both of these aspects. We propose adopting the differential heterogenesis framework, which enables us to define differential operators that change in space and time, producing event-based and historical dynamics. In this construction, heterogeneous operator assemblages will be constrained by the principles of autopoiesis and operational closure, providing a theoretical framework for event-based and historical living systems. Finally, we provide a specific example of assembly with Gray-Scott kinetic equations, along with numerical simulations and results.
☆ Learning developmental dynamics from lineage context
Gokul Gowri, Nic Fishman, William N. Colgan, Minming Wang, Yusuke Kijima, Sachin A. Kumar, Aleksandr Zimin, Luke W. Koblan, Marinka Zitnik, Zachary D. Smith, Stephen Bates, Philippe Rigollet, Jonathan S. Weissman
A central goal of biology is to predict the dynamics of multicellular systems. Lineage context (the states of a cell's sisters, cousins, etc.) could inform these predictions, but how best to leverage it remains unclear. Here we introduce lineage inpainting, a generative modeling approach that uses lineage context with tree-structured transformers and stochastic interpolants to model cell state dynamics across lineage trees. Applied to a lineage-traced transcriptional atlas of mouse embryogenesis, it recovers dynamics not resolved by gene expression alone, including multiple transcriptional convergence events where distinct lineages are drawn to a common state. Despite being fit on unperturbed development, lineage inpainting can generate hypotheses about perturbation responses by simulating counterfactuals. These simulations agree in many cases with observed developmental phenotypes, including a single-cell measurement of the Tbx5 knockout response. Our results show that lineage information provides a rich source of context for learning multicellular dynamics.
☆ Yoga biomechanics through energy optimization: diverse kinematics and low-dimensional kinetics
Sriram Sekaripuram Muralidhar, Manoj Srinivasan
Yoga is widely practiced for improving physical and mental health, yet the biomechanical demands of individual yoga poses remain poorly characterized. Existing experimental studies typically examine only a small number of poses or muscles, limiting their ability to guide pose selection for rehabilitation or exercise prescription. Here we present a computational framework for predicting whole-body joint torques and contact forces from two-dimensional images of yoga poses. The framework combines manual pose digitization, a sagittal-plane MuJoCo human model, and constrained metabolic energy minimization subject to static equilibrium. We applied the framework to 55 sagittally symmetric yoga poses from Light on Yoga, generating a dataset of joint kinematics, contact locations, and predicted joint torques and contact forces. These poses collectively span nearly the full physiological range of motion of the major joints while exhibiting a wide range of predicted joint torques, with the greatest average demands on the lower extremities and lumbar region. Principal component analysis revealed a striking contrast between kinematics and kinetics: nearly all ten dimensions were required to represent the joint-angle data, whereas the first three principal components captured over 90% of the variance in joint torques. Thus, yoga poses exhibit substantial diversity in body configurations but comparatively low-dimensional patterns of predicted joint loading. Predicted joint torque rankings were also largely insensitive to uncertainty in the relative weighting of joint metabolic costs. This framework enables systematic biomechanical analysis of yoga poses and provides a foundation for quantitatively evaluating their mechanical demands and informing future rehabilitation strategies.
☆ Agentic LLM reasoning in pediatric diabetes outcome prediction from multimodal medical records
Daniel Palacios, Guantong Qi, Anna Kadi, Natali Borrego, Sukru Aras, Michael L. Ferm, Elizabeth Kubota-Mishra, Maria J. Redondo, Zhandong Liu, Mustafa Tosur, Hu Chen
Large language model (LLM) agents are proposed for clinical prediction but are untested in pediatrics, where cohorts are small and outcomes are uncommon. Using records from 2,489 youth with type 2 diabetes (T2D) and 4,018 with type 1 diabetes (T1D), spanning structured data, note-derived social determinants and, in T1D, continuous glucose monitoring data, we predicted seven T2D outcomes, four also in T1D, at 24 months from 18 months of follow-up. CEDAR (Clinical Evidence-Driven Agent for Risk-prediction), an evidence-citing, self-auditing LLM pipeline, outperformed trained machine learning in both cohorts (pooled area under the receiver operating characteristic curve 0.815 versus 0.656 to 0.700 in T2D and 0.756 versus 0.603 to 0.631 in T1D), most on the sparsely screened outcomes. Only 4 of 70,246 values it cited did not match the chart. Reading only the evidence and reasoning cited by CEDAR, two pediatric endocrinologists reached the same prediction as CEDAR in about nine of ten cases, agreeing with each other on 90.5%.

☆ PCR-ALERA: A PCR-ELISA analogue but antibody-free DNA detection system
Miles Bonenberger, Roland Krämer
PCR-ALERA is a new concept for DNA detection that combines DNA amplification by polymerase chain reaction (PCR) with an activator-linked enzyme reactivation assay (ALERA). PCR-ALERA resembles the commercial, semi-quantitative PCR-ELISA assay that offers high sensitivity and high-throughput screening in microplates. PCR-ALERA overcomes a major drawback of PCR-ELISA since it does not require the time-consuming handling of expensive antibodies. In the first step of the assay, commercially available modified nucleotides were incorporated during PCR amplification of the target DNA. A selenocysteine containing peptide (the activator) was linked to the amplified DNA by click chemistry. This peptide has been optimized for the highly efficient activation of the latent protease papain by selenolate-triggered disulfide cleavage. The DNA-peptide hybrid was then immoblized in microplate wells and incubated with latent papain. The latter was readily activated and converted a fluorogenic substrate to a fluorescent product with high catalytic turnover. Overall assay time is about 4 hours including PCR.

☆ Beyond $χ^2$: Ensemble null tests for reliable scientific discovery
Adrian E. Bayer, David N. Spergel
Unrecognized systematic errors can survive standard checks and undermine scientific discoveries. Null tests and experimental controls can expose these failures, but averaging measurements or reducing them to scalar statistics can hide important structure. We propose ensemble null tests that compare multivariate distributions with references from observations or simulations. We demonstrate their benefits across science. In cosmic microwave background simulations, they detect systematic effects missed by conventional $χ^2$ tests. In Gaia data, they detect velocity asymmetry as sensitively as an informed search. In a CRISPR screen, they expose a control mismatch in DNA damage and reveal which cellular profiles drive it. As scientific datasets grow, ensemble testing turns repeated measurements into a broader search for unknown unknowns, strengthening the empirical foundation for scientific discovery.

☆ The Verification Wall in Microbial Metabolomics NeurIPS 2026
Takahiko Koyama
Computational methods now propose structures for gut microbial metabolites faster than any laboratory can confirm them. We argue that verification, not generation, is the binding constraint, and that better models will not relieve it. Confirming a structure to the field's own standard means having the molecule in hand. Automation can lower what that costs per compound, but the cost stays proportional to the number of structures and never amortizes across them. Two substitutes are on offer and neither removes that requirement. In-silico spectral prediction ranks candidates instead of confirming one, and the strongest current model puts the right structure first 33.5% of the time on a scaffold-split benchmark. Any assignment resting on spectral similarity is, by the field's own definition, made without a reference standard. Microcrystal diffraction does return a structure, but it needs the compound to crystallize out of a trace mixture, which is a different problem rather than an easier one. We sorted 10,645 computationally inferred metabolite structures by what verifying each would take. Between 5% and 37% have no verification route identified under our analysis. That spread reflects where the thresholds are set rather than a measured count of unverifiable compounds, and the inaccessible ones cluster among the most speculative entries. Those figures understate the problem, because the gut makes far more compounds than any database records. HMDB holds only what someone has already thought worth cataloguing, and structure generators are bounded by nothing at all. The claim is easy to disprove. Show a route that confirms structures as fast as they are proposed. Until someone does, two problems stay unsolved. Nobody knows how to spend a verification budget that will always be too small, or what a biologist may conclude from a structure that was never confirmed.
comment: 11 pages, 2 figures. Accepted at the AI for Science workshop (NeurIPS 2026). Code and data at https://github.com/tkoyama-keio/verification-wall-metabolomics
☆ Confidence Filtering in Video-Based Gait Assessment: Measurement Error versus Selection Shift
David Regev, Eran Beeri Bamani
Video-based gait assessment often keeps only high-confidence pose observations, which can lower measurement error but change the movement assessed. We compared confidence selection with budget-matched random selection in a Parkinson-related cohort (n = 96) and an NF2-related replication cohort (n = 18). In the Parkinson-related cohort, confidence selection produced a small but consistent shift in the observed movement summary (+0.05 SD, 95% CI 0.03 to 0.07), while held-out Mini-BESTest error stayed flat (-0.04 points, -0.13 to 0.05); a policy preserving motion-group proportions reduced the shift, while NF2 showed no shift and its predictor did not beat a constant baseline. Because this shift mixes measurement error with selection shift, we decompose and simulate both on the cohort's window structure. Filtering lowers local error when error depends on confidence, but biases the endpoint once confidence tracks movement more than error. In the Parkinson-related data, confidence was only weakly tied to movement (rho = 0.14). There, filtering is harmless if well-tracked windows are unbiased. If instead systematic error only redistributes within participants, filtering can add up to 0.58 degrees. Unchanged clinical error from such small-cohort predictors therefore cannot show that quality filtering preserves the assessed movement.
comment: 6 pages + 2-page appendix, 4 figures, 2 supplementary figures, 2 tables. Submitted to the 2027 IEEE International Conference on Rehabilitation Robotics (ICORR)
♻ ☆ gpuGEM: GPU-based linear optimisation of genome-scale metabolic models
Farid Zare, Timothy Hulshof, Ines Thiele, Ronan M. T. Fleming
Summary: Genome-scale metabolic models increasingly challenge CPU linear-programming solvers in repeated optimisation. We present gpuGEM, an open-source Python library for GPU-based optimisation using NVIDIA cuOpt, with size-adaptive settings validated against HiGHS and Gurobi. Across ten models spanning four orders of magnitude, gpuGEM achieved 1.1x to 94x speed-ups over the faster HiGHS method (dual simplex or interior point), and outperformed Gurobi on three models (1.6x to 2.5x). In candidate-gene prioritisation, gpuGEM reduced end-to-end query time from 41 s to 21 s. Availability and Implementation: gpuGEM is released under the MIT licence at https://github.com/Digital-Metabolic-Twin-Centre/gpuGEM and on PyPI (pip install gpugem); release v0.1.3 is archived at https://zenodo.org/records/23422813. It requires an NVIDIA GPU of compute capability 7.0 or newer (Volta onward), Python 3.11 or later, CUDA 12 and cuOpt 26.6 or later. Contact: ronan.mt.fleming@gmail.com Supplementary information: Supplementary data are available as an ancillary file on arXiv.

Biomolecules 5
☆ What to randomize matters: a controlled study of volume, axis and representation in low-data molecular generation
Neerav Kaushal, Alisa Teige, Ajay MNV Penmatsa
Chemical language models can struggle to generate new molecules when trained on small datasets, where a model may reproduce training examples rather than learn a useful generative distribution. Randomized SMILES augmentation varies atom traversal, whereas Sequential Attachment-based Fragment Embedding (SAFE) also allows the order of fragment blocks to vary. How these distinct sources of representational variation affect low-data molecular generation has not been clearly studied. Here, using 1,000 seed molecules to train an autoregressive sequence model, we compare canonical SAFE with randomized SAFE at increasing corpus sizes, isolate atom-order and fragment-order axes of SAFE randomization, and compare fully randomized SAFE with randomized SMILES at molecule-by-molecule matched training corpora. Three findings emerge. First, randomization of SAFE alone raises the genuine yield of novel molecules more than 450-fold, turning a model that merely reproduces its training set into a practical molecular generator. Second, the two axes of randomization act jointly rather than additively: atom order alone reaches a tenth, and fragment order alone a seventh, of the genuine yield obtained when both vary, which is four times their combined contribution. Third, at matched corpus size and compute, randomized SAFE delivers 2.8 times the genuine yield of randomized SMILES. Welch tests with Games-Howell post-hoc comparisons resolve every difference on the primary outcomes. In the low-data regime, thus, what one randomizes rather than only how much determines whether augmentation produces new molecules or merely new spellings of the training set.
comment: 27 pages, 9 figures, 5 tables
♻ ☆ Spontaneous Mutations from Terahertz Proton Tunneling
Noah Bray-Ali
Protons in the bonds between base pairs of the double helix store the code of life by breaking the dyad symmetry that swaps the sense strand with its complementary partner. When these bonds break during replication and transcription, pairs of protons switch sides, restoring dyad symmetry and destroying genetic information. The observed rate of such spontaneous mutations is obtained in the sudden approximation for bond breaking provided protons in bonds between bases tunnel across the gap with terahertz frequency.

♻ ☆ Bridging the Simulation-to-Experiment Gap with Generative Models using Adversarial Distribution Alignment
Kai Nelson, Tobias Kreiman, Sergey Levine, Aditi S. Krishnapriyan
A fundamental challenge in science and engineering is the simulation-to-experiment gap. While we often possess prior knowledge of physical laws, these physical laws can be too difficult to solve exactly for complex systems. Such systems are commonly modeled using simulators, which impose computational approximations. Meanwhile, experimental measurements more faithfully represent the real world, but experimental data typically consists of observations that only partially reflect the system's full underlying state. We propose a data-driven distribution alignment framework that bridges this simulation-to-experiment gap by pre-training a generative model on fully observed (but imperfect) simulation data, then aligning it with partial (but real) observations of experimental data. While our method is domain-agnostic, we ground our approach in the physical sciences by introducing Adversarial Distribution Alignment (ADA). This method aligns a generative model of atomic positions -- initially trained on a simulated Boltzmann distribution -- with the distribution of experimental observations. We prove that our method recovers the target observable distribution, even with multiple, potentially correlated observables. We also empirically validate our framework on synthetic, molecular, and experimental protein data, demonstrating that it can align generative models with diverse observables. Our code is available at https://kaityrusnelson.com/ada/.
♻ ☆ A Co-Design Approach to Physically-Informed Molecular Docking for Quantum Annealers
Francesco Micucci, Matteo Barbieri, Gabriella Bettonte, Domenico Bonanni, Anita Camillini, Anna Fava, Daniele Gregori, Andrea R. Beccari, Gianluca Palermo
Molecular docking is a crucial step in computer- aided drug design, involving the identification of ligand poses that are physically plausible within a protein pocket. Recent studies have shown that this problem can be formulated as a geometric weighted subgraph isomorphism, which can then be encoded as a QUBO and thus solved on D-Wave quantum annealers. In this work, we adopt a co-design approach, addressing molecular docking at the formulation and encoding levels, and examining how hardware topology affects solution quality on current quantum annealers. We propose new QUBO formulations that extend the state-of-the-art ones by including van der Waals, Coulomb, hydrogen bonding, and hydrophobic interactions. Experimental results show that incorporating physicochemical terms improves the average docking quality by more than 18% compared to the purely geometric baseline on D-Wave quantum annealers with the Pegasus topology, and by up to 29% on those with the Zephyr topology. The choice of topology, along with an improved encoding design, yields significant gains in embedding efficiency and the number of valid solutions. Our designs reduce the number of physical qubits required by up to 14.5% and decrease the chain break fraction by an order of magnitude, thereby enhancing confidence in the resulting solutions.

♻ ☆ Latent Style-based Quantum Wasserstein GAN for Drug Design
Julien Baglio, Yacine Haddad, Richard Polifka
The development of new drugs is a time-consuming and expensive process, with average costs estimated to be up to around $2.5 billion. The first step in this process is the design of the new drug, for which de novo drug design, assisted by artificial intelligence, has blossomed in recent years and has revolutionized the field. In particular, generative artificial intelligence has delivered promising results in drug discovery and development, reducing costs and time to solution. However, classical generative models, such as generative adversarial networks (GANs), can be difficult to train and are prone to mode collapse. Quantum computing may be an avenue to overcome some of these issues, providing compact models with far fewer trainable parameters, thereby enhancing the generalizability of GANs. We propose a new latent style-based quantum Wasserstein GAN (QGAN) for drug design, combining a variational autoencoder that maps molecular structures into a latent space with a hybrid quantum GAN. Our pipeline is benchmarked against classical models of the industry-relevant MOSES suite. Style-based noise reuploading at every rotational gate and a Wasserstein gradient penalty are used to improve trainability and to mitigate mode collapse. Training and large-scale sampling with circuits of up to 15 qubits are performed on noiseless quantum simulators to validate our architecture. We present an inference-only demonstration on an IBM Heron quantum computer using 5 qubits, sampling 2,500 new molecules, to investigate quantum noise effects. Our default QGAN, using 110 trainable generator parameters, reaches statistically comparable overall performance and statistically significant gains on selected drug-relevant metrics relative to both the MOSES classical latent GAN baseline, which contains about 700,000 trainable parameters for the generator, and a classical latent GAN with matched generator capacity.

QM 7
☆ Open-Source Framework for the Visualization, Quantification, and Analysis of 4D Flow and High-Dimensional Magnetic Resonance Images
Andras Lasso, Julia Iacovell, Csaba Pinter, Kevin K. Whitehead, Sehdev Pressley, Danish Vaiyani, Matthew Daemer, Katherine Hurm, Analise M. Sulentic, Christian Herz, Ryan T. Black, Seda Aslan, Christopher W. Roy, Mark A. Fogel, Jing Huang, Matthew A. Jolley
Background: Four-dimensional (4D) flow cardiovascular magnetic resonance (CMR) captures 3D anatomy and blood velocity across the cardiac cycle, but current post-processing software is closed, varies across platforms, and sits outside open-source image-computing frameworks. We developed an open-source framework for the import, visualization, and quantification of 4D flow and other high-dimensional CMR within SlicerHeart, an extension for 3D Slicer. Methods: Import 4D Flow reads vendor DICOM, applies preprocessing, and stores velocities as a time-resolved vector field; Analyze 4D Flow provides measurement planes, streamline and Doppler-like visualization, segmentation propagation, and quantification. Underlying both, we extended the Insight Toolkit to store five- and higher-dimensional arrays. Flow quantification was compared with a commercial package (cvi42) and 2D phase contrast in 13 patients with congenital heart disease across 108 paired regions of interest (Bland-Altman analysis and intraclass correlation coefficients, ICCs). Consensus-recommended processing methods were also implemented as an open-source library with an automated test suite of analytic and CFD phantoms and clinical acquisitions. Results: The modules integrate with 3D Slicer's segmentation, registration, and rendering tools and export velocity fields and geometry for computational modeling. Agreement with cvi42 was close: forward volume bias -0.1 mL (95% limits of agreement 2.2 to -2.3; ICC 0.997), effective volume per minute bias -0.015 L/min (0.21 to -0.24; ICC 0.994). Against 2D phase contrast, forward volume bias (-2.2 mL) and mean absolute percentage error (21.8%) matched cvi42 (-2.1 mL; 21.3%). Conclusions: This open, extensible framework brings 4D flow CMR into an established image-computing ecosystem and agrees closely with a commercial reference; every step can be inspected, reproduced, and improved.

☆ What to randomize matters: a controlled study of volume, axis and representation in low-data molecular generation
Neerav Kaushal, Alisa Teige, Ajay MNV Penmatsa
Chemical language models can struggle to generate new molecules when trained on small datasets, where a model may reproduce training examples rather than learn a useful generative distribution. ☆ The Mean First-Reaction Time on a Thin Filament in a 3-D Domain
Denis S. Grebenkov, Theodore Kolokolnikov, Alan E. Lindsay, Michael J. Ward
We study the mean first-reaction time (MFRT) of a Brownian particle in a bounded three-dimensional domain $Ω$ with a reflecting boundary $\partialΩ$, that contains a thin reactive filament. The filament is a tube of cross-sectional diameter $\varepsilon\ll 1$ around a smooth closed curve $Γ$, and its surface is partially reactive with reactivity $b$. As $\varepsilon\to 0$ the filament reduces to a line source in the outer problem. Its cross-section enters only through a logarithmic reactive capacitance $d_c(b)$, so that the MFRT is $\mathcal{O}(-\log[\varepsilon d_c])$. This contrasts with the $\mathcal{O}(\eps^{-1})$ scaling for spatially localized traps of radius $\eps$. Using matched asymptotic expansions, we derive a Fredholm integral equation on $Γ$ for the line density of the reactive flux. It sums all powers of $ν\equiv -1/\log[\varepsilon d_c]$ and involves two geometric quantities: the regular part $R$ of the Neumann Green's function, which encodes the interaction of $Γ$ with $\partialΩ$, and an intrinsic self-energy of $Γ$ that depends only on its shape. The framework extends to multiple filaments, splitting probabilities, a spatially heterogeneous diffusivity, and the principal eigenvalue of the Laplacian. For the unit sphere we solve the integral equation with a simple trapezoidal Nyström scheme, and we validate the predictions for rings, linked rings and torus knots against full 3-D finite-element solutions.
☆ Diagnosing parameter unidentifiability in stochastic dynamics with an application to flocking models
Natsuki Kurita, Hiroshi Kori
Although flocking behaviour has been studied extensively in active matter physics and mathematical biology, methods for assessing parameter estimability remain underdeveloped. For models formulated as stochastic differential equations (SDEs), we develop a Hessian-based diagnostic framework that detects unidentifiable directions and indicates whether, and how, estimation precision can be improved. The framework utilizes the submartingale structure of the loss function's Hessian and tracks its minimum eigenvalue over time. Under convergence of the time-averaged Hessian, we prove that this eigenvalue grows linearly for identifiable models, but only sublinearly, as $o(t)$, for unidentifiable ones; for a specific type of parameter redundancy, this $o(t)$ bound is tightened to $o(\sqrt{t}\log t)$. Applying the framework to stochastic flocking models, we demonstrate that, in identifiable settings, the magnitude of the eigenvalue reflects the attainable estimation precision. We further uncover a physical mechanism of unidentifiability: as the system approaches the ordered state, the parameters become algebraically degenerate, even though the model contains no trivial redundancy. Identifiability therefore depends on the dynamical regime, not only on model structure. Our framework offers a practical tool for reliable parameter estimation, not only in flocking models but in SDE models across diverse fields.
comment: 18 pages, 5 figures, Supplementary Information available as an ancillary file
☆ Who Called? V33DA: A Physically Verified Multimodal Benchmark for Vocal Attribution in Zebra Finch Groups NeurIPS 2026
Maris Basha, Yuhang Wang, Xiaoran Chen, Longbiao Cheng, Luca Yapura, Anja T. Zai, Mathieu Salzmann, Richard Hahnloser
Deciding which member of an animal group produced a vocalization and where that vocalization was produced are central to studying social communication but are rarely evaluated with physically verified ground truth. Existing resources either localize sounds without identifying the caller, or recognize individuals from isolated vocalizations without reliance on the 3D candidate geometry. We close this gap with V33DA, a dataset-centered multimodal benchmark for spatial vocal attribution in social zebra finches: it comprises 33,625 verified events coupling 5-channel audio with three camera views, calibrated 3D pose for every visible candidate, and per-bird radio telemetry across 10 individuals and three experiments. Caller identity is verified from an on-body accelerometer-derived vibration signal; this channel is withheld from benchmark models and used only by an on-body reference ceiling. Given a detected vocalization and the visible candidate birds, the task is to select the caller. We evaluate it under three regimes: session-disjoint testing on familiar birds from held-out recording dates, held-out-experiment transfer to a new cohort, and leave-one-individual-out evaluation on a bird whose vocalizations are held out from training. Reference methods expose a consistent gap between in-domain accuracy and transfer to unseen individuals: methods that can exploit familiar vocal identity perform well on known callers but degrade sharply on unseen ones, whereas candidate-aware methods preserving explicit spatial reasoning transfer substantially better. The on-body reference stays near-perfect across regimes, confirming the internal consistency of the accelerometer-verified labels. We release V33DA with fixed protocols and code for the evaluated methods, and V33DA++, an unevaluated companion set of verified overlapping-call events and +/-2 s context windows.
comment: Accepted at NeurIPS 2026 (Evaluations and Datasets Track). 43 pages, 3 figures. Dataset: https://huggingface.co/datasets/songbirdini/v33da ; Code: https://github.com/marisbasha/v33da
♻ ☆ Kinetic Proofreading Activation Times Beyond the Weibull Limit
Thomas Mourier, Cameron Hopkinson, Kenny Wong
T cells can detect a few foreign peptide-major histocompatibility complexes (pMHCs) among a far larger number of self pMHCs. Kinetic proofreading explains this discrimination: a receptor that has bound its ligand must complete nKP signaling steps, each at rate kp, before it activates, and dissociation at any step resets its progress. When many receptors are engaged at once, the time to the first activation has been approximated by a Weibull law taken from the short-time limit of this process. The Weibull law is convenient, but it does not contain the dissociation rate koff, even though koff is what proofreading uses to tell ligands apart. Using the exact first-passage law of the underlying continuous-time Markov chain together with stochastic simulations, we show that the relative error of the Weibull law is set by a single dimensionless number $Λ$, the Weibull-predicted activation time measured in units of nKP + 1 bound-state lifetimes. For $Λ$ < 1 the first receptor to activate does so before its ligand has ever dissociated, and the Weibull law holds. As $Λ$ grows, activation relies increasingly on repeated unbinding and rebinding. The true activation time then exceeds the Weibull prediction by up to 2900-fold on our parameter grid, and the effective Weibull shape falls to one, so activation becomes memoryless instead of timer-like. We derive a closed form that keeps koff by treating the successful pass through the proofreading chain separately from the time lost to failed attempts. It matches exact medians to within 2% in 19 of 25 parameter sets, with a worst case of about 6%, and reproduces the loss of timer-like behavior. Because $Λ$ is computed from the Weibull prediction itself, it shows in advance how far that formula can be trusted; where it cannot, the new expression requires only a one-dimensional root find.
comment: 20 pages, 4 figures, submitted to Physical Biology
♻ ☆ pyTransRate: reference-free quality assessment of de novo transcriptome assemblies with current aligners and quantifiers
Matthew MacManes
Summary: The original software TransRate scores a de novo transcriptome assembly by mapping the reads it was built from back to it, with no reference required. Its last release (1.0.3) depends on Ruby 2.2 and on aligner and quantifier versions that can no longer be installed on most systems. pyTransRate is a Python reimplementation of the TransRate method built on snap-aligner 2.0.5 and salmon 2.7.0. In 5,031 paired comparisons (39 assembly-level metrics on each of 129 public assemblies), it reproduces the original sequence metrics and read counts exactly and ranks assemblies consistently with 1.0.3 (Spearman rho = 0.94 for assembly score, 0.98 for good-mapping rate), although its read-based scores are not numerically interchangeable with the original. It also reads gzip- and bgzip-compressed input directly. Availability and Implementation: pyTransRate is implemented in Python 3 and is freely available under the MIT license at https://github.com/macmanes-lab/pytransrate. It runs on Linux and macOS; snap-aligner and salmon are installed from Bioconda. Supplementary information: Supplementary data are available online. Supplementary Table S1 lists the assembly and read accessions for every assembly analyzed; Supplementary Table S2 gives per-assembly values from both implementations.

☆ A unified coarse-grained neural network potential for the dynamics of biomolecular complexes
Antonio Mirarchi, Stephen E. Farr, Adrià Peréz, Gianni De Fabritiis
The behavior of biomolecular complexes is governed by chemically specific interactions and often involves rare events that atomistic molecular dynamics struggle to capture within accessible timescales. Here we introduce AMARO v2.0, a coarse-grained neural network potential that retains all heavy atoms and treats solvent implicitly. Hydrogen-aware bead types and total-charge conditioning provide a shared representation for proteins, drug-like molecules, and their complexes, including ternary protein-protein-ligand systems. The model preserves folded proteins and captures key features of protein conformational free-energy landscapes. Across 262 complexes spanning 11 targets, the learned potential retains most ligand poses, although quaternary structure reorientation is observed for the HIF2A ternary complex. In targeted case studies, AMARO v2.0 distinguishes the two protonation states of trypsin-benzamidine and reaches the crystal binding pose in 3.3-fold more trajectories than all-atom molecular mechanics at a fixed estimated GPU-time budget. The same framework also recovers a reference binding pose in TYK2 and supports binding free-energy estimation for both trypsin-benzamidine and TYK2-EJM31, although the resulting values are more energetically favorable than the experimental references. SHP2 and CDK8 systems expose remaining limitations in binding-pose recovery and site specificity, respectively. Together, these results support AMARO v2.0 as a shared heavy-atom potential for biomolecular complex dynamics.
☆ Accurate structural modelling of protein-glycan recognition with GlycoWeave
Huifeng Zhao, Jintu Zhang, Yifei Xiao, Youqiao Qian, Silong Zhai, Zichang Jin, Li Wang, Yuchao Wang, Hang Dong, Yifan Zhao, Zhenxing Wu, Hongyan Du, Yuansheng Huang, Bowei Zhao, Xiaorui Wang, Shukai Gu, Jike Wang, Zhongjun Li, Zhongtang Li, Tingjun Hou, Yu Kang
Protein-glycan recognition underlies diverse biological processes, yet accurate prediction of protein-glycan complex structures remains challenging owing to the chemical diversity, conformational flexibility and sparse experimental structural coverage of glycans. Here we present GlycoWeave, a glycan-specific diffusion framework that integrates chemically constrained conformational sampling, multiscale molecular representations and multi-source learning. By operating in internal-coordinate space, GlycoWeave preserves glycan connectivity and stereochemistry while efficiently exploring bound conformations. A heterogeneous equivariant network couples atom-level interaction geometry with monosaccharide-level structural context, whereas multi-source, multi-head training integrates experimental complexes with structures refined using classical and quantum mechanics-informed potentials. On a benchmark of 698 protein-glycan complexes, GlycoWeave achieves a 71.9% Top-25 high-quality success rate, compared with 19.2% for AlphaFold 3, while requiring only 0.34 s per sampled pose. GlycoWeave further preserves glycan structural validity, recovers native protein-glycan interactions and reproduces linkage-specific conformational preferences, maintaining high accuracy across diverse glycan architectures and protein environments. These results establish GlycoWeave as an accurate, efficient and chemically faithful framework for modelling protein-glycan recognition. GlycoWeave is freely available at https://cadd.zju.edu.cn/glycoweave.
♻ ☆ mdRNAcat: A Molecular Dynamics RNA Catalog of REST2 Conformational Ensembles
Anton E. Dorn, Emile de Bruyn, Fabrice von der Lehr, Philipp Knechtges, Stefan Kesselheim, Alexander Schug
Learning molecular models of RNA requires simulation data that capture conformational variability and provide consistent physical training signals. Here, we introduce mdRNAcat, a curated dataset of all-atom molecular dynamics (MD) simulations of 48 RNA systems selected to promote sequence diversity. Each system is simulated using replica-exchange with solute scaling (REST2), with 32 Hamiltonian states and production lengths of 200-300 ns per state. The dataset provides atomic coordinates, instantaneous forces, and forces reevaluated under the unscaled reference Hamiltonian, together with structural annotations and replica-exchange metadata. Coordinates and forces are available at 1 ps intervals for the reference state and 100 ps intervals across all states in a standardized HDF5 format. We characterize conformational sampling, exchange behavior, base pairing, and sugar-pucker distributions to document the properties of the simulated ensembles. mdRNAcat provides a reusable resource for developing and evaluating data-driven RNA models, including coarse-grained force fields.
comment: 24 pages, 9 figures. Dataset and accompanying resources available on Hugging Face under the HelmholtzAI-FZJ organization
♻ ☆ From static structures to dynamic landscapes: cryo-EM redefines RNA biology
Shekhar Jadhav, Spandan Saha, Qingbin Shang, Marco Marcia
RNA molecules perform diverse biological functions by dynamically exploring multiple conformational states rather than adopting a single static structure. Capturing these ensembles is a challenge in molecular biology. Recent advances in cryoEM are now transforming this landscape by enabling the visualization of RNA molecules across a broad spectrum of functionally relevant conformations at near atomic resolution. Here, we examine how cryoEM is reshaping RNA structural biology changing focus from the analysis of static structures to dynamic conformational landscapes. Through 8 representative case studies we illustrate how cryoEM has revealed previously inaccessible mechanisms of RNA motion, including folding processes, ligand-dependent switching, and cooperative assembly. We specifically discuss emerging experimental and computational approaches that address and overcome the challenges associated with studying dynamic RNAs, particularly in construct design, sample preparation, vitrification, and data analysis. These novel methods resolve conformational variability and enable the reconstruction of discrete and continuous RNA conformational landscapes from cryoEM data, highlighting how structural heterogeneity can be harnessed to extract functional insights. Looking forward, the integration of cryoEM with complementary biophysical techniques and time resolved methodologies promises to bridge structural and temporal resolution, to routinely derive experimental molecular movies of RNA in action. These advances will not only deepen our understanding of RNA biology but also provide new opportunities for RNA targeted therapeutics and the rational design of dynamic RNA-based nanodevices. By connecting structural snapshots into coherent dynamic models, cryoEM is establishing a framework for quantitative descriptions of RNA energy landscapes and their functional roles.
♻ ☆ Commutative Algebra Modeling of Mutation and Disease Association
JunJie Wee, Mushal Zia, Faisal Suwayyid, Hongsong Feng, Yuta Hozumi, Guo-Wei Wei
Genetic mutations can lead to disease by disrupting molecular interactions, altering protein stability, inducing misfolding, and promoting protein aggregation. Although existing predictive models have achieved promising performance, they often lack mechanistic interpretability and fail to fully incorporate the underlying physical and chemical principles governing mutation-induced effects. Moreover, current approaches typically address disease association, protein stability changes, and solubility alterations as separate prediction tasks rather than integrating them within a unified framework. In this study, we introduce a multiscale commutative algebra modeling framework for predicting disease-mutation association. Leveraging persistent Stanley-Reisner theory, we extract algebraic invariants to build two predictive models: Commutative Algebra Neural Network (CANet) and Commutative Algebra Gradient Boosting Tree (CATree). The proposed framework is applied to three biologically connected tasks: disease-associated mutation identification, mutation-induced protein stability change prediction, and mutation-induced protein solubility change classification. Across six benchmark tasks, CANet and CATree consistently achieve leading predictive performance, with up to 7.5% relative improvement over existing methods. These results demonstrate that commutative algebra provides a unified, interpretable, and generalizable modeling strategy for mutation-disease association prediction.

☆ HGPRGDel: GPR-Aware Learning and Ranking for Growth-Coupled Gene Deletion Design
Ziwei Yang, Takeyuki Tamura
Designing microbial strains for growth-coupled production, in which cellular growth is linked to the synthesis of a target compound through genetic interventions, is a fundamental goal in metabolic engineering. Constraint-based methods can identify growth-coupled deletion strategies, but design for each target typically requires a separate search across a large number of possible gene-deletion combinations. This motivates a reusable approach that learns from previously evaluated strain designs to prioritize candidate interventions before downstream constraint-based evaluation. We present a reproducible protocol for growth-coupled gene deletion design that learns and ranks gene-deletion priorities for each target metabolite. Using computationally curated deletion strategies as learning resources, the protocol generates continuous deletion-priority rankings, constructs candidate strategies at adjustable search-space budgets, and evaluates these candidates through constraint-based metabolic analysis. Its learning component, HGPRGDel, explicitly represents gene-protein-reaction (GPR) logic, capturing how multiple genes can jointly support a reaction or alternative genes can maintain its activity, while integrating molecular and reaction-level information. Across e_coli_core, iMM904, and iML1515, the protocol achieves the highest macro-F1 and AUC among the evaluated learning configurations. HGPRGDel's rankings recovered an average of 90% of the reference deletion genes from MetNetComp by examining fewer genes than the evaluated baseline methods. By separating learned intervention prioritization from downstream constraint-based assessment, the protocol provides a reproducible framework for reusing strain-design knowledge while controlling the size of the candidate space and retaining mechanistic evaluation of growth-coupled production.

☆ Stationary Action Principles for Neuronal Growth
Nathan Brodeur, Cristian Staii
Neuronal growth is guided by biochemical, mechanical, and geometrical cues while being influenced by stochastic fluctuations in cytoskeletal dynamics, adhesion, and growth cone sensing. Here, we develop two complementary variational descriptions of mechanically guided axonal growth. First, for neurons growing on periodic parallel micropatterns, we use Langevin and Fokker--Planck models to derive a stochastic path action incorporating deterministic substrate guidance and random fluctuations. The probability of a growth trajectory decreases exponentially with this action, and the most probable trajectories correspond to minimum action paths for fixed boundary conditions. Second, for axons growing on substrates with spatially varying stiffness, we show that stiffness dependent traction generates mechanical guidance and axonal turning at stiffness interfaces. In the quasi-static limit, this response leads to a Fermat-like spatial action with an effective axonal refractive index determined by the traction and substrate stiffness relationship. The resulting framework predicts axonal refraction across stiffness boundaries, including an approximate Snell-like relation and a critical angle condition. Although the stochastic and Fermat-like actions have distinct physical meanings and ranges of validity, both describe how substrate mechanics constrain axonal trajectories. These results connect measurable quantities, including drift, diffusion, stiffness, traction, and growth direction, to global properties of axonal paths and provide a framework for designing biomimetic substrates for controlled neuronal growth and regeneration.
comment: 28 pages, 6 figures
☆ A Differentiable, GPU-Accelerated Finite-Element Formulation in JAX for Large-Deformation Shells with Contact: Application to Patient-Specific Aortic Valve Simulation
Mohsen Nakhaei, Alison M. Pouch, Matthew A. Jolley, Wensi Wu
Patient-specific aortic-valve models can help assess how tissue properties and geometry influence leaflet deformation and coaptation, with potential to inform intervention planning. Large deformations, near-incompressibility, and evolving contact make these simulations computationally demanding and can challenge nonlinear convergence. Differentiable, GPU-accelerated methods offer efficient derivative evaluation and parallel computation, but their matrix-free application to soft-tissue shells with contact remains less established. We developed a forward solver using tatva's energy-based finite-element framework in JAX. The solver combines finite-strain extensible-director shells, hyperelastic constitutive models, and a finite-range repulsive contact energy. Nonlinear equilibrium is solved using a regularized, matrix-free Newton-Krylov method with two-level preconditioning. Automatic differentiation evaluates tangent-vector products without forming or storing the primary tangent matrix, while GPU execution parallelizes element and quadrature-point computations. Constitutive, shell, contact, and self-contact responses were assessed against analytical, semi-analytical, and published benchmarks. At a contact-free plate equilibrium, implicit sensitivities of the converged apex displacement to constitutive parameters, thickness, and pressure agreed with finite differences from independent nonlinear simulations. Simulations of three patient-specific aortic-valve models (two tricuspid and one bicuspid) achieved final relative equilibrium residuals below 10^-4. Median GPU runtimes were approximately 15-25 minutes, with 5.3-6.5-fold speedups relative to corresponding CPU runs on the tested hardware. These results provide component-level verification and demonstrate GPU-accelerated forward valve simulation in the tested cases, supporting future gradient-based inverse studies.
♻ ☆ A general-purpose multi-path stacked coupling for stochastic reaction networks
David F. Anderson, Jingyi Ma
Stochastic reaction networks are continuous-time Markov chain models for interacting populations, with applications in biochemistry, epidemiology, ecology, and related areas. Coupling multiple stochastic reaction networks effectively is a fundamental challenge in stochastic simulation. Existing variance-reducing coupling methods are typically designed for pairs of processes, though specialized multi-path constructions have been developed for particular applications. To fill this gap, we introduce the multi-path stacked coupling (MSC), a space-time Poisson construction that jointly simulates a finite collection of stochastic reaction networks with common reaction channels and reaction vectors, but possibly different intensity functions and initial conditions. Each pairwise marginal of MSC has the same law as the corresponding split-coupled pair, allowing existing variance bounds to transfer directly; in finite-state settings, we also obtain first-order expansions for the mean and second moment of finite-difference numerators. We demonstrate the framework through three sensitivity estimation tasks requiring several nearby parameterized paths: estimating many first derivatives simultaneously, estimating a single first derivative using a wider finite-difference stencil, and estimating higher-order derivatives. Numerical experiments on a processive phosphorylation network demonstrate strong performance in each of the three application areas considered, consistent with the theoretical advantages of MSC: across all three applications, MSC achieves the smallest root mean square error (RMSE) among the methods considered over the tested computational budgets.

♻ ☆ Latent Representation Learning from 3D Brain MRI for Latent Analysis and Prediction in Multiple Sclerosis
Trinh Ngoc Huynh, Nguyen Duc Kien, Nguyen Hai Anh, Dinh Tran Hiep, Manuela Vaneckova, Tomas Uher, Jeroen Van Schependom, Stijn Denissen, Tran Quoc Long, Nguyen Linh Trung, Guy Nagels
Neurological diseases can cause significant cognitive impairment, yet linking brain structure to cognitive function remains challenging due to the limitations of conventional methods and the black-box nature of deep learning. We propose InfoVAE-Med3D, an adaptation of InfoVAE for three-dimensional brain MRI that learns informative latent representations by relaxing the mutual information regularization penalty between inputs and latent representations. We evaluated the method on a healthy control (HC) dataset (\(n=6{,}503\)) with age and sex information and a multiple sclerosis (MS) dataset (\(n=916\)) with age, sex, and Symbol Digit Modalities Test (SDMT) scores. Two-dimensional visualizations revealed sex-related clustering and smooth transitions in grey and white matter volumes. Removing the mutual information regularization penalty generally improved MRI reconstruction and brain age and SDMT prediction using support vector regression, outperforming other variational autoencoder variants and achieving competitive performance with deep learning predictors, particularly on the MS dataset. InfoVAE-Med3D provides informative latent representations for investigating brain-related characteristics and supports latent-space analysis and imaging biomarker discovery for cognitive impairment, particularly in MS.

♻ ☆ Dynamic heart rate variability for classification of arrhythmia burden
Brian Kang, Michael Wacker, Steven S. Kang
Purpose: Heart rate variability (HRV) may provide information beyond heart rate (HR) for identifying arrhythmia burden, but the value of temporal HRV variation remains unclear. This study developed a dynamic HRV measure quantifying the interquartile range of window-level root mean square of successive RR-interval differences (IQRRMSSD) and evaluated its utility in classifying total abnormal-beat and premature ventricular contraction (PVC) burden compared with the conventional RR-based HRV measure, RMSSD. Methods: Manually annotated 30-minute ECG recordings from 43 individuals were analyzed using overlapping windows. A linear support vector classifier was used to classify individuals as having high or low arrhythmia burden, defined using 1% and 10% burden thresholds. Four feature sets were evaluated: HR alone, HR with RMSSD, HR with IQRRMSSD, and all three measures. Performance was assessed using repeated stratified five-fold cross-validation. Results: HRV-containing feature sets generally outperformed HR alone, depending on outcome and threshold. At the 1% threshold, IQRRMSSD showed particularly high sensitivity for total abnormal-beat and PVC burden ($0.970 \pm 0.064$ and $0.882 \pm 0.193$, respectively). At the 10% threshold, conventional RMSSD showed the strongest overall discrimination, achieving a ROC-AUC of 0.947 for PVC burden. Combined features sometimes improved the sensitivity-specificity balance but not overall discrimination. Conclusion: These findings suggest that a conventional RR-based HRV measure is informative for elevated ectopic burden, while a dynamic HRV measure may provide complementary information for lower-burden abnormalities.
♻ ☆ Video-rate quantitative phase and polarimetric imaging (QP2I) for label-free imaging of contracting muscle cells
Lucas Kreiss Roohian, Nachiket Pathak, David Ripp, Mitchell Harling, Nils M. Kronenberg, Marcel Schubert, Malte C. Gather
Quantitative phase imaging (QPI) has become a standard approach for label-free microscopy of transparent specimens, mapping the optical path length. However, the isotropic phase is blind to the molecular order underlying optical anisotropy. Implementations that also add quantitative contrast to birefringence rely on multi-frame illumination or polarization-state scanning, or on dedicated interferometric hardware and thus trade away acquisition speed or the simplicity of a standard microscope. We present Quantitative Phase and Polarimetric Imaging (QPPI), a computational imaging framework that converts a standard differential interference contrast (DIC) microscope with a polarization camera into a single-shot, video-rate, dual-contrast imaging system. We developed a new globally regularized phase reconstruction that eliminates artifacts of prior DIC-to-QPI approaches, providing quantitative phase information, and used per-pixel Stokes-based reconstruction to obtain quantitative anisotropic retardance. We validate the approach across simulations, static reference targets, fixed muscle tissue and living dynamically moving cells. QPPI enables all-optical and label-free studies of contractile dynamics in live cardiomyocytes. It provides both structural and functional readouts at sufficient frame rates to resolve fast cardiomyocyte contractions. Conventional fluorescent labels bleach over time, perturb physiology, and preclude long-term monitoring. Instead, QPPI enables long-term, label-free interrogation of dynamic cellular processes at quantitative contrast and provides structural readouts similar to those provided by established fluorescent labels, such as frame-by-frame sarcomere length. It also offers previously inaccessible measurements, like quantitative analysis of retardance changes during muscle contraction, which provides a direct optical signature of muscle photoelasticity.
♻ ☆ Multiscale MIGHT: Modeling Local and Systemic Particle Transport in the Cardiovascular System
Theofilos Symeonidis, Timo Jakumeit, Fardad Vakilipoor, Robert Schober, Maximilian Schäfer
Analytical models of signaling molecule (SM) transport in the cardiovascular system (CVS) force a choice between whole-body scope and vessel-level resolution: Pharmacokinetic models for systemic transport lump each organ and lack small-scale structure, whereas synthetic molecular communication (MC) channel models resolve branched vessel networks (VNs) only for a single pass or capture recirculation only in topologically simple loops, and only partially include SM degradation. To close this gap, we propose Multiscale MIGHT, an extension of the Mixture of Inverse Gaussians for Hemodynamic Transport (MIGHT) model that jointly captures branched topologies, closed-loop circulation, organ clearance, and chemical degradation of SMs. First, we connect the ends of a branched VN to form a closed loop with local and global SM degradation, model the closed-loop channel impulse response for arbitrary transmitter (Tx) and receiver (Rx) positions as a Laplace-domain feedback system, and approximate it in closed form by an inverse Gaussian mixture over multi-pass trajectories. The model is validated against finite-element simulations in COMSOL. Second, we set up a whole-body model, where an exemplary local VN, resolved at vessel-level detail, is embedded into the remaining systemic circulation, represented by lumped organ blocks. We simulate whole-body SM transport for various Tx sites and SM species. Our results show that, at an Rx in a local VN, only the initial pass produces a distinct peak in the SM concentration, significantly shaped by the local vasculature in case of small Tx-Rx distances; subsequent passes arrive strongly dispersed and are shaped almost entirely by organ clearance and chemical degradation rather than by the local VN topology.

♻ ☆ AI-guided deep visual proteomics reveals recurrence-associated morpho-molecular signatures within triple-negative breast cancers
Yesung Cho, Ji Hwan Park, Chan Il Kim, Hyewon Kim, Honglan Li, Yumin Lee, Geongyu Lee, Sujeong Hong, Seong Min Park, Yoonyoung Lee, Hee Sool Rho, Sumin Lee, Amos Chungwon Lee, Changhwan Lee, Hwanyoung Shim, Hyunwook Kim, Hyeji Shin, Sanha Park, Jihoon Yu, Yoon Hee Shin, Sooheon Kim, Hyunjin Park, Seung Min Park, Sangwan Kim, Yujung Kim, Jinwon Jeong, Sung-Im Do, Eun-Young Kim, Dongmyung Shin, Ingu Do, Jongbae Park
Outcome informed AI can localize prognostically relevant regions in digital pathology, but directly characterizing the molecular basis of these spatial predictions remains challenging. Here, we developed an outcome informed, AI-guided deep visual proteomics framework that uses recurrence-risk heatmaps to guide physical tissue sampling and mass spectrometry-based spatial proteomics in triple-negative breast cancer (TNBC). In 156 patients, high- and low-risk regions coexisted within the tumor compartment and showed distinct nuclear and architectural features, indicating intratumoral variation beyond broad tissue identity. Guided by these maps, we physically isolated and profiled 46 AI-defined high- and low-risk tumor regions from two recurrent cases. High-risk regions were enriched for mitotic programs, whereas low-risk regions showed immune- and antigen-presentation-associated programs. We used proteins that consistently differed between high- and low-risk regions to construct a 13-protein composite. The composite showed a significant effect to discriminate highly recurrent patients from less recurrent patients in an expanded TNBC cohort, while the corresponding transcript-based score showed a similar trend in METABRIC. Integrating the protein composite with the H&E-derived risk score improved out-of-bag discrimination, increasing the C-index from 0.679 to 0.739 and the 5-year time-dependent AUC from 0.675 to 0.770. These findings establish outcome-informed H&E risk maps as spatial guides for molecular discovery, extending prognostic AI from prediction toward direct biological interrogation of recurrence-associated tissue states.

♻ ☆ scTREAT: Latent Diffusion for Single-Cell Drug-Response Prediction
Zhaokang Liang, Shuyang Zhuang, Xiaoran Jiao, Weian Mao, Hao Chen
Generating drug-induced transcriptional responses requires a model to account for cellular background together with molecular identity and dose. We present scTREAT, the first diffusion framework to factorize state and drug guidance for single-cell drug-response prediction. A shared latent expression space connects baseline and response, while Guided Decomposable Attention (GD-Attn) injects a baseline representation and a dose-conditioned molecular embedding. Independent channel dropout exposes four conditioning states during training; inference combines three conditional predictions with separately specified state and drug coefficients, and dose modulates drug-guidance strength. On condition-level pseudobulk profiles derived from the Tahoe-100M single-cell atlas, scTREAT achieves the best value on 10 of 12 metrics for unseen cell line-drug combinations (UC) and 11 of 12 for unseen drugs (UD). Ablations examine condition injection, molecular representation, and guidance design. On UD, the relative gains over the second-best model reach 36.11% and 34.21% for DEG logFC-Spearman and logFC-Pearson. These results demonstrate the effectiveness of combining shared latent expression modeling with explicit state and drug conditioning for response prediction.

♻ ☆ Commutative Algebra Modeling of Mutation and Disease Association
JunJie Wee, Mushal Zia, Faisal Suwayyid, Hongsong Feng, Yuta Hozumi, Guo-Wei Wei

♻ ☆ Reading Between the Reads: A Decade On, Revisiting Sequencing Protocol Annotation in the Sequence Read Archive
Jamie J. Alnasir
Background: Experimental metadata are essential for interpreting archived sequencing data. In 2015, Alnasir and Shanahan examined the annotation of fragmentation, adapter ligation and enrichment in the Sequence Read Archive (SRA). This study revisits those measurements using the original SQL keyword definitions on a September 2026 NCBI metadata snapshot reconstructed in the historical SRAdb schema. Results: The contemporary database contained 41,593,302 experiments, compared with 419,620 in 2013. In library_construction_protocol, the proportion matching all three historical keyword groups declined from 4.06% to 1.42%. The protocol field was NULL in 67.65% of contemporary experiments. Searching design_description alongside the protocol field increased combined three-step keyword detection to 1,197,367 experiments (2.88%), with most additional matches occurring where the protocol field was NULL. Coverage varied across sequencing library strategies, ranging from 0.26% for AMPLICON to 7.69% for RNA-Seq. Independent comparison of 150 sampled experiment records against source XML confirmed agreement in all historical keyword classifications. Conclusions: The original criteria identify a smaller proportion of experiments in the designated protocol field than a decade earlier. Additional preparation-related terminology is detectable in general experiment descriptions, exposing the limitations of searches restricted to one metadata field. Differences among sequencing strategies complicate historical comparisons as preparation methods evolve. The findings support more consistent protocol annotation and the need to distinguish keyword occurrence from the adequacy of experimental documentation.

☆ Multiscale MIGHT: Modeling Local and Systemic Particle Transport in the Cardiovascular System
Theofilos Symeonidis, Timo Jakumeit, Fardad Vakilipoor, Robert Schober, Maximilian Schäfer

The model is validated against finite-element simulations in COMSOL. Second, we set up a whole-body model, where an exemplary local VN, resolved at vessel-level detail, is embedded into the remaining systemic circulation, represented by lumped organ blocks. We simulate whole-body SM transport for various Tx sites and SM species. Our results show that, at an Rx in a local VN, only the initial pass produces a distinct peak in the SM concentration, significantly shaped by the local vasculature in case of small Tx-Rx distances; subsequent passes arrive strongly dispersed and are shaped almost entirely by organ clearance and chemical degradation rather than by the local VN topology.
comment: 9 pages, 5 figures; Submitted to IEEE International Conference on Communications (ICC) 2027
☆ Escaping cells dilute CRISPR screen responses unequally across cell types
Xuanrui Huang
Single-cell CRISPR screens run in many cell types are used to compare how cell types respond to perturbation, to train models that transfer effects between them and to score such models. These uses assume that cells carrying a guide RNA had their target knocked down. Yet cells grow for days before sequencing: when an essential gene is knocked down, knocked-down cells are depleted or stop dividing while cells that escaped knockdown keep growing, and the measured response, an average over all cells carrying the guide, is diluted. We define the escape fraction of a perturbation as the share of its measured cells whose target was not effectively knocked down, and estimate it from each cell's counts of the targeted gene with a two-group mixture that recovers known fractions in simulated mixtures. In most cell types the escape fraction rises with how essential the gene is, it is higher in a later screen of the same cells, and it reaches about half of the measured cells in human embryonic stem cells (hESCs). It predicts how much each response is diluted: the response size falls to (1 - e)^2 of that of the knocked-down cells. Because the escape fraction differs between cell types, it changes all three uses: hESCs respond like K562 cells unless knockdown depletes them, the coefficient a simple transfer model learns is lowered by the dilution of the cell type it predicts, and a benchmark scored on all cells rewards predictions shrunk towards no change, partly because that ground truth is itself diluted. Where cells stop dividing instead, the response also contains expression differences between cell-cycle stages. Response sizes should be compared on knocked-down cells or corrected for the escape fraction.
comment: 23 pages, 6 figures; Supplementary Information and data files are included as ancillary files. Code: https://github.com/xuanruihuang/which-cells-are-measured
☆ Multivariate Augmented Prediction-Powered Survival Inference for EHR Association Studies With Limited Chart Review
Xicheng Xie, Katherine Fiona Simpson, Hyojung Jang, Theresa L. Walunas, Lili Zhao
Objective: To reduce bias and improve precision in survival association studies using electronic health record (EHR) data when computational phenotypes (CPs) for outcomes and covariates are error-prone and chart review is limited. Materials and Methods: We developed Multivariate Augmented Prediction-Powered Survival (MAPS) regression, which combines chart-reviewed and CP-derived labels across different validation patterns. MAPS uses a discrete-time survival model with a complementary log-log link to estimate hazard ratios (HRs). It incorporates data-adaptive augmentation weights, inverse-probability sampling weights, and patient-level cluster-robust variance estimation. We compared MAPS with chart-reviewed data (CRD) and CP-only analyses in simulation studies and a masked-validation study of immune-related adverse events (irAEs) among 1,026 patients receiving immune checkpoint inhibitors. Results: In the main simulations, MAPS produced approximately unbiased estimates and generally near-nominal 95% confidence interval (CI) coverage, with narrower intervals than CRD. CP-only estimates became increasingly biased as CP errors increased. In the EHR analysis, MAPS estimates closely agreed with fully validated estimates and improved precision over CRD, particularly at lower validation rates. CP-only analysis suggested increased irAE risk associated with pre-existing autoimmune disease (AID; HR=1.53; 95% CI, 1.12-2.09), whereas the fully validated analysis did not (HR=0.93; 95% CI, 0.67-1.29). Discussion: Precision gains depended on CP quality and validation rates, while small validation samples may affect confidence interval coverage. Conclusion: MAPS provides a flexible and efficient approach to EHR-based survival inference by combining limited chart-reviewed data with error-prone CPs across different validation patterns.

☆ Structure, Not Sequence: Tracking and Validating Folding Mechanisms in ESMFold's Trunk with Sparse Autoencoders
Bohan Liu, Xiaohan Fan, Wenqian Ye, Aidong Zhang
Structure predictors like ESMFold fold proteins using neural network computation and have transformed structural biology. However, the driving mechanism from amino-acid (AA) sequence to 3D structure is insufficiently understood, and biological meaning remains locked in the dense network. Sparse autoencoders (SAEs) have shown promising results in obtaining interpretable concept features over an overcomplete basis of intermediate protein sequence-model representations. Yet folding models, which predict physical shape from AA sequence semantics, have no such account. We hypothesize that the features carrying the folding computation evolve in the 3D structural regression process. In this paper, we interpret the folding trunk in three steps. First, a pilot analysis on the representation distribution across modules and recycles locates distinct spatial computation in the trunk. Second, the dictionaries expose a division of labor: the single representation carries inherited semantics, and the pair representation computes the pairwise geometry. Third, we interpret the dictionary at scale with a reasoning language model, trace how the concepts compose across blocks by gradient-based attribution, and follow the interpreted code to the model's folding output, verifying where the computation aligns with, or departs from, known biochemistry. Together, the sparse dictionaries we train capture the relational geometry the trunk computes beyond the sequence model, and their concept activations forecast the model's own fold quality, turning the black-box folding trunk into a sparse, interpretable, and scientifically verifiable circuit.
♻ ☆ Video-rate quantitative phase and polarimetric imaging (QP2I) for label-free imaging of contracting muscle cells
Lucas Kreiss Roohian, Nachiket Pathak, David Ripp, Mitchell Harling, Nils M. Kronenberg, Marcel Schubert, Malte C. Gather
Quantitative phase imaging (QPI) has become a standard approach for label-free microscopy of transparent specimens, mapping the optical path length. However, the isotropic phase is blind to the molecular order underlying optical anisotropy. Implementations that also add quantitative contrast to birefringence rely on multi-frame illumination or polarization-state scanning, or on dedicated interferometric hardware and thus trade away acquisition speed or the simplicity of a standard microscope. We present Quantitative Phase and Polarimetric Imaging (QPPI), a computational imaging framework that converts a standard differential interference contrast (DIC) microscope with a polarization camera into a single-shot, video-rate, dual-contrast imaging system. We developed a new globally regularized phase reconstruction that eliminates artifacts of prior DIC-to-QPI approaches, providing quantitative phase information, and used per-pixel Stokes-based reconstruction to obtain quantitative anisotropic retardance. We validate the approach across simulations, static reference targets, fixed muscle tissue and living dynamically moving cells. QPPI enables all-optical and label-free studies of contractile dynamics in live cardiomyocytes. It provides both structural and functional readouts at sufficient frame rates to resolve fast cardiomyocyte contractions. Conventional fluorescent labels bleach over time, perturb physiology, and preclude long-term monitoring. Instead, QPPI enables long-term, label-free interrogation of dynamic cellular processes at quantitative contrast and provides structural readouts similar to those provided by established fluorescent labels, such as frame-by-frame sarcomere length. It also offers previously inaccessible measurements, like quantitative analysis of retardance changes during muscle contraction, which provides a direct optical signature of muscle photoelasticity.
♻ ☆ Genotypic Triggers: Exposing Pharmacogenomic Blind Spots via Host-Specific Backdoors in Generative Antimicrobial Peptide Models
Doniyorkhon Obidov, Xiaolong Guo, Yonghui Li, Kaichen Yang
Large Language Models (LLMs) have accelerated drug discovery, particularly in the automated design of antimicrobial peptides (AMPs). ♻ ☆ Shared clones make random cell halves look reproducible in pooled screens
Xuanrui Huang
Single-cell perturbation screens are used to train and evaluate models of perturbation effects, and model scores are judged against the reproducibility of the measured effects. Reproducibility is usually judged by splitting each perturbation's cells into two random halves, assumed to be independent replicates. In pooled screens such as Perturb-seq, the cells carrying a guide RNA descend from a limited number of transduced cells, so two random halves contain the same clones: pseudoreplication whose unit is not marked in the data. Using null perturbations (no expected effect) as controls, we compared random halves with independent replicates from separate transductions, and tested directly with halves that differ only in their clones. In a genome-scale screen of primary T cells, null perturbations agreed between random halves almost always and between independent replicates only half the time, the rate expected from noise alone; more cells did not remove it. With halves that share no clone, split by the T-cell receptor constant region or by a clone barcode, the excess agreement largely disappeared. Under a simple model, consistent with a screen that recorded clones, its size equals the share of expression differences set by clones divided by the effective number of clones per perturbation; in cell lines an upper bound on it was small. In the T-cell screen, shared clones inflated benchmark ceilings and reliability labels, the more so in donors with fewer effective clones, and when a model's input and scoring answer come from one transduction, published models that predict effects in a new cell state scored well even on perturbations with no expected effect; for the model tested (STATE), the gain vanished with the same guide from another transduction as answer. Reproducibility is better judged across separate transductions than between random halves.

♻ ☆ MedEvo: Medical Reasoning Evolution through Knowledge Navigation
Xiaoliang Wang, Zhuo Chen, Chuanyang Zheng, Quanbin Wang, Jiangwei Lao, Yingao Liu, Jiajia Liu, Jinming Cao, Honglin Lin, Zhihong Lu, Hao Jin, Jian Wang, Jinjie Gu, Lijun Wu
Medical multimodal reasoning models are typically trained on teacher-generated rationales, discarding the valid reasoning in students' own failed trajectories, while general-domain correction methods offer little guidance for errors in image reading, anatomical context, or differential diagnosis. We propose MedEvo, which uses medical knowledge, namely a tag taxonomy and error-type diagnosis, to navigate both trajectory repair and data selection. After each round, MedEvo identifies capability gaps across medical tags on a held-out feedback set and prioritizes related questions for new rollouts and repairs. With supervised fine-tuning (SFT) alone, MedEvo raises Qwen3.5-9B mean accuracy across seven benchmarks from 65.57% to 69.12% (47.40% to 54.50% on the multimodal track of MedXpertQA (MedXpertQA-MM)), surpassing teacher-distillation SFT (66.93%). Our analyses show that failed trajectories contain reasoning worth preserving, that repair success does not imply learning value, and that independent feedback is key to sustained improvement.

♻ ☆ ECG-RSI: Recursive Self-Improvement through Targeted Electrocardiogram Waveform Evolution

♻ ☆ FRIGID: Scaling Diffusion-Based Molecular Generation from Mass Spectra at Training and Inference Time

☆ MechGate: combining mechanism-derived measures of permeation and efflux to screen small molecule drugs targeting Escherichia coli
Lang Qin, Jagath C Rajapakse
Most antibiotic molecules must cross the cell membrane and escape efflux pumps before hitting their targets. For de novo antibiotic design, very few of the in-silico-generated drug molecules can successfully pass these two steps. Existing evaluations of generated molecules do not separate these steps. Therefore, we introduce MechGate, a mechanism-resolved screening tool evaluating small drug molecules against E. coli, measuring permeation, efflux and antibacterial activity. After SMILES input, five measures are reported side by side: two for permeation via porins, one for permeation via the phospholipid membrane, one for efflux and one for activity. Each measure is read on its own, so a molecule that fails can be traced to the step that stops it. We also find that the four public source libraries are separable from one another using physicochemical features alone; every measure here therefore draws both classes from a single library. All code is open-source and requires no commercial software.
