☆ ProtAgent: Post-Training Compact Protein Tool Agents with Interaction-Level Credit Assignment
Yuxiang Wang, Baojian Zhou, Qifang Zhao, Keyue Jiang, Yanghua Xiao, Xiaoxiao Xu
Protein annotation agents must choose suitable bioinformatics tools, interpret their outputs, and use the resulting evidence in the final answer. Most existing systems rely on frontier-scale models. We ask whether a compact model can instead learn this behavior through post-training with real tool interaction. We present ProtAgent, a two-stage pipeline that uses supervised fine-tuning (SFT) to learn the tool interface and standard on-policy REINFORCE to improve the interaction policy. Intermediate tool calls receive action rewards, and the final response receives a terminal answer reward. Step-level returns assign these rewards to the responses that produced them and to preceding actions. The episode-level baseline instead assigns one episode return to every action. On a fixed closed-loop protocol with real bioinformatics tools, ProtAgent-8B achieves 61.00% success under the annotation-coverage metric, compared with 41.60% for DeepSeek-V3.2-Thinking 671B and 60.80% for the strongest evaluated frontier system. In the three-seed comparison, mean success is 42.70% with episode-level returns and 61.40% with step-level returns under identity return normalization; the corresponding group-relative results are 36.50% and 58.20%. The metric measures recovery of reference annotations under this protocol; it does not by itself establish biological correctness or complete evidence grounding.

☆ Multiscale Topology-Regulated Nonlinear Dynamics for Protein B-Factor Prediction and Single-Cell Type Classification
Zihan Wang, Mushal Zia, Xiaoqi Wei, Li Shen, Jian Jiang
Protein B-factor prediction and single-cell type classification are important for characterizing structural variability and cellular heterogeneity, with applications in biological function analysis and drug discovery. However, static geometric descriptors do not directly capture how multiscale connectivity shapes responses to local perturbations. Here, we introduce multiscale graph-coupled Burgers response learning (MGCBRL), a framework integrating algebraic topology and nonlinear dynamics to construct predictive biological representations. Specifically, a filtration converts protein coordinates or cellular principal component scores into weighted graphs at ten neighborhood scales. Independent unit impulses initiate node-specific responses governed by nonlinear Burgers dynamics. Statistical summaries or sampled source-node trajectories then encode the influence of structural neighborhoods on transient signal evolution. The simulated responses serve as computational probes rather than models of biological motion. For cross-protein prediction, multiscale response statistics are combined with spectral perturbation descriptors measuring changes in Laplacian spectra after node removal. For single-cell classification, principal component scores augment these features to retain explicit expression-space information, and the resulting descriptors support downstream supervised learning. Across 104 proteins and 13 single-cell datasets, MGCBRL achieved configuration-selected mean scores of 0.888 for within-protein Pearson correlation and 0.950 for transductive cell classification balanced accuracy, with leave-one-protein-out mean correlations of 0.496-0.558. Our framework provides a shared strategy for transforming multiscale biological connectivity into informative response descriptors, supporting predictive analysis of protein structures and transcriptomic profiles.
♻ ☆ Denoising Diffusion Variational Inference: Diffusion Models as Expressive Variational Posteriors
Yingheng Wang, Wasu Top Piriyakulkij, Volodymyr Kuleshov
We propose denoising diffusion variational inference (DDVI), a black-box variational inference algorithm for latent variable models which relies on diffusion models as flexible approximate posteriors. Specifically, our method introduces an expressive class of diffusion-based variational posteriors that perform iterative refinement in latent space; we train these posteriors with a novel regularized evidence lower bound (ELBO) on the marginal likelihood inspired by the wake-sleep algorithm. Our method is easy to implement (it fits a regularized extension of the ELBO), is compatible with black-box variational inference, and generally outperforms alternative classes of approximate posteriors based on normalizing flows or adversarial networks. We find that DDVI improves inference and learning in deep latent variable models across common benchmarks as well as on a motivating task in biology---inferring latent ancestry from human genomes---where it outperforms strong baselines on the 1000 Genomes dataset.

☆ Computational De Novo Design of Miniprotein Candidate Neutralizers Targeting the IL-23 Cytokine Subunit p19
Raghav Paladugu, Fardina Fathmiul Alam
Interleukin-23 (IL-23) drives T-helper-17 inflammation underlying inflammatory bowel disease and psoriasis; anti-p19 antibodies are clinically effective but large, costly, and injectable. De novo miniproteins are an attractive alternative, yet published designs target the IL-23 receptor; the IL-23 cytokine itself has not been addressed. A further obstacle is that the standard confidence score (ipTM) tracks construct length rather than interface quality. ☆ What Transfers Across Protein Pockets? Experience Reuse in Molecular Optimization for Agentic Structure-Based Drug Design
Zaifei Yang, Weiyu Chen, Yaqing Wang, James Kwok
LLM agents optimize a ligand in a protein pocket by editing it step by step and docking each edit. Recent agents first spend extra dockings probing the complex with controlled edits; guidance induced from these probes is reported to help more than the chemical knowledge the model already carries. The probes are then discarded with the instance that produced them, so the obvious next step is to keep them in an experience base, letting a new pocket inherit what earlier ones already paid for. Such a base is read through a \emph{key}: the record properties under which past edits are pooled and a new one is looked up. Using the full log of one such run over the CrossDocked2020 test-set pockets, we ask whether its keys recur on a pocket the base has never seen and still separate improving from worsening edits. \crchange{Using} the contacts at the site being edited, the action, and the round as \crchange{a} key, the base separates them sharply. Within our analysis, this separation is largely explained by how much the edit changed the molecule's size, the action, and the round, none of which describes the target. That is not because the target is irrelevant: naming it outright separates them several times more sharply. For a pocket absent from the base, transfer must rely on a description rather than its identity. Among the exact-match descriptions we evaluate, the more specific ones rarely recur, whereas the coarser ones provide little separation; the signals that do transfer primarily describe the edit and the molecule it produced rather than the target. Under the representations and lookup scheme studied here, an experience base may be more useful for prioritizing an agent's own proposals for docking than for directly prescribing what to build.
☆ Agent-guided epitope excision enables economical de novo binder design
Jun W. Kim, Artiom Myaskovsky, Oscar Donosa, Lawrence Chan, Jamie Rogers, Avinatan Hassidim, Yossi Matias, Annalisa Pawlosky
De novo protein design campaigns remain bottlenecked by the inference compute time required to generate and rank candidates, limiting wider functional exploration. Here, we introduce and evaluate an epitope excision approach that truncates targets to local interface windows to accelerate both backbone diffusion and structure prediction. Across 15 targets and 3,666 designs, excised windows closely reproduced full-target binding interfaces (median CαRMSD of 0.37 Å) and made it practical to design binders against the 719-residue extracellular domain of fibroblast activation protein alpha (FAP) by cutting RFdiffusion and AlphaFold3 GPU time by ~ 88% in one of the epitopes. Pairing this workflow with an agentic optimization loop allowed the system to autonomously identify catalytic-site targeting strategies. Under a fixed compute budget, a human-in-the-loop (HITL) workflow raised the computational hit rate from 5.06% to 9.33% while further cutting GPU hours by ~ 42%. We also found that omitting target multiple sequence alignments during refolding roughly substantially increased the apparent yield. Wet-lab validation with yeast display identified nanomolar binders from the generated designs with the HITL and the fully autonomous agent (human-out-of-the-loop, HOOTL) arms recovering more enriched designs than the PI-alone baseline. Together, target excision and agent-guided search offer a practical way to scale de novo binder design to large, therapeutically relevant proteins.

♻ ☆ Probe Before You Edit: Probing-Guided Molecular Optimization for LLM Agents in Structure-Based Drug Design NeurIPS 2026
Zaifei Yang, Weiyu Chen, Yaqing Wang, James Kwok
Structure-based drug design increasingly employs LLM agents to iteratively refine ligands against a target pocket, yet a viable ligand must satisfy two often-conflicting objectives---binding affinity and druggability---which single optimization steps rarely improve together. To quantify this difficulty, we introduce two diagnostic metrics: the first measures how often a single edit improves both objectives, and the second measures how often a gain on one objective comes with a loss on the other. Applying these diagnostics to current LLM-agent pipelines exposes a consistent failure mode: the agent performs molecular editing without knowing how the pocket-ligand complex responds to local modifications, thus rarely achieving joint improvement. Inspired by medicinal chemists, who probe the pocket-ligand complex with controlled analog edits before choosing an optimization direction, we propose \textbf{PROBE}, an optimization framework built around edit--response probing. PROBE first decomposes the ligand into editable sites and builds a pocket-specific \textbf{site map} that flags where joint gains are plausible, where the two objectives are likely in tension, and where liability substructures should be changed; it then performs controlled probe edits whose responses are distilled into an \textbf{EditManual}. Guided by the site map and EditManual, PROBE runs an iterative multi-agent optimization loop. On the CrossDocked2020 benchmark, PROBE achieves state-of-the-art performance and substantially mitigates the failure modes exposed by our diagnostics metrics. Codes are in https://github.com/yzf-code/PROBE/tree/main
comment: Accepted at NeurIPS 2026. Revised manuscript

☆ Shared clones make random cell halves look reproducible in pooled screens
Xuanrui Huang
Single-cell perturbation screens are used to train and evaluate models that predict perturbation effects, and model scores are judged against how reproducible the measured effects are. Reproducibility is usually judged by splitting each perturbation's cells into two random halves. This assumes that the halves are independent replicates. In pooled screens such as Perturb-seq, however, the cells carrying a guide RNA descend from a limited number of transduced cells, so two random halves contain the same clones: pseudoreplication whose unit is not marked in the data. Using perturbations that should have no effect as controls, we compared random halves with independent replicates from separate transductions, and tested directly with halves that differ only in their clones. In a genome-scale screen of primary T cells, null perturbations agreed between random halves almost always and between independent replicates only half the time, the rate expected from noise alone; measuring more cells did not remove this agreement. Splitting the cells into halves that share no clone, by the T-cell receptor constant region or by a clone barcode, largely removed the excess agreement. Its size equals the share of expression differences set by clones divided by the effective number of clones per perturbation; across 16 screens it was largest in T cells transferred into mice and in a genome-scale primary T-cell screen, one to two orders of magnitude smaller in cell lines, and smallest in non-dividing neurons. Shared clones inflate benchmark ceilings and reliability labels, and when a model's input and its scoring answer come from one transduction, published models that predict perturbation effects in a new cell state appear better than a baseline that ignores the perturbation; against cells from another transduction, most fall below it. Reproducibility should be judged across separate transductions.

☆ Video-rate quantitative phase and polarimetric imaging (QP2I) for label-free imaging of contracting muscle cells
Lucas Kreiss Roohian, Nachiket Pathak, David Ripp, Mitchell Harling, Nils Kronenberg, Marcel Schubert, Malte C. Gather
Quantitative phase imaging (QPI) has become a standard approach for label-free microscopy of transparent specimens, mapping the optical path length. However, the isotropic phase is blind to the molecular order underlying optical anisotropy. Implementations that also add quantitative contrast to birefringence, rely on multi-frame illumination or polarization-state scanning, or on dedicated interferometric hardware, that trade away acquisition speed or the simplicity of a standard microscope. We present Quantitative Phase and Polarimetric Imaging (QP2I), a computational imaging framework that converts a standard differential interference contrast (DIC) microscope with a polarization camera into a single-shot, video-rate, dual-contrast imaging system. We developed a new globally regularized phase reconstruction that eliminates artifacts of prior DIC-to-QPI approaches, providing quantitative phase information and used per-pixel Stokes-based reconstruction to obtain quantitative anisotropic retardance. We validate the approach across simulations, static reference targets, fixed muscle tissue and living dynamically moving cells. Thereby, QP2I enables to study contractile dynamics in live cardiomyocytes all-optically and label-free. It provides both structural and functional readouts at frame rates that are sufficient to temporally resolve fast cardiomyocyte contractions. While conventional fluorescent labels bleach out over time, perturb physiology, and preclude long-term monitoring, QP2I provides similar established structural readouts, such as frame-by-frame sarcomere length, as well as previously inaccessible measurements, like quantitative analysis of retardance changes during muscle contraction, as a direct optical signature of muscle photoelasticity. QP2I thus enables long-term, label-free interrogation of dynamic cellular processes at quantitative contrast.
☆ Automated screening of Parkinson's Disease using Smooth Pursuit Eye Movements
J. Camilo Puerta-Acevedo, Maria F. Alcala-Durand, Julián D. Arias-Londoño, Elisa Luque-Buzo, Francisco Grandas-Pérez, Juan I. Godino-Llorente
Smooth pursuit eye movements are frequently altered in Parkinson's disease, but previous studies have relied mainly on conventional parameters that describe the relationship between gaze and target. This study investigates whether complexity and nonlinear dynamics measures of the gaze position sequences can support the automatic screening of Parkinson's disease. Gaze position sequences were recorded with a video eye tracker in 41 participants with early Parkinson's disease and 47 healthy controls during 12 smooth pursuit tasks. Four experimental frameworks and four embedding delay strategies were compared. The selected experimental setting combined baseline wander removal with a fixed embedding delay. After feature selection, a support vector machine was trained for each task and axis and their outputs were combined in a mixture of experts. The pipeline was developed in 70 participants and evaluated on an external test set of 18 participants. On the external test set, the pipeline obtained an area under the receiver operating characteristic curve of 0.86. The most informative features described less persistent and more irregular gaze position sequences in Parkinson's disease, mainly along the horizontal axis. These results support complexity and nonlinear dynamics measures of smooth pursuit as candidate markers for Parkinson's disease screening, pending validation in larger independent cohorts.

♻ ☆ AI-guided deep visual proteomics reveals recurrence-associated morpho-molecular signatures within triple-negative breast cancers
Yesung Cho, Ji Hwan Park, Chan Il Kim, Hyewon Kim, Honglan Li, Yumin Lee, Geongyu Lee, Sujeong Hong, Seong Min Park, Yoonyoung Lee, Hee Sool Rho, Sumin Lee, Amos Chungwon Lee, Changhwan Lee, Hwanyoung Shim, Hyunwook Kim, Hyeji Shin, Sanha Park, Jihoon Yu, Yoon Hee Shin, Sooheon Kim, Hyunjin Park, Seung Min Park, Sangwan Kim, Yujung Kim, Jinwon Jeong, Sung-Im Do, Eun-Young Kim, Dongmyung Shin, Ingu Do, Jongbae Park

☆ RiboBridge: protein-conditioned de novo RNA binder structure design
Zhou Zhang, Hanqun Cao, Minghao Sun, Odin Zhang, Yang Zhang, Pheng Ann Heng, Wujun Li, Tianfan Fu
Designing RNA molecules that bind to target protein structures requires generating both an RNA conformation and its bound complex geometry, without assuming a predefined binding site. Existing approaches generally model these coupled processes within a unified representation, entangling RNA conformation generation with protein-relative placement. In this work, we introduce RiboBridge, a structure-first framework that separates RNA conformational generation from protein--RNA assembly through cross-frame interaction geometry. RiboBridge first proposes multiple candidate RNA-binding interfaces on the protein surface, generates canonical RNA conformations within a pretrained RNA structural latent space (RiboSphere), and then predicts protein--RNA interaction constraints to bridge the independently defined RNA and protein coordinate frames. The resulting interaction geometry is converted into a geometrically consistent protein-relative pose through rigid placement. We evaluate RiboBridge on a temporally split, sequence-filtered, and redundancy-controlled benchmark of 124 protein--RNA complexes. Under post-hoc interface-correspondence evaluation, where native information is used only after generation to select among already generated predicted-pocket candidates, RiboBridge achieves the highest fNAT and DockQ and the lowest interface and ligand RMSDs among compared methods. Component ablations and stage-wise diagnostics further characterize the contributions of RNA conformational generation, interaction prediction, and rigid placement, identifying the main sources of complex-recovery error.
☆ Supercoiling Drives DNA-Protein Co-Condensation
Sihan Liu, Hongbo Zhao, Andrej Košmrlj
The co-condensation of DNA and proteins plays an important role in genome compaction, including heterochromatin formation and chromosome segregation. Meanwhile, transcription and replication continuously generate DNA supercoiling, yet how the resulting torsional stress affects co-condensation remains poorly understood. Here, by coupling DNA elasticity to a protein concentration field, we show that supercoiling acts as a topological catalyst for co-condensation through a mutually reinforcing interplay with protein wetting. Specifically, protein wetting facilitates twist-to-writhe conversion, generating highly writhed DNA conformations that in turn promote capillary collapse into a protein-rich droplet. In the absence of supercoiling, DNA remains kinetically trapped in metastable extended states within our model, whereas sufficiently strong supercoiling removes the nucleation barrier and enables spontaneous co-condensation. These results establish topology as a distinct control parameter for biomolecular condensation and provide a mechanical framework for how active genomic processes can dynamically regulate large-scale genome organization.

☆ Behavioral Mapping of Hierarchical Dynamical Models Accelerates Bayesian Parameter Inference
Jingsong Bai, Ayush Pandey
Bayesian parameter inference for complex nonlinear dynamical models is often limited by the cost of repeated model simulation. We present a behavioral mapping framework that uses the inferred posterior of a higher-level model to accelerate inference of a lower-level model without requiring state or parameter correspondence between them. Building on approximate simulation relations for hierarchically related models, we define the notion of behavioral compatibility and quantify the behavioral support of detailed model parameters using the reduced model posterior. We learn a level set of behavioral support using a Gaussian process and use it to filter detailed model parameter proposals before expensive simulations during Markov Chain Monte Carlo (MCMC) inference. We derive conditions under which the behavioral support level set retains all detailed model parameters that fit the experimental data within a prescribed tolerance. We apply this method to a hierarchy of gene-expression models fitted to cell-free experimental data. After initial setup costs, our approach speeds up MCMC by 8.02% for the phenomenological model and 15.21% for the mechanistic model.
☆ Kinetic Proofreading Activation Times Beyond the Weibull Limit
Thomas Mourier, Cameron Hopkinson, Kenny Wong
T cells can detect a few foreign peptide-major histocompatibility complexes (pMHCs) among a far larger number of self pMHCs. Kinetic proofreading explains this discrimination: a receptor that has bound its ligand must complete nKP signaling steps, each at rate kp, before it activates, and dissociation at any step resets its progress. When many receptors are engaged at once, the time to the first activation has been approximated by a Weibull law taken from the short-time limit of this process. The Weibull law is convenient, but it does not contain the dissociation rate koff, even though koff is what proofreading uses to tell ligands apart. Using the exact first- passage law of the underlying continuous-time Markov chain together with stochastic simulations, we show that the relative error of the Weibull law is set by a single dimensionless number Λ, the Weibull-predicted activation time measured in units of nKP + 1 bound-state lifetimes. For Λ < 1 the first receptor to activate does so before its ligand has ever dissociated, and the Weibull law holds. As Λ grows, activation relies increasingly on repeated unbinding and rebinding. The true activation time then exceeds the Weibull prediction by up to 2900-fold on our parameter grid, and the effective Weibull shape falls to one, so activation becomes memoryless instead of timer-like. We derive a closed form that keeps koff by treating the successful pass through the proofreading chain separately from the time lost to failed attempts. It matches exact medians to within 2% in 19 of 25 parameter sets, with a worst case of about 6%, and reproduces the loss of timer-like behavior. Because Λ is computed from the Weibull prediction itself, it shows in advance how far that formula can be trusted; where it cannot, the new expression requires only a one-dimensional root find.

♻ ☆ Hodge-Decomposition of Brain Networks

☆ QUBO-Based Conformer Selection with Quantum-Classical Hybrid Optimization for Multiconformer Ligand Modeling
Jiyun Lee, Haeseong Kim, Junho Lee, Joonsuk Huh
A ligand can adopt multiple conformations within the same binding site, and these conformations can interact differently with the surrounding protein. Multiconformer modeling must therefore determine which candidate conformations to retain and what occupancies to assign to them. An established automated approach uses mixed-integer quadratic programming (MIQP) with hard occupancy and conformer-count limits for this selection. Here, we reformulated conformer selection as a quadratic unconstrained binary optimization (QUBO) problem with finite penalties and discrete occupancies. We solved the QUBO using a quantum-classical hybrid solver. We compared this workflow with the MIQP-based qFit-ligand workflow across 83 paired X-ray ligand entries using the same experimental inputs and the same qFit-ligand processing protocol. For each entry, both final models were evaluated against the same composite-omit map generated from the common input structure before the two workflows diverged. Quantum-qFit produced higher ligand-region real-space correlation coefficients (RSCC) in 62 entries 74.7% and lower occupancy-weighted torsional strain in 66 entries 79.5%. Similar directional trends remained in the 28 entries with matched final conformer counts. The molecular Electron Density for Individual Atoms score (EDIAm) decreased in most entries. This result shows that higher regional map agreement did not necessarily correspond to stronger atom-level density support. Overall, the results show that the conformer-selection formulation can change which ligand states are represented from the same experimental data and provide a QUBO framework for studying multiconformer ligand selection.

☆ Escaping the Phylogenetic Trap: Persistent Homology Enables Cross-Species Ion Channel Classification via Multimodal Graph Neural Networks
Arezoo Bybordi, Favour Achimba, Mariam Gelashvili, Jessy Ramirez, Weigang Qiu, Mandë Holford, Anita Raja
Protein classifiers are highly sensitive to evaluation protocol: random train/test splits induce phylogenetic leakage, allowing models to exploit taxon-specific correlations and substantially overestimate out-of-distribution performance. This issue is particularly acute for venom compound classification by ion-channel target (Ca2+, K+, Na+), where reported accuracies of 80-90% do not reflect generalization to unseen taxa. We formulate cross-species prediction as a structured distribution shift in which taxonomic identity acts as a confounder, requiring representations that capture structural features invariant across phylogeny. Using a shared multimodal backbone that fuses ESM-2 sequence embeddings with a disulfide-aware graph attention network, we systematically compare five structural inductive biases (spectral, Riemannian, SE(3)-invariant, statistical-manifold, and persistent-homology representations) under a leave-one-taxon-out evaluation protocol on a benchmark of 1,373 ColabFold-predicted venom compounds spanning five taxa. The persistent-homology representation summarizes backbone geometry through topological descriptors that are robust to local structural perturbations, providing an inductive bias toward phylogenetically invariant learning. Under conventional i.i.d. splits, all models achieve similar accuracy (81-86%), masking differences in inductive bias. Under taxonomic shift, this parity collapses: a sequence-only baseline drops to approximately 30%, the remaining geometric priors cluster at 54-55%, while our persistent-homology model (PH-GNN) achieves 63.4%, outperforming the next-best method by over 8 percentage points. These results demonstrate that random-split evaluation obscures meaningful differences in representation quality and identify topological representations as an effective inductive bias for learning under structured biological distribution shift.

☆ AI Mindmeld: A Latent Interconnect Protocol
Siavash Golkar, Jake Kovalic, Shirley Ho
LLMs can act as scientific agents using tools to consult specialists and run experiments. Yet specialist information must enter their context, generally as text, images, audio or video: a bottleneck between models with rich, high-dimensional representations. We advocate direct representation exchange: a mindmeld conveying many properties, including information an LLM would not think to request. It should be extensible to new specialists, flexible across questions and tasks, and compositional across independently connected specialists without joint training. The Latent Interconnect Protocol (LIP) learns connectors mapping specialist representations into LLM internal states. Training only connectors keeps this practical as LLMs grow and evolve. We call on specialist developers to provide consistent representation interfaces and, especially, on LLM developers to train models to receive, combine and reason with these inputs. ♻ ☆ Pushing Biomolecular Utility-Diversity Frontiers with Supergroup Relative Policy Optimization
Xinwu Ye, He Cao, Hao Li, Bin Feng, Zijing Liu, Xiangru Tang, Yu Li, Shenghua Gao
Biomolecular generators are often adapted with reward feedback to improve task-specific utility, but pushing utility alone can concentrate generation on a narrow family of candidates. Maintaining diversity is difficult because sample diversity is a set-level property. We introduce Supergroup Relative Policy Optimization (SGRPO), a flexible GRPO-style framework that directly constructs rewards from set-level diversity. For each condition, SGRPO samples a supergroup of candidate sets, compares their diversity under the same condition, and redistributes the group diversity reward to individual rollouts through leave-one-out diversity contributions before combining it with rollout-level utility. This design decouples SGRPO from a particular generator, utility reward, or diversity metric, and allows instantiation with different GRPO-style approaches. We evaluate SGRPO on three main settings: de novo small-molecule design, pocket-based small-molecule design, and de novo protein design, instantiating it with both GRPO and Coupled-GRPO across autoregressive and discrete diffusion generators. Across decoding sweeps, SGRPO expands the utility-diversity Pareto frontier and achieves the best frontier-level metrics relative to baselines. Our analyses further show that direct set-level diversity rewards remain effective with small groups and help preserve broader generation-distribution coverage during post-training. The code is available at https://github.com/IDEA-XL/SGRPO.
♻ ☆ Advances in Machine Learning for Directed Evolution: A Five-Year Retrospective
Bruce J. Wittmann
The last five-plus years have seen many protein engineering disciplines transformed by advances in machine learning (ML), but the same cannot be said for directed evolution. Reflecting on a previously co-authored perspective, I discuss why I believe this to be the case, arguing that a disconnect between the goals of machine-learning-assisted directed evolution (MLDE) researchers--"identify an optimal protein"--and the goals of directed evolution more broadly--"identify a sufficient protein given time and resource constraints"--is a principal culprit. As an example, I highlight how nearly all current MLDE methods neglect to account for the cost of DNA synthesis, resulting in strategies that have limited practical applicability regardless of the underlying models' capabilities. I close by discussing recent works that are exceptions to this overarching trend, and emphasize that the last five years of efforts in ML-assisted protein engineering and the prescribed reframe of MLDE objectives need not be mutually exclusive.

☆ Learning Epidemic Threshold Equations from Simulation: REEBLRA, an Interpretable Bayesian Classification Framework for Reproduction Numbers
Jomar F. Rabajante, Norvin P. Bansilan, Maria Czarina T. Lagura-Arreza, Destiny SM. Lutero, Emerson R. Rico
The basic reproduction number, $R_0$, is central to infectious-disease modelling, yet analytic expressions can be difficult to derive for high-dimensional, nonlinear, or structurally complex models. We introduce REEBLRA ($R_0$ Equation Estimation using Bayesian Logistic Regression Algorithm), an interpretable framework that learns explicit surrogate equations for epidemic thresholds from parameter--response data. Rather than directly regressing $R_0$, REEBLRA estimates $\Pr\{R_0(\boldsymbolθ)>c\}$ for parameter vector $\boldsymbolθ$ and threshold $c$. It provides a binary equation for classifying epidemic invasion ($R_0>1$) and successive threshold equations defining ordered brackets that approximate $R_0$, the expected number of secondary infections caused by a typical case in a fully susceptible population. The resulting equations support parameter screening, sensitivity interpretation, threshold classification, and exploratory analysis when analytic derivation is cumbersome. Bayesian logistic regression using standardized polynomial-like features and proper weakly informative priors yields regularized estimates, posterior uncertainty, and finite coefficients under complete or quasi-complete separation. We establish a mechanistic--surrogate concordance principle linking the full fitted-score gradient to the local gradient of $\log R_0$ under explicit regularity and tangency assumptions, allowing conditional interpretation of threshold directions. Across seven analytic-label benchmarks associated with ODE models, including a 15-compartment five-group SIR system, five-fold binary AUC was 0.999--1.000 and ordered-bracket accuracy was 0.951--0.998. REEBLRA complements rather than replaces mechanistic derivations, bridging simulation, machine learning, and reproduction-number analysis. An accompanying Shiny app supports implementation.

☆ Non-Destructive Detection of Early Drought Stress via Spectral Submanifold Analysis
Sunghwan Jung, Isabella Thiel, Jisoo Yuk, George Haller
Non-destructive early detection of water stress is critical for precision agriculture, yet current detection methods are often labor-intensive or destructive. We propose a rapid, non-invasive method based on Spectral Submanifold (SSM) reduction to analyze the nonlinear vibration dynamics of soybean plants. Unlike traditional linear modal analysis, our data-driven approach reveals a distinct transition in the dominant dynamics of the plant stem as drought progresses. Specifically, we uncover a sign reversal in the nonlinear damping coefficient within SSM-reduced models of full plants. Our finding reveals a fundamental transition in the plant's dissipative mechanics from a pressurized, fluid-rich vessel exhibiting superlinear (viscoelastic) damping to a dry, cellular solid dominated by sublinear (frictional) damping. This study provides the first experimental evidence that SSM-based nonlinear model reduction can detect the changing internal state of a living organism, offering a physics-based metric for high-throughput plant phenotyping.

♻ ☆ Can synthetic data reproduce real-world findings in epidemiology? A replication study using adversarial random forests
Jan Kapar, Kathrin Günther, Lori Ann Vallis, Klaus Berger, Nadine Binder, Hermann Brenner, Stefanie Castell, Beate Fischer, Volker Harth, Bernd Holleczek, Timm Intemann, Till Ittermann, André Karch, Thomas Keil, Lilian Krist, Berit Lange, Michael F. Leitzmann, Katharina Nimptsch, Nadia Obi, Iris Pigeot, Tobias Pischon, Tamara Schikowski, Börge Schmidt, Carsten Oliver Schmidt, Anja M. Sedlmair, Justine Tanoey, Harm Wienbergen, Andreas Wienke, Claudia Wigmann, Marvin N. Wright
Synthetic data hold substantial potential to address practical challenges in epidemiology due to restricted data access and privacy concerns. However, many current methods suffer from limited quality, high computational demands, and complexity for non-experts. Furthermore, common evaluation strategies for synthetic data often fail to directly reflect statistical utility and measure privacy risks sufficiently. Against this background, a critical underexplored question is whether synthetic data can reliably reproduce key findings from epidemiological research while preserving privacy. We propose adversarial random forests (ARF) as an efficient and convenient method for synthesizing tabular epidemiological data. Reduced dimensionality and variable complexity further enhanced synthesis quality. ♻ ☆ Population Neuroscience 3.0: Bridging large-N and small-N paradigms in brain-wide association studies
Zi-Xuan Zhou, Xi-Nian Zuo
Brain-wide association studies (BWAS) leveraging large datasets, have led to divergent and sometimes opposing views on research practices and priorities. The debates span multiple dimensions, including large-N vs.\ small-N sampling, cross-sectional vs.\ longitudinal, observational vs.\ interventional/causal, population generalizability vs.\ individual specificity, data-driven vs.\ hypothesis-driven, prediction-oriented vs.\ explanation-oriented, and molecular/cellular vs.\ systems-level. Taken together, these tensions are not only about methodology, but also about how large-N population studies and small-N, mechanism-focused studies should relate to one another. Shifts along these axes have sharpened disagreements and added to fragmentation in an already heterogeneous field of cognitive neuroscience. Here, we sketch an integrative response grounded in population neuroscience, organized around a closed-loop ``design--analysis--interpretation'' research cycle that aims to build consensus while bridging these divides. Our central claim is that population neuroscience offers a distinctive population-level vantage point for identifying general patterns, characterizing inter-individual variabilities, and benchmarking intra-individual changes, thereby providing reference frameworks through which small-N, mechanism-focused studies can co-evolve with large-N population studies. Population neuroscience is not simply about providing larger N for BWAS; more fundamentally, it is about accumulating cross-scale priors and shared infrastructures for design, analysis, and interpretation across human neuroscience studies. In this sense, we outline a ``third-generation'' agenda for population neuroscience that shifts emphasis from amassing isolated associations to building reference frameworks for cumulative mechanistic and translational research.

☆ Spatially Informed and Direction Sensitive Representation Learning for Spatial Transcriptomics NeurIPS 2026
Azim Dehghani Amirabad, Ali Khodabandeh Yalabadi, Artem Moskalev, Yun-Ching Chen, Mehdi Pirooznia, Scott Oloff, Rui Liao
Cellular states are shaped by both intrinsic gene expression programs and the spatial microenvironment in which cells reside. Spatial transcriptomics makes this context measurable, but effective representation learning requires capturing both spatially informative molecular signals and intercellular organization. Existing approaches address these challenges only partially: conventional gene selection can overlook spatially structured signals, while methods for encoding cell-cell relationships often either reduce a cell's microenvironment to proximity alone, discarding direction, or rely on absolute coordinates tied to an arbitrary tissue frame. Consequently, cells with similar neighbor distances but distinct spatial organization may receive overly similar representations, despite experiencing different biological contexts that shape their cellular states. We introduce Tessera, a spatial representation learning framework that addresses both molecular selection and cellular context. Tessera combines spatially informed gene selection with a geometric inductive bias that contextualizes each cell within its local tissue environment. By modeling relative cell-cell relationships, Tessera is translation-invariant while preserving direction, allowing neighboring cells to contribute differently according to their spatial context and cellular state. Across held-out tissues and technologies, Tessera outperforms existing spatial foundation models in representation quality and generalizes zero-shot to contexts up to $16\times$ larger than those seen during training.
comment: This manuscript has been accepted at the NeurIPS 2026 Workshop on AI for Drug Discovery: Bridging the Translation Gap (AI4DD)
☆ An Open Reliability Atlas of Sensitivity and Failure Modes in the Pulse Physiology Engine
Santino Nanini, Arnaud Wiedemann, Yassir Mamouni, Cyril Schweitzer, Philippe Jouvet, Michael Sauthier
Pulse is an actively maintained open-source adult-physiology simulator, yet its maintainers report no formal sensitivity analysis of its operational inputs. We present the first open reliability atlas across its critical-care envelope: Morris elementary-effects sampling of seven inputs, four severity scenarios, male and female bodies (1,280 simulations). Sensitivity is skewed: three inputs carry a median 93% of each output's total. A four-flag classifier reveals failure modes: two operator-actionable, one sex-stratified. Tidal-volume oscillation (20.6% of runs) is consistent with patient-ventilator dyssynchrony; locking the VC-AC trigger to -2 cmH2O collapses that variability. At FiO2 = 1.0, transient hyperoxia makes 120-second runs underestimate plateau PaO2; 300-second healthy-lung reruns give a median 643 mmHg. Solver divergence was all-male in an input-extreme regime (20/1,280); reducing the male blood-volume baseline to the 4,082 mL floor eliminated it. Runs and flags are released openly, showing users which regions need checking before training on Pulse output.

☆ VGL-stGen: Learning a shared multimodal model for lung histology and spatial transcriptomics
Haenara Shin, Dongjoo Lee, Sungwoo Bae, Kwon Joong Na, Daeseung Lee, Hongyoon Choi
VGL-stGen is a multimodal large language model adapted from a pretrained medical vision-language model using paired lung H&E and spatial transcriptomics with single-cell references. Training integrates image-expression and expression-cell-type pairs, and gene-conditioned pseudo-labels transfer transcriptomic cell-type supervision to histology images. One deployed checkpoint supports thirteen evaluated tasks spanning tissue and cell-type classification, gene prediction and conditional expression completion from combinations of histology, cell identity and observed gene expression. Task-specific decoding produces categorical labels, gene lists and structured expression levels. For conditional completion, fixing queried gene identities and predicting their levels autoregressively improves structured prediction. Controlled adaptation shows that restricting supervision to requested expression-level tokens can further improve image-conditioned completion. Image-permutation experiments indicate that correctly paired histology adds information beyond observed expression. External classification relies partly on computational references with shared annotation lineage, and some H&E sources were exposed during cancer training or model development. Within these conditions, VGL-stGen demonstrates how a medical vision-language backbone can learn from spatial transcriptomics and histology to connect tissue morphology and molecular states through shared generative modeling.

♻ ☆ DRIADA: an open-source Python toolkit for linking single-neuron selectivity and population activity to behavior
Nikita Pospelov, Viktor Plusnin, Olga Rogozhnikova, Anna Ivanova, Vladimir Sotskov, Margarita Orobets, Ksenia Toropova, Olga Ivashkina, Vladik Avetisov, Konstantin Anokhin
Understanding how individual neurons contribute to population activity during behavior is central to understanding brain functions across biological scales. We developed DRIADA, an open-source Python toolkit that connects analyses of individual neurons, population activity, and neuronal networks to the same behavioral parameters. We evaluated it on synthetic populations with known ground truth, calcium-imaging recordings from dorsal hippocampal CA1 in 13 mice across 39 sessions, and a simulated continuous attractor neural network (CANN) with known toroidal organization. Across most hippocampal sessions, embeddings of spatially selective neurons predicted held-out position better than equally sized random subsets and all recorded neurons. The same workflow related population dimensions back to individual neurons, including cells without detected selectivity for the measured behavioral variables. In the simulated network, complementary analyses captured features consistent with its known organization. Thus, DRIADA provides a common framework for relating neuronal selectivity, population structure, and network activity to behavioral dynamics.
♻ ☆ Statistical inference for a multiscale stochastic model of enzyme kinetics via propagation of chaos
Arnab Ganguly, Wasiur R. KhudaBukhsh
We study a class of Stochastic Differential Equations (SDEs) with jumps modeling multistage Michaelis--Menten enzyme kinetics, in which a substrate is sequentially transformed into a product via a cascade of intermediate complexes. These networks are typically high-dimensional and exhibit multiscale behavior with a strong coupling between different components, posing substantial analytical and computational challenges. In particular, the problem of statistical inference of reaction rates is significantly difficult and becomes even more intricate when direct observations of system states are unavailable and only a random sample of product formation times is observed. We address this problem in two stages. First, in a suitable scaling regime consistent with the Quasi-Steady State Approximation (QSSA), we rigorously establish a stochastic averaging principle yielding a reduced model for the product-substrate dynamics. Guided by the reduced-order dynamics, we next construct a novel Interacting Particle System (IPS) that approximates the product-substrate process at the particle level. This IPS plays a pivotal role in the inference methodology, and we prove several non-asymptotic bounds and limiting results for this system. These results facilitate the construction of an estimator based on a product-form approximate likelihood requiring only a random sample of product formation times. This approach does not need access to the system states, and we rigorously prove consistency of the estimator.

♻ ☆ Curriculum-Augmented GFlowNets For mRNA Sequence Generation
Aya Laajil, Abduragim Shtanchaev, Sajan Muhammad, Aya El Mir, Eric Moulines, Tiago Silva, Salem Lahlou
We develop an approach for the de novo synthesis of messenger RNA (mRNA) sequences encoding target proteins. This requires optimizing multiple biological objectives over large combinatorial spaces defined by synonymous codon sequences, a setting where Reinforcement Learning (RL) methods suffer from mode collapse and Generative Flow Networks (GFlowNets) face sparse rewards over long horizons. To address these challenges, we propose Curriculum-Augmented GFlowNets (CAGFNs), a multi-objective GFlowNet trained with an adaptive length-based curriculum driven by learning progress. In a nutshell, CAGFNs learn from progressively harder tasks corresponding to longer target proteins. We evaluate CAGFNs on CodonDesignEnv, a novel codon-level environment that preserves protein identity via dynamic action masking. Our experiments show that CAGFNs achieve competitive or stronger Pareto-front coverage and diversity while converging up to 4 times faster than non-curriculum GFlowNets, and maintaining 100% sequence uniqueness compared to RL baselines.
♻ ☆ Data-Driven Soft Labeling Scales DNA Read Classification to Whole-Body Cell-Type Deconvolution NeurIPS 2026
Dmytro Rizdvanetskyi, Nathan Roos, Pavlo Lutsik
Cell-type deconvolution, the task of estimating the proportions of constituent cell types in a heterogeneous biological sample, is a core problem in computational biology. Methods that rely on epigenetic marks such as DNA methylation typically operate on aggregated methylation estimates, discarding the pattern-level information carried by individual DNA reads. Existing read-level approaches that exploit this information are scarce, and all remain restricted to few-class settings; scaling them further is an open problem because, at scale, non-discriminative reads dominate and hard labels conflict with the many-to-many mapping between methylation patterns and cell types, preventing classifier convergence. To overcome this, we propose data-driven soft labels that estimate the conditional cell-type distribution for each read, and integrate this scheme into $Syto$, a new modular framework for read-level classification-based deconvolution. On a whole-body atlas of 39 human cell types, $Syto$ reduces MSE by 3.7$\times$ over the best examined baseline, with gains transferring to an out-of-distribution dataset spanning 16 tissues. $Syto$ lays the foundation for modeling increasingly large cell-type panels, with improved applications in biology and healthcare. The proposed soft-labeling scheme is further translatable to any setting with a many-to-many signal-to-label mapping.

♻ ☆ Quantifying reticulocyte biomechanics in health and disease
Zhaojie Chai, Jianlu Zheng, He Li, Ming Dao, George Em Karniadakis
Red blood cell (RBC) populations are mechanically and morphologically heterogeneous, yet how this heterogeneity governs transport, splenic retention and blood rheology in confinement remains unclear. We combine microchannel experiments with dissipative particle dynamics (DPD) simulations to determine how the morphology, deformability and hydrodynamic interaction of immature RBCs (reticulocytes) shape microconfined flow in acute and chronic mountain sickness. Reticulocyte-rich samples present the three shapes that the maturation literature assigns to successive reticulocyte stages -- multilobular, cup-shaped and near-discocytic -- which we model as R1-R3, each with a membrane shear modulus within the range reported for reticulocytes. A 5-um microchannel resolves these models only weakly, R1 taking at most ~18% longer than control discocytes to cross it, whereas a 1.5-um splenic slit, driven at its own loading, delays R1 by ~30%; both geometries order the models by shear modulus. In cell pairs, a leading cell never lets a follower pass below its own single-cell threshold, ruling out the order-of-magnitude reduction that a wake-"unjamming" picture would suggest; instead the follower queues behind the leader, and a stiffer leader, which clears the slit more slowly, delays it more. The controlling variable is the single-cell critical pressure gradient Delta P_c, which rises monotonically from control discocytes through the reticulocyte subtypes to sickled cells. Set against published rheograms, an analytical estimate attributes chronic-mountain-sickness hyperviscosity mainly to hematocrit-driven crowding rather than single-cell rheology. These results place benign acclimatization, chronic-mountain-sickness hyperviscosity and sickle-cell-trait splenic syndrome on a single mechanical axis defined by Delta P_c.

♻ ☆ Small Average Error, Wide Individual Error: General-Purpose LLMs Estimating Energy Intake from Meal Photos in a Feeding Trial
Xiaomeng Li
Photo-based estimation of food energy could ease dietary assessment, and general-purpose multimodal large language models (LLMs) make such estimates easy to obtain. Most evaluations use one photo taken before eating and compare with energy served, while dietary assessment needs energy eaten. We evaluated eight models from three vendors (Claude Haiku, Sonnet and Opus 5.5; GPT-6 luna, 6.1 sol and 6 astra; Gemini 3.8 Flash and 4 Argon) on 802 meals from ACETADA, a controlled feeding trial with weighed intake, with the before photo only, before and after photos in separate calls (subtraction), or both photos in one request. Three results ran against intuition. Subtraction under-estimated intake for six of eight models, because the models still report hundreds of kilocalories for a nearly empty tray. Scaling the before-only estimate by the average share eaten (0.87) matched much of the gain from the after photo; beyond that constant, the second photo lowered meal-level error by only 2.2 to 4.4 points, mainly in meals with food left. With one photo, two models had a near-zero intake bias only because they under-estimated the served energy and ignored uneaten food. In ablations, adding the after photo improved accuracy under both prompt formats for seven models, while prompt effects varied across models. Over 148 feeding days, five models kept the 90% confidence interval of their mean error within a pre-set 10% margin; one model also met the pre-set 30% limits of agreement, narrowly (-28% to +27%; limits outside 30% cannot be excluded), and the other seven did not. Protein was under-estimated by every model, most in protein-dense meals; repeated calls changed error by at most 1.4 points; a high reasoning effort helped only the smallest model. A low average bias is not evidence of accurate individual intake estimation; such estimates should support, not replace, assessment by a dietitian.

☆ ProteinGeoBench: Frontier Structural-Biology Agents Are Fluent at Lookup but Lost in Geometry
Matt Turk, Brandon L. Neel, Diana M. Lopez, Zachary D. Smith, Grace N. Johnecheck, James B. Golden
Frontier agents retrieve structural metadata well and compute geometry from coordinates less reliably. We ask how much of a benchmark score reflects that gap and how much reflects the evaluation configuration around the model. We study ProteinGeoBench, a suite of 131 protein-structure tasks, using 4,249 rollouts from four frontier models and 4,486 further trials across 26 models. Three of the four frontier models fall within 0.031 of one another (0.785 to 0.816) with no difference between them resolved, while the fourth scores 0.707 and all three gaps to it exclude zero. Three of the four do better on metadata lookup than on numeric calculation. The fourth is slightly better on numeric. Numeric tasks are 28 of the 131 but half of the twelve tasks that all four models fail. The ranking also depends on the evaluation budget: with one run per task, 23.9% of bootstrap replicates put a different model first. On a selected 15-task slice, removing network access lowers scores more than blocking eight structural-database domains, but the two contrasts were graded in different passes and cannot be cleanly decomposed. Swapping in each model's own command-line agent changes scores by -0.071 to +0.086, a comparison too small to rule out moderate harness effects. Across 26 models, numeric performance rises with non-numeric performance rather than staying flat. The experiments use different task subsets, so they cannot rank model, harness, access and task composition against one another. Evaluations should report results by task family, along with the number of runs, the harness and the access conditions.

☆ Accurate quantification of protein concentrations and state populations by 2D 1H-N Q-BEST NMR
Yacine Ouali, Beate Bersch, Bernhard Brutscher
We introduce 2D Q-BEST NMR as a quantitative approach for determining protein concentrations and relative state populations from a set of three 1 H-15 N correlation spectra. The BEST methodology ensures short overall experimental times and highest sensitivity. The method was validated using ubiquitin as a model system, where a precision of approximately 15-20% was achieved at the level of individual cross-peaks. This precision can be further improved by averaging corrected intensities of well-resolved resonances corresponding to the same protein state. The approach was subsequently applied to the quantification of protein concentrations across multiple samples and to the determination of conformational state populations in proteins exhibiting intrinsic or ligand-induced heterogeneity. In both cases, 2D Q-BEST NMR provides accurate and robust quantification of NMR-visible species, demonstrating its utility as a general spectroscopic tool for quantitative biomolecular NMR analysis.
☆ Magnolia: A Persistent-Memory Agent Framework for Computational Chemistry
Tao Jiang, Benjamin Navet, Yousouf Taghzouti, Fabien Gandon, Louis-Félix Nothias, Élise Dumont
Chemistry has recently entered a high-data regime as molecular databases from high-throughput platforms or computational studies have become available. Increasing automation within the chemical sciences leads to a more digital and precision chemistry. Dedicated scientific agents are becoming central to guiding the chemist through an ever-increasing amount of data and findings, including negative ones. Here, we introduce Magnolia, a persistent-memory artificial intelligence agent framework that integrates computational protocols, from docking to quantum chemistry, lab notebooks and chat logs relying on large language models (LLMs). It relies on a three-tier memory system: session-level observations are automatically captured and distilled into structured learnings, confirmed and elevated to project-tier entries and, when human-validated, promoted to cross-project rules that actively guide subsequent work and provide direct chemical feedback. We illustrate Magnolia on four case studies: quantum-chemical evaluation of aromatic systems (COMPAS database), molecular dynamics of butane and of modified DNA, and a protein-peptide docking campaign. Across these projects, Magnolia tracked 317 runs and accumulated 116 project-tier entries, from which four cross-project rules were promoted; in controlled replays of four tasks, runs with memory passed the checks that runs without it failed (for example, ten of ten docking-configuration checks, against one to four of ten). By combining automation, reproducibility, and human-in-the-loop control, our Magnolia framework streamlines docking, molecular dynamics and quantum chemistry calculations with direct analysis and LLM-augmented feedback, offering accelerated access to computational (bio)chemistry.

♻ ☆ CPTCs Drive Somatic-Visceral Communication via the Wnt Axis in Somatic Mechanotherapy: A Single-Cell Deep Learning Study
Haixiang Huang, Zhenwei Zhang, BingBing Shen, Jianming Yue, Lu Mei, Xudong Zhu, Yonghong Shi, Qianmei Zhu, Yeping Shi, Yifan Luo, Yitong Xing, Meng Dai, Qiusheng Chen
Somatic mechanical stimulation (e.g., acupuncture) exerts systemic immunomodulatory effects, yet the cellular bridge translating peripheral physical force into visceral repair remains elusive. Here, employing a custom interpretable deep learning framework (CARSS) on single-cell RNA sequencing data, we identify CD34+PDGFRα+ telocytes (CPTCs) as the primary mechanosensors in both fascia and colon during bacterial colitis. We show that somatic mechanotherapy triggers an AP-1/Hsp70-dependent transcriptional program in fascial CPTCs, inducing systemic Wnt elevation, which elicits a "transcriptional resonance" in colonic CPTCs, reprogramming their communication network from an inflammatory amplifier to a Wnt-driven regenerative hub. Mechanistically, this axis activates epithelial \b{eta}-catenin/Myc signaling, suppressing apoptosis and restoring barrier integrity independent of immune cells. Our findings define a CPTC-Driven Mechano-Resonance Axis, where CPTCs serve as synchronized relay stations that convert somatic mechanical stimulation into systemic regenerative microenvironments.
♻ ☆ RNAGenScape: Property-Guided, Optimized Generation of mRNA Sequences with Manifold Langevin Dynamics NeurIPS 2026
Danqi Liao, Chen Liu, Xingzhi Sun, Dié Tang, Haochen Wang, Scott Youlten, Srikar Krishna Gopinath, Haejeong Lee, Ethan C. Strayer, Antonio J. Giraldez, Smita Krishnaswamy
Generating property-optimized mRNA sequences is central to applications such as vaccine design and protein replacement therapy, but remains challenging due to limited data, complex sequence-function relationships, and the narrow space of biologically viable sequences. Generative methods that drift away from the data manifold can yield sequences that fail to fold, translate poorly, or are otherwise nonfunctional. We present RNAGenScape, a property-guided manifold Langevin dynamics framework for mRNA sequence generation that operates on a learned manifold of real data. By performing iterative local optimization constrained to this manifold, RNAGenScape preserves favorable computational viability proxies, accesses reliable guidance, and discourages excursions into regions poorly supported by the training data. The framework integrates three components: (1) an autoencoder jointly trained with a property predictor to learn a property-organized latent manifold, (2) a denoising autoencoder that projects updates back onto the manifold, and (3) a property-guided Langevin dynamics procedure that performs optimization along the manifold. Across three real-world mRNA datasets spanning two orders of magnitude in size, RNAGenScape increases median oracle-predicted property gain by up to 267% and success rate by up to 31%, while maintaining good biological viability proxy measures and achieving competitive inference efficiency compared to existing generative approaches.

☆ What Language Adds, and at What Cost: Four Controlled Probes of Pathology Vision-Language Models
Wenhao Zhang, Zhongliang Zhou, Yi Ping, Zirui Wang, Sheng Li
Vision--language models (VLMs) promise to make computational pathology queryable and able to draft reports, but how much language adds beyond the vision backbone is unclear, because almost every system is evaluated by its developers on test sets of their own choosing. We grade published evidence by how it was produced and test four claims with controlled probes that reuse released checkpoints and public data, varying one input at a time. Report text adds prognostic signal: adding a frozen embedding of the stage-redacted report to a slide embedding raises the pooled C-index by 0.019 for progression-free interval and by 0.016 for overall survival across 1{,}633 TCGA patients, and restoring the stage statement adds nothing. Prompts add no measurable accuracy at inference: with features and pooling fixed, zero-shot classification is statistically indistinguishable from a supervised linear probe for lung and renal cancer subtyping. Slide-level question answering is barely grounded: SlideChat answers 74.82\% of SlideBench-VQA(TCGA) items correctly with the slide and 74.41\% without it, whereas its language backbone alone reaches 43.52\%. Paraphrasing leaves accuracy unchanged but flips 8--23\% of answers. Self-reported gains from agentic navigation and few-shot language priors cannot yet be attributed to language. Language thus adds real but narrow value, and we propose minimum controls that would expose these failure modes in future evaluations.
☆ Bombadil: A Foundation Model for Plant Electrophysiology
James Kirk, Piotr Bednarski, Filip Twardy, Justin Mast, Lee von Kraus
Living plants, like animals, use electrical signals to carry information about their physiological state across their body, from roots to branches. These signals can be recorded, as they are in animals, from a pair of differential extracellular electrodes inserted into signal-conducting tissue. While machine-learning models have been trained to predict specific plant stress states from these signals, each of these models has been trained for only a single task and none have been designed to transfer across tasks, species, or environments. These approaches do not allow easy scaling across the wide range of species and environments that are relevant to agriculture, forestry, and horticulture for both commercial and consumer plant care. Here we present Bombadil, a foundation model for plant electrophysiology, pretrained without labels on over 4 million hours of electrical signals recorded from 100 plant species in ten distinct environments, including laboratories, greenhouses, commercial orchards, and natural forests. In this way, Bombadil is able to learn the fundamental features of plant electrical signals across a wide array of species and environments. Then, from those same electrical signals, Bombadil tracks changes in seven physiological indicators of plant stress, even among species and stressors with no labels in training.

☆ Simulation-free approximate Bayesian computation for stochastic reaction networks
Zekai Li, Mauricio Barahona, Philipp Thomas
Uncovering stochastic reaction networks from data is central to single-cell biology. Simulation-based inference has evolved into a flexible paradigm for this purpose, circumventing the computational intractability of the likelihood. Notably, approximate Bayesian computation performs this inference by carrying out extensive Gillespie simulations, usually at a high computational cost. Here, we propose a simulation-free approximate Bayesian computation (SFABC) framework that avoids both simulation and explicit likelihood solutions of the network dynamics. Instead, SFABC exploits the Chemical Master Equation to formulate a tractable convex optimization with ensuing constraints, which are then used to accept sampled parameters consistent with the observed data. The resulting posterior approximation comes with theoretical guarantees: it can be tightened systematically, and it provably converges to the exact Bayesian posterior distributions. Using synthetic benchmarks, we show that SFABC matches the accuracy of standard simulation-based algorithms at a substantially reduced computational cost. We then showcase the applicability of SFABC to experimental systems biology by analyzing gene transcription bursting kinetics from snapshot, time-resolved, and cell-cycle-resolved single-cell data.
☆ PyGPLA: A Python Package for Generalized Phase Locking Analysis
Amir Khani, Christian Beste, Shervin Safavi
Spike-field couplings measure the links between neuronal spiking and local field potential (LFP) and provide a tool to study how neural populations coordinate activity across spatial and temporal scales. While modern high-density electrophysiology enables the simultaneous recording of numerous LFP channels and neuronal units, this massive scale poses significant statistical and computational challenges for conventional pairwise coupling analyses. The number of channel-unit comparisons grows rapidly, and a coherent population-level structure can be obscured by high-dimensional coupling matrices. Generalized Phase Locking Analysis (GPLA) addresses this challenge by organizing spike-triggered LFP phase information into a complex coupling matrix and extracting dominant low-rank modes. These modes summarize whether a population is coupled to the field, which channels and units contribute to the coupling, and how their relative phases are organized. Here, we present PyGPLA, an open-source Python package implementing GPLA for high-density electrophysiological recordings. Designed for straightforward integration into existing neural data analysis pipelines, PyGPLA supports multivariate spike-LFP coupling analysis, from coupling estimation and interpretation to significance assessment using analytical and surrogate-based tests.

☆ How Robust Are Prognostic Radiomic Features? Comparing Selection Techniques on Pooled Glioblastoma Cohorts
Yannick Sodonougbo, Marlene Schnieders, Alexia Rizoudis, Santiago Cepeda, Frank Kramer, Dominik Müller
Radiomic survival studies in glioblastoma report heterogeneous prognostic features, but how much of this stems from the methodology itself is rarely examined. We analysed the association of 463 radiomic, anatomical and clinical features from pre-treatment MRI with overall survival in 705 IDH-wildtype glioblastoma patients from three public cohorts, using three techniques: a log-rank test after median split, univariate Cox regression (both Benjamini-Hochberg corrected) and an XGBoost regressor interpreted with SHAP. The log-rank test selected 99 features, Cox regression 72 and SHAP its top 20; five were selected by all three: age, a peritumoural T2 texture feature, the number of connected peritumoural mask components and two image-level intensity maxima. The two survival tests shared 51 features, about half of the SHAP top 20 were also selected by either test, and where selections overlapped, effect directions agreed. Many selections were PyRadiomics diagnostic descriptors that are not restricted to tumour voxels. Analytical pipeline choices, including ranking method, pre-filtering and descriptor handling, can substantially influence which features appear prognostic.

☆ EMBER: Predicting TCR--pMHC Binding Without a Docked Complex
Lin Ha, Chen Zhou, Haozheng Luo, Han Liu, Xiaoyu Zhang, Yan Chen
We propose EMBER, a structure-based framework for TCR--pMHC binding prediction that eliminates the need for a predicted docked complex, and with it the placement error that dominates such predictions. Traditional structure-based predictors utilize the co-folded complex structure of the receptor and the antigen and interpret binding from its contact surface. Consequently, every downstream feature inherits any deviation the structure-prediction tool introduced. Methodologically, EMBER folds the TCR and pMHC independently, encodes them as SE(3)-invariant residue graphs, and compares their learned representations without constructing a shared coordinate frame. A relative pose is therefore not an input of the model, so placement error cannot propagate into the prediction. Computationally, EMBER preprocesses $O(N{+}M)$ monomer folds where a complex-based predictor needs $O(NM)$ operations. Experimentally, under epitope-disjoint evaluation with six retrained baselines, EMBER is first on every metric under all four folding tools, by 0.9 to 14.4% in micro-AUROC, 18.5 to 25.3% in micro-AUPRC and 10.6 to 24.1% in MCC. Its ranking is also up to 85% more stable across folding tools.
☆ Reading Between the Reads: A Decade On, Revisiting Sequencing Protocol Annotation in the Sequence Read Archive
Jamie J. Alnasir
Background: Experimental metadata are essential for interpreting archived sequencing data. In 2015, Alnasir and Shanahan examined the annotation of fragmentation, adapter ligation and enrichment in the Sequence Read Archive (SRA). This study revisits those measurements using the original SQL keyword definitions on a September 2026 NCBI metadata snapshot reconstructed in the historical SRAdb schema. Results: The contemporary database contained 41,593,302 experiments, compared with 419,620 in 2013. In library_construction_protocol, the proportion matching all three historical keyword groups declined from 4.06% to 1.42%. The protocol field was NULL in 67.65% of contemporary experiments. Searching design_description alongside the protocol field increased combined three-step keyword detection to 1,197,367 experiments (2.88%), with most additional matches occurring where the protocol field was NULL. Coverage varied across sequencing library strategies, ranging from 0.26% for AMPLICON to 7.69% for RNA-Seq. Independent comparison of 150 sampled experiment records against source XML confirmed agreement in all historical keyword classifications. Conclusions: The original criteria identify a smaller proportion of experiments in the designated protocol field than a decade earlier. Additional preparation-related terminology is detectable in general experiment descriptions, exposing the limitations of searches restricted to one metadata field. Differences among sequencing strategies complicate historical comparisons as preparation methods evolve. The findings support more consistent protocol annotation and the need to distinguish keyword occurrence from the adequacy of experimental documentation.

☆ Cross-disorder Learning of Imaging-derived Joint Mophometric Markers in Alzheimer's and Parkinson's Diseases
Yuji Zhao, Boris Gutman
As two most prevalent neurodegenerative diseases (NDDs), Alzheimer's disease (AD) and Parkinson's disease (PD) shared commonalities both in symptomology and pathophysiology. However, to what extent and in what way these two distinct disorders overlap remain underexplored. In this study, we propose an explicit, interpretable and spatially adaptive transfer learning framework to elucidate the morphometric biomarkers linked to AD and PD. We applied such high-dimensional machine learning model to two most popular open-source neuroimaging datasets, i.e., Alzheimer's Disease Neuroimaging Initiative (ADNI) and Parkinson's Progression Markers Initiative (PPMI). On the one hand, our cross-disorder classifier revealed neuro-anatomical alteration patterns well aligning with previous neuroimaging research findings and underscoring patients' clinical presentations. On the other hand, the spatially adaptive trained classifier using shared prior achieved statistically superior diagnostic performance compared to respective individual models, which further highlights the cross-disorder framework's efficacy on modeling limited-size datasets across disease domains.
☆ QProtFuse: Query-Guided Layer Fusion for Retrieval-Augmented Protein Function Prediction
Mianzhi Pan, Peishuo Liu, Yawen Ouyang, Botian Wang, Yiming Rong, Hao Zhou, Jianbing Zhang
Predicting protein functions from amino acid sequences is essential to understanding biological processes. Large language models (LLMs) offer flexible function descriptions to natural-language queries, yet their limited protein understanding motivates retrieval-augmented generation (RAG). Existing protein RAG methods primarily expand external retrieval signals while overlooking the rich knowledge encoded within a single protein language model (PLM). We show that retrieval performance varies across PLM layers and biological tasks, making conventional final-layer selection suboptimal. To this end, we propose QProtFuse, which uses queries to adaptively fuse multi-layer protein features and jointly learns protein and annotation retrieval through intra-modal and cross-modal contrastive objectives. On Prot-Inst-OOD, QProtFuse achieves the best protein and annotation retrieval scores, reaching 16.4\% Recall@1 with annotation reranking. These gains improve downstream RAG-based function descriptions, yielding the highest LLM-as-a-judge scores for both Qwen3-8B (6.22/10) and DeepSeek-V4-Pro (6.68/10). Ablations and weight analyses demonstrate the value of query-guided fusion and complementary training objectives for exploiting a PLM's internal knowledge.
♻ ☆ DoAtlas-2: A Foundation for Self-Evolving Causal Biomedical Discovery
Yulong Li, Rong Xia, Yuxuan Zhang, Jianxu Chen, Xiwei Liu, Haochen Xue, Maosheng Li, Yuhang Liu, Yibo Yuan, Yutong Xie, Chong Li, Jionglong Su, Hagai Rossman, Eran Segal, Imran Razzak
We introduce DoAtlas-2, a foundation for self-evolving causal biomedical discovery that organizes knowledge around causal mechanisms and advances through external evidence from human populations. DoAtlas-2 integrates 771 research resources covering more than 720,000 participants in 48 countries, from longitudinal clinical phenotypes, medical imaging, and continuous physiological signals to eight molecular layers, together with an evidence network of approximately 4.7 million literature-derived records over 93,566 concepts and 149,383 candidate causal relations. DoAtlas-2 autonomously formulates research questions from evidence gaps and unresolved mechanisms, prespecifies their causal designs, and generates validated analyses. Supporting, challenging, and unresolved results continuously revise mechanistic interpretations, the causal evidence state, and the discovery frontier, so that DoAtlas-2 self-evolves within a closed loop of hypothesis generation, empirical testing, and renewed discovery. DoAtlas-2 has systematically evaluated 2,031 research questions. In the Human Phenotype Project (HPP), it formulated 4,014 candidate pathway questions across vascular, early-glycemic, and hepatic-metabolic systems, and screening of the first 1,079 yielded statistical support for 756. Representative studies identify blood pressure as a convergence node linking adiposity, hepatic, and lipid phenotypes to vascular outcomes, and show that an adiposity-inflammation-blood-pressure pathway is largely attenuated by joint adjustment for body mass index (BMI) and smoking. The discovered vascular network constitutes a completely interpretable predictive foundation, admitting exact attribution of every prediction and closed-form mediation effects. DoAtlas-2 thereby unifies causal mechanism discovery, population-evidence testing, and interpretable prediction within one continuously evolving foundation.

♻ ☆ Direct RNA sequence design under codon constraints using expressive tensor-based secondary structure models
Mark Fornace, Christina Wuyan Wang, Michael Lindsey
Nucleic acid sequence design via codon optimization is a fundamental task with applications across synthetic biology, mRNA therapeutics, and vaccine design. Given a target protein, it is a major open challenge to navigate the combinatorially large design space of codon sequences mapping to its amino acid sequence. Computational approaches generally seek to optimize simple objectives based on the codon sequence, possibly together with more complicated contributions based on secondary structure analysis. In this work, we demonstrate a direct and efficient algorithm to sample sequences from a suitable Boltzmann distribution defined in terms of the codon sequence and a fully detailed secondary structure free energy model, as well as related algorithms for exact computation of statistical quantities such as free energies, base pairing probabilities, and base and codon marginals. These algorithms draw upon a recently developed tensor-based formulation of secondary structure thermodynamics and demonstrate, for the first time, that global sequence design can be accomplished with respect to a highly accurate free energy model. Moreover, the algorithms can leverage any available CPU and GPU resources in parallel for massive computational speedups.
♻ ☆ An Open-Source Web-Based Viewer for Disseminating Image-Derived Models
Christian Herz, Kyle Sunderland, Matthew Daemer, Julia Iacovella, Sehdev Pressley, Ashley Koluda, Csaba Pinter, Kevin Zuber, John Daniels, Allen Ladd, Mali Hetmaniuk, Kevin K. Whitehead, Andras Lasso, Matthew A. Jolley
Patient-specific 3-dimensional (3D) models are increasingly central to planning surgery and catheter-based intervention in children with congenital heart disease (CHD), whose anatomy is often atypical and variable. These models inform care only when the surgeons and interventional cardiologists who make treatment decisions can rotate, crop, and measure them directly. Desktop modeling applications require local installation, capable hardware, and training. Commercial web-based viewers are portable but closed, and, in our experience, cannot display volume rendering, time-resolved (e.g., 4-dimensional) data, or the custom workflows centers build for CHD. Because CHD populations are small and heterogeneous, such workflows are unlikely to be prioritized commercially, and end users have no means to add them. To address these limitations, we developed the SlicerHeart Viewer, an open-source module that presents a simplified interface to a complete 3D Slicer/SlicerHeart session running on a server. Clinicians access models created by clinical modeling services from any device through a web browser. Because 3D Slicer runs underneath, volume rendering, time-resolved data, and any SlicerHeart workflow, including those developed for CHD, are available without modification. The module is independent of the hosting environment; we chose internal servers behind institutional single sign-on, and a cloud-hosted option is in development. Over one year at a single pediatric center, nearly all of 82 modeled cases were shared through the Viewer with clinicians from five specialties, with cases loading in 5 to 10 seconds. The approach is not specific to CHD and is deployable at other centers.
