♻ ☆ Propensity Straight-Through Gradients for Discrete Stochastic Systems
Jose M. G. Vilar, Leonor Saiz
We introduce the propensity straight-through (PST) estimator for gradient-based learning through exact simulations of continuous-time Markov chains (CTMCs). These models describe discrete stochastic dynamics across physics, chemistry, and biology, but their categorical event choices prevent ordinary differentiation. PST preserves exact forward trajectories and differentiates, in the backward pass, the normalized reaction propensities the simulator already computes. The affine state update makes this rule exact for the one-step conditional-mean sensitivity. We derive the per-step discrepancy for one-step sensitivities composed along nonlinear trajectories in closed form, show that it vanishes for affine downstream dependence, and bound it quadratically in the stoichiometric jump size. Across reversible dimerization, a genetic oscillator, a 50-task repressilator suite, and experimental patch-clamp recordings, PST recovers parameters to within 0.06-1.7% and converges 2.4-3.0-fold faster than the Gumbel-Softmax straight-through estimator at matched computational cost. At deep-learning scale, PST trains all 203,796 parameters of a stochastic reaction network using exact discrete events from initialization. By differentiating an exact conditional mean, PST eliminates reaction-selection temperature tuning and provides a scalable route to learning through exact stochastic dynamics.

♻ ☆ Data-driven multi-agent modelling of calcium interactions in cell culture: PINN vs Regularized Least-squares
Aurora Poggi, Giuseppe Alessio D'Inverno, Hjalmar Brismar, Ozan Öktem, Matthieu Barreau, Kateryna Morozovska
Data-driven discovery of dynamics in biological systems allows for better observation and characterization of processes, such as calcium signaling in cell culture. Recent advancements in techniques allow the exploration of previously unattainable insights of dynamical systems, such as the Sparse Identification of Non-Linear Dynamics (SINDy), overcoming the limitations of more classic methodologies. The latter requires some prior knowledge of an effective library of candidate terms, which is not realistic for a real case study. Using inspiration from fields like traffic density estimation and control theory, we propose a methodology for characterization and performance analysis of calcium delivery in a family of cells. In this work, we compare the performance of the Constrained Regularized Least-Squares Method (CRLSM) and Physics-Informed Neural Networks (PINN) for system identification and parameter discovery for governing ordinary differential equations (ODEs). The CRLSM achieves a fairly good parameter estimate and a good data fit when using the learned parameters in the Consensus problem. On the other hand, despite the initial hypothesis, PINNs fail to match the CRLSM performance and, under the current configuration, do not provide fair parameter estimation. However, we have only studied a limited number of PINN architectures, and it is expected that additional hyperparameter tuning, as well as uncertainty quantification, could significantly improve the performance in future works.

♻ ☆ RNAGenScape: Property-Guided, Optimized Generation of mRNA Sequences with Manifold Langevin Dynamics NeurIPS 2026
Danqi Liao, Chen Liu, Xingzhi Sun, Dié Tang, Haochen Wang, Scott Youlten, Srikar Krishna Gopinath, Haejeong Lee, Ethan C. Strayer, Antonio J. Giraldez, Smita Krishnaswamy
Generating property-optimized mRNA sequences is central to applications such as vaccine design and protein replacement therapy, but remains challenging due to limited data, complex sequence-function relationships, and the narrow space of biologically viable sequences. Generative methods that drift away from the data manifold can yield sequences that fail to fold, translate poorly, or are otherwise nonfunctional. We present RNAGenScape, a property-guided manifold Langevin dynamics framework for mRNA sequence generation that operates on a learned manifold of real data. By performing iterative local optimization constrained to this manifold, RNAGenScape preserves favorable computational viability proxies, accesses reliable guidance, and discourages excursions into regions poorly supported by the training data. The framework integrates three components: (1) an autoencoder jointly trained with a property predictor to learn a property-organized latent manifold, (2) a denoising autoencoder that projects updates back onto the manifold, and (3) a property-guided Langevin dynamics procedure that performs optimization along the manifold. Across three real-world mRNA datasets spanning two orders of magnitude in size, RNAGenScape increases median oracle-predicted property gain by up to 267% and success rate by up to 31%, while maintaining good biological viability proxy measures and achieving competitive inference efficiency compared to existing generative approaches.

♻ ☆ Harmonic Torsional Diffusion for Protein-Ligand Flexible Docking NeurIPS 2026
Maksim Zhdanov, Pavel Strashnov, Vladislav Kurenkov
Molecular docking requires reasoning jointly about ligand pose and protein flexibility. Most diffusion-based docking models predict torsional updates with generic Euclidean heads that ignore the periodic geometry of angular variables. This mismatch is especially limiting in flexible docking, where ligand conformations and pocket side chains co-adapt to form the bound complex. Here, we introduce Harmony, a harmonic torsional diffusion framework for flexible protein-ligand docking. Harmony parameterizes ligand and side-chain torsional score fields as derivatives of learned harmonic potentials on the circle, which are noised mode by mode in the Fourier basis owing to the properties of variance-exploding diffusion on the torus. This construction makes periodicity explicit and gives the model a frequency-aware inductive bias over rotameric motion. On the PDBBind benchmark, Harmony improves ligand pose accuracy and pocket all-atom reconstruction over recent flexible docking methods. On PoseBusters, it improves the physical validity of generated complexes. Case studies on EBNA1 and KRAS G12D illustrate the method's behavior on a polar and a shallow binding site, respectively. Together, these results indicate that aligning the score parameterization with the geometry of the diffusion process is a simple and effective lever for improving flexible docking.

☆ Spontaneous brain-wide rotational dynamics constrain cue-evoked behavior
Sihan Chen, Yunlong Xu, Doruk Efe Gökmen, Michel Fruchart, Brent Doiron, Vincenzo Vitelli
Uncovering low-dimensional representations of brain-wide neural dynamics and their relationship to behavior remains a central challenge. Here, we show that spontaneous brain-wide rotational dynamics constrain cue-evoked behavior and learning. To systematically establish these findings, we developed a dimensionality-reduction technique called Rotational Component Analysis (RCA) that optimizes the detection of low-variance rotational modes even within individual trials. We applied RCA to motor-cortical spike-train data from monkeys and humans, mouse cortex-wide calcium imaging, and human functional magnetic resonance imaging (fMRI) and magnetoencephalography (MEG). In mice, a rotational mode identified from activity recorded at rest predicts cue-evoked behavior: the phase of rotation when a randomly timed cue arrives predicts whether the animal subsequently pulls the lever. We explain this surprisingly strong correlation using both a recurrent neural network (RNN) and a minimal two-dimensional model of response learning, in which the intrinsic rotational mode serves as a dynamical constraint on response and learning. We further identify double-rotation modes containing two centers of rotation that separate preparatory from task dynamics in both mouse and human data. Remarkably, corresponding modes also organize neural dynamics during a non-motor human task, and rotational irreversibility captures early psychosis even when principal component analysis shows no group difference. Our work establishes rotational dynamics as low-dimensional coordinates that constrain how ongoing brain states are converted into behavior. More broadly, RCA provides a versatile tool for detecting hidden nonreciprocal dynamics in biological and physical datasets.
☆ Label-Space Mismatch Distorts Cross-Cohort Evaluation in Breast Cancer Molecular Subtyping
Suxing Liu, Byungwon Min, Zhijian Zheng, Jiahao Wang
Cross-cohort evaluation of breast cancer subtype classifiers is unreliable when source and target cohorts use different annotation standards: METABRIC labels six classes, PAM50 defines five, and a claudin-low prediction cannot be correct under a five-class standard. A classifier transferred from 1,369 METABRIC patients (train n=958) to TCGA-BRCA (n=987) and GSE21653 (n=266) separates three evaluation operations routinely conflated in cross-site comparisons. (1) Label-space restriction - an evaluation-rule change, not a retraining gain - is the dominant correction (73.76%->79.74% on TCGA-BRCA; 63.16%->68.42% on GSE21653); (2) EM prior correction on the reconciled space is an optional calibration step (Brier fidelity up, macro F1 down; to 80.65% and 74.06%); and (3) the target label space is not recoverable from unlabelled predictions (EM retains prior 0.0751 on the absent class; calibrated detectors fail), so compliance must come from cohort documentation. The protocol exposes why internal validation overstates cross-cohort value: multimodal fusion gains do not transfer (no configuration beats mRNA alone, n=694; McNemar p=0.13-1.00), and the genomic panel adds nothing to pathological complete response prediction over clinical variables (DeltaAUC = +0.0045). Clinically, without label-space compliance, cross-site accuracy comparisons misattribute annotation-standard differences to model skill; Luminal A/B and claudin-low boundary errors map to distinct treatment decisions; and subtype accuracy is a weaker portable endpoint than survival discrimination (external C-index 0.577-0.584). Reporting the cohort's allowed label set and applying label-space compliance are therefore prerequisites before any cross-cohort performance claim.
♻ ☆ Double-Take: Improving Capacity, Binding, and Selectivity In Long-Context Within Bidirectional Memory
Vakul Raghavan, Utkarsh Grover, Mingyang Mao, Chengwen Wang, Xiaomin Lin
Processing a long sequence does not guarantee that distant information remains usable. As context grows, a fixed-size memory must retain many associations, connect information across positions, and preserve relevant structure without being overwhelmed by everything it observes. These are distinct requirements: a memory may store more information without representing the relationships needed for retrieval, and it may transport those relationships without preserving the ones that matter. Thus, the challenge is not simply extending context length, but constructing a memory that remains usable as context grows. We study this problem in large-chunk test-time training (TTT) and identify two basic failures: single-step writes accumulate interference, limiting capacity, while self-associative writes cannot form cross-position bindings. We introduce Double-Take (DT), which repairs these failures with multi-step unpinned updates and cross-position mixing, and adds bidirectional change-sensitive writes for selective retention. Capacity alone leaves end-to-end recall at the $0.10$ chance floor; adding binding raises it to $0.92$. Trained only on $32\,\mathrm{kb}$ genomic contexts, DT reaches $0.927$ AUROC at $131\,\mathrm{kb}$ and $0.856$ near $1\,\mathrm{Mb}$, while transferring zero-shot from human to mouse. Together, these results identify capacity, binding, and selectivity as three requirements for usable long-range memory.

♻ ☆ Sensitive dependence of the motion of a legged robot on granular media
Chen Li, Paul B. Umbanhowar, Haldun Komsuoglu, Daniel E. Koditschek, Daniel I. Goldman
Legged locomotion on flowing ground ({\em e.g.} granular media) is unlike locomotion on hard ground because feet experience both solid- and fluid-like forces during surface penetration. Recent bio-inspired legged robots display speed relative to body size on hard ground comparable to high performing organisms like cockroaches but suffer significant performance loss on flowing materials like sand. In laboratory experiments we study the performance (speed) of a small (2.3 kg) six-legged robot, SandBot, as it runs on a bed of granular media (1 mm poppy seeds). For an alternating tripod gait on the granular bed, standard gait control parameters achieve speeds at best two orders of magnitude smaller than the 2 body lengths/s ($\approx 60$ cm/s) for motion on hard ground. However, empirical adjustment of these control parameters away from the hard ground settings, restores good performance, yielding top speeds of 30 cm/s. Robot speed depends sensitively on the packing fraction $φ$ and the limb frequency $ω$, and a dramatic transition from rotary walking to slow swimming occurs when $φ$ becomes small enough and/or $ω$ large enough. We propose a kinematic model of the rotary walking mode based on generic features of penetration and slip of a curved limb in granular media. The model captures the dependence of robot speed on limb frequency and the transition between walking and swimming modes but highlights the need for a deeper understanding of the physics of granular media.
♻ ☆ WILSON - Multi-magnification composites as a foundation for patient-level pathology analysis and diagnostic text generation
Saghir Alfasly, Wataru Uegami, Sobhan Hemati, Wenchao Han, Xiaojia Tang, Kevin Thompson, Daniel Stone, Ghazal Alabtah, Chris Hartley, Chady Meroueh, John C. Cheville, Thomas J. Flotte, Saba Yasir, Michael R. Lucas, Eric W. Klee, Cheryl L. Willman, Judy C. Boughey, Matthew P. Goetz, Krishna R. Kalari, H. R. Tizhoosh
Pathologists examine tissue sections across multiple magnifications and slides, integrating morphological information to establish a diagnosis. In contrast, most pathology foundation models encode thousands of image patches from a single representative ("best") slide and aggregate their features, limiting multiscale and patient-level contextual integration. Here we introduce WILSON, a vision-language foundation model that represents whole-slide images and multi-slide cases through multi-magnification composites. Trained on approximately 189,000 slides spanning 42 organs and 838 diagnostic entities, WILSON enables patient-level analysis, multimodal retrieval and diagnostic text generation. Without task-specific training, WILSON outperformed a dedicated case-level model (macro-F1, 0.52 versus 0.38) and matched substantially larger slide-level models while requiring 400-1,350 times fewer computational operations. End-to-end fine-tuning improved breast cancer subtyping and stromal lymphocyte grading. These findings demonstrate that compact, multiscale representations can support computationally efficient, clinically grounded pathology foundation models.

☆ Aptamers as a sandbox for lab-in-the-loop agentic molecular discovery
Zachary Cutts, John Coller, Rachel Chen, Hua Tang, Mathias Bellaiche, Ryder Easterlin, Dina Zverinski, Petar Sirkovic, Avinatan Hassidim, Yossi Matias, Pooja Rao, Annalisa Pawlosky
Despite breakthroughs in AI-driven molecular generation, existing discovery pipelines operate largely in silico, with wet-lab feedback reserved for validation rather than iterative improvement. Whether large language model (LLM)-based reasoning agents can accelerate de novo discovery by iterating with lab data remains unproven. Here we use protein-binding aptamer design as a sandbox (1-2 week cycles, \$1-\$3 per candidate) to demonstrate a wet lab in a closed loop with AI systems. We tasked agentic pipelines with developing aptamer binders against $α$-thrombin and myoglobin across three design-test-improve cycles ($\sim$1,000 candidates per round; $\sim$4,400 constructs). Statistically significant, bead-corrected on-target hit rates among new candidates rose across rounds from 0.0% in Round 1 to 26.0% in Round 2 and 42.1% in Round 3 on $α$-thrombin (37.1% excluding literature motif descendants), and from 10.2% in Round 1 to 3.7% in Round 2 and 33.5% in Round 3 on myoglobin. Single-clone bead-binding titrations of eight full-length $α$-thrombin candidates confirmed concordance with pooled sequencing enrichment (Pearson $r = 0.90$). In these assays, literature-derived motifs engineered by the agents to reduce off-target reactivity in the pooled screen reached apparent affinities of $K_D = 24.6$--$133$ nM, whereas de novo scaffolds showed low-micromolar binding ($K_D > 1\ μ$M). We also report how our pipelines used myoglobin binding data to exploit the G-quadruplex binder space, diagnose cross-reactivity as on-target specificity dropped from 75.0% to 19.1%, and recover by developing strategies that restored specificity to 72.4%. We demonstrate that empirical lab data is more valuable than existing computational proxies, showing how iterative experimental feedback enables AI agents to diagnose assay artifacts and refine molecular designs across rounds.

☆ CADS: Measuring the Incremental Contribution of LLM Proposers in Sequential Biological Screens
Guanxing Chen, Nihui Shao, Haohuai He, Zhenchao Tang, Pengwei Hu, Zhi-An Huang
Large language models are increasingly used to guide sequential biological screens, yet standard evaluations rely on raw hit counts that conflate a proposer's value with the discovery capacity of the underlying algorithmic harness. Adaptive sampling and corrupted experimental readouts also leave standard feedback comparisons without a false-discovery guarantee. We present Certified Agentic Discovery Screens (CADS), an error-controlled measurement framework that quantifies a proposer's incremental value against an identical LLM-free loop. By integrating capped likelihood-ratio bounds, e-processes, and complete-family e-BH, CADS controls the terminal false discovery rate under the stated null and corruption conditions despite arbitrary dependence, adaptive sampling, and optional stopping. In DepMap-backed simulations, CADS exposes two evaluation artifacts: the LLM-free harness alone clears the feedback gate, and raw origin counts overstate LLM contributions because LLM picks displace fallback discoveries. On the initial 20 seeds, supplying tested-candidate history to Qwen2.5-7B shifts its net contribution from +0.35, with a 95% interval covering zero, to +2.90, with the interval above zero. This initial net effect did not persist in a 50-seed matched-quota replication. These results support net gain over an LLM-free loop, measured between separately certified sets, as the appropriate measure of proposer contribution.

♻ ☆ SiLVi: Simple Interface for Labeling Video Interactions
Ozan Kanbertay, Richard Vogg, Elif Karakoc, Peter M. Kappeler, Claudia Fichtel, Alexander S. Ecker
Computer vision methods are increasingly used for the automated analysis of large volumes of video data collected through camera traps, drones, or direct observations of animals in the wild. While recent advances have focused primarily on detecting individual actions, much less work has addressed the detection and annotation of interactions -- a crucial aspect for understanding social and individualized animal behavior. Existing open-source annotation tools support either behavioral labeling without localization of individuals, or localization without the capacity to capture interactions. To bridge this gap, we present SiLVi, an open-source labeling software that integrates both functionalities. SiLVi enables researchers to annotate behaviors and interactions directly within video data, generating structured outputs suitable for training and validating computer vision models. By linking behavioral ecology with computer vision, SiLVi facilitates the development of automated approaches for fine-grained behavioral analyses. Although developed primarily in the context of animal behavior, SiLVi could be useful more broadly to annotate human interactions in other videos that require extracting dynamic scene graphs. The software, along with documentation and download instructions, is available at: https://silvi.ozankanbertay.com.

♻ ☆ Do Vision CIL Methods Transfer to Protein? A Diagnostic Benchmark of Frozen Protein Encoders NeurIPS 2026
Ziyuan Gao
Protein function annotation operates over an expanding label space, making class-incremental learning (CIL) a natural framework for incorporating newly discovered functions without full retraining. Yet existing CIL methods have been developed largely in vision and have not been systematically evaluated over frozen protein representations. We conduct a controlled benchmark of nine representative CIL methods across five sequence-, structure-, and sequence--structure-based protein encoders and two enzyme function datasets at different scales, while keeping the encoders frozen and evaluation settings consistent across representations. We find that no method transfers reliably across encoders, and that these failures persist across alternative phase constructions. Diagnostic analyses reveal that this instability arises from weak gradient-based stability signals, substantial heterogeneity in embedding geometry, and covariance assumptions whose appropriate capacity changes with both dataset scale and encoder. These findings suggest that the representation itself should be treated as a central component of continual-learning design rather than a passive feature extractor. More generally, reliable protein CIL will require encoder-aware methods that adapt their statistical assumptions and capacity to the geometry of the representation on which they operate. Code is available at https://github.com/zygao930/ProteinCIL.

☆ Multiscale Quantum-Classical Simulation of Protein Autoproteolysis in a Million-Atom Membrane Environment
Alexander Orlov, Alexis Ralli, Tim Weaving, Michael Williams de la Bastida, Reuben Martin, Zohim Chandani, Minh Chung, P. V. Sriluckshmy, Fedor Simkovic, Luigi Iapichino, Peter J. Love, Christian B. Mendl, Andrea Townsend-Nicholson, Peter V. Coveney
Predicting chemical reactions in biomolecules is challenging because reactive centers are embedded within complex environments that strongly influence their energetics and mechanisms. Here, we present a hybrid quantum-classical workflow that combines molecular dynamics, density functional theory, and correlated wavefunction methods, tightly integrating CPUs, GPUs, and quantum processing units (QPUs) to enable simulations across multiple length and accuracy scales. We apply this approach to ADGRG1 autoproteolysis, a maturation reaction in an adhesion G protein-coupled receptor that has not previously been studied using correlated electronic-structure methods. Our simulations treat a biologically realistic system containing more than one million atoms, including the full-length uncleaved receptor embedded in an endoplasmic-reticulum membrane, while incorporating a 54-qubit IQM quantum processor and up to 1,200 NVIDIA H100 GPUs. These results demonstrate how quantum computers can serve as accelerators within scalable scientific workflows, extending correlated electronic-structure methods to biologically relevant systems.
☆ LiveCoMS Roundtable: Discussing Markov state models after two decades of development v1.0
Daniel M. Zuckerman, Gregory R. Bowman, Amedeo Caflisch, Aaron R. Dinner, Ahmed Fadhluddin, Giuseppe Giangreco, Xuhui Huang, Bettina G. Keller, Stefan Klus, Feliks Nüske, Michael O'Connor, Edina Rosta, Christof Schütte, Gerhard Stock, Vincent Voelz, Jonathan Weare, Antonia S. J. S. Mey
Markov state models (MSMs) play a central role in the analysis of molecular dynamics (MD) simulations for biomolecules, and MSM methodologies have been under continual development since their introduction to the field in the early 2000s. MSMs take MD trajectory data as input and produce a more compact, i.e., coarse-grained, and interpretable representation of the system in terms of conformational states and quantitative observables such as the equilibrium populations of states and the timescales of interconverting between states. Numerous strategies and considerable mathematical formalism have been developed to improve coarse-graining in both time and space to enable the most reliable representation of the underlying biomolecular dynamics. Nevertheless, there remains considerable discussion in the literature regarding best practices and MSM limitations, as well as proposals to abandon or extend the first-order Markovian framework in common use. This Roundtable article gathers together diverse opinions from $> 10$ authors active in MSM development, and by design, each author or author group offers a separate contribution. No consensus view is provided. Instead, the article is meant to be a guide for readers wishing to be exposed to a wide set of expert perspectives.

☆ Image Moment Estimation for Crowded Datasets
Andy Zhang, Oscar Mickelin, Amit Singer
Single-particle cryogenic electron microscopy (cryo-EM) is an imaging technique capable of reconstructing high-resolution three-dimensional (3D) structure of biological macromolecules from a set of noisy projection images taken at random viewing directions. The mathematical model for cryo-EM images typically assumes that each image contains exactly one particle, which is centered at the origin. However, many experimental datasets contain particle images which include multiple particles: one central particle and a few crowding particles. This deviation from the model leads to inaccurate estimation of image statistics, such as the low-order image moments. To account for this, we propose a method to estimate image moments while correcting for the effect of crowding particles. This method can be run directly from a set of cryo-EM micrographs with picked particles, and we demonstrate its efficacy on a wide array of synthetic data.

☆ MultiPASE: Shape-Aware Comparative 3D Genome Analysis
N. Nipu, G. Johnson, C. Roth, D. Rogers, S. R. Starkenburg, C. R. Steadman, G. E. Marai
3D genome structures reconstructed from Hi-C data provide valuable insights into chromatin organization, but comparing them across conditions and time points remains challenging because of noise, variability, and arbitrary orientation. State-of-the-art comparison approaches often rely on contact-matrix-based summaries, which do not preserve the spatial geometry of the reconstructed structures and make integration with gene expression and chromatin accessibility difficult. There is therefore a need for robust structural alignment and spatial abstraction methods that support integrated analysis across conditions and time points. We present MultiPASE, a computational pipeline for comparative analysis of reconstructed 3D genome structures represented as gene-level spatial point clouds. MultiPASE aligns point clouds into a shared coordinate frame using coarse-to-fine rigid registration with PCA-based axis disambiguation and robust ICP refinement, then summarizes spatial organization using two complementary shape abstraction methods that produce stable, nested, multi-scale representations from dense core regions to broader structural extent. Using Hi-C-derived reconstructions from viral infection experiments, we show how the approach reveals condition-dependent structural changes that are difficult to interpret from raw 3D structures alone. By integrating spatial structure with gene expression and chromatin accessibility, MultiPASE supports interactive analysis of spatial organization and gene relocation patterns. A quantitative validation, reported in the Supplementary Materials, shows that the proposed shape abstractions preserve non-convex features, remain stable across samples, and that the alignment pipeline is necessary for meaningful comparison. A usage case and a usability evaluation with eight participants demonstrate the pipeline's effectiveness for comparative structural analysis.
☆ Indirect Influences of Artificial Intelligence Advice on Complex Sepsis Treatment Decisions
Venkatesh Sivaraman, Joel Levin, Eric P. Mason, Victor Talisa, Suchi Saria, Jean Feng, Andrew J. King, Jeremy M. Kahn, Adam Perer
Sepsis is a major public health concern, and many sepsis patients die due to ineffective hemodynamic management. Artificial intelligence could support hemodynamic decisions, particularly in complex cases where conflicting information and comorbidities drive uncertainty and practice variation. However, these systems' real-world value depends on not just accuracy, but how they influence the quality of clinician decisions. We first retrospectively evaluated an AI system's recommendations in 6,928 sepsis patients, finding that they improved upon the original providers' decisions overall but had significantly worse performance in complex cases. We then conducted a pre-registered experiment with 228 US critical care physicians to test their use of this imperfect advice in six complex treatment decisions, using one of three advice types designed to vary reasoning autonomy. Participants given simple model recommendations overcame poor advice, choosing optimal treatments in 56.3% of decisions compared to 31.8% if they had always followed the recommendations. They also made significantly better decisions than those receiving a less prescriptive advice type (42.9%). Physicians selectively adapted and improved weak advice, likely because it prompted them to reconsider their assessment. Our findings suggest that improving sepsis care decisions with AI may require accounting for these indirect influences resulting from human-AI collaborative reasoning.
☆ gpuGEM: GPU-based linear optimisation of genome-scale metabolic models
Farid Zare†, Timothy Hulshof†, Ines Thiele, Ronan M. T. Fleming
Summary: Genome-scale metabolic models increasingly challenge CPU linear-programming solvers in repeated-optimisation workflows. We present gpuGEM, an open-source Python library for GPU-based GEM optimisation using NVIDIA cuOpt, with size-adaptive settings validated against HiGHS and Gurobi. Across a ten-model benchmark suite spanning four orders of magnitude in problem size, gpuGEM achieved speed-ups of 1.1x to 94x relative to the faster of the HiGHS dual-simplex and interior-point methods for each model. gpuGEM also outperformed Gurobi on three of ten models, with speed-ups of 1.6x to 2.5x. In a candidate-gene prioritisation workflow, gpuGEM reduced end-to-end query time from 41 s to 21 s. Availability and Implementation: gpuGEM is released under the MIT licence at https://github.com/Digital-Metabolic-Twin-Centre/gpuGEM and on PyPI (pip install gpugem). An archived release(v0.1.2) is available at https://zenodo.org/records/23422813. gpuGEM requires an NVIDIA GPU, Python 3.11 or later, CUDA 12 and cuOpt 26.6 or later. cuOpt requires a GPU with Volta architecture or newer (compute capability 7.0 or newer). Contact: ronan.mt.fleming@gmail.com Supplementary information: Supplementary data are available at Bioinformatics online.

☆ ProtoGTX: Prototype-Aligned Graph Transformer for Explainable Whole-Slide Image Analysis
Iori Azuma, Hiroyuki Kusuhara, Tadahaya Mizuno
Deep learning has achieved strong performance in computational pathology, but explaining whole-slide image (WSI) predictions remains challenging. Existing explanation methods often highlight important regions without identifying their morphological patterns, leaving a gap between patch-level computational evidence and slide-level pathological interpretation. We propose ProtoGTX, a graph-based framework that combines spatial contextualization with a flexible morphological prototype vocabulary. ProtoGTX separates the reference prototypes used to define morphological concepts from the predictive model: the reference vocabulary can be generated from training data by unsupervised clustering or supplied externally, and remains fixed during classifier training. Patch features are first contextualized over a spatial graph, after which learnable prototypes representing morphological concepts are optimized through cross-attention. Their slide-level activation profile is aligned with the morphological composition induced by the fixed reference prototype bank. On the CPTAC and BRACS datasets, ProtoGTX outperforms the evaluated MIL, prototype-based, and graph-based baselines, with particularly pronounced improvements on BRACS. Qualitative analyses further show that spatial contextualization transforms fragmented patch-level prototype assignments into more coherent tissue regions. In BRACS, prototype maps also co-localize with pathologist annotations and regions relevant to slide-level prediction. These results demonstrate that ProtoGTX provides complementary semantic and spatial explanations while retaining a prototype vocabulary that can be defined independently of the predictive model. Code is available at https://github.com/mizuno-group/ProtoGTX.

♻ ☆ Shape-Constrained Bayesian Active Learning of Self-Limiting Saturation Curves
Pouyan Navabi, Christos G. Takoudis
Saturation curves, monotone responses that rise from zero to a plateau, recur across gas adsorption, enzyme kinetics, and self-limiting growth. Learning one from a few costly, noisy measurements is a common bottleneck. We present a Bayesian active-learning platform built on a penalized monotone I-spline. Non-negative weights make every posterior draw rise from zero and never fall, a survey measurement anchors the plateau in place of a flatness prior, and the input reaching any saturation level is read from the posterior by level crossing, assuming no kinetic form. Each measurement is placed where the surrogate is least certain, which beats random designs and edges a log-spaced grid of the same budget. One fixed configuration recovers five kinetically distinct regimes from twenty measurements to a saturation error of 0.037 to 0.041, below the output noise, and averages 0.045 on 250 curves from an unseen distribution. Running unconstrained Gaussian processes (GPs) and two unpenalized monotone bases through the same loop separates each ingredient's contribution. Logarithmic resolution and the roughness penalty carry the accuracy, while monotonicity guarantees a level crossing unique on every draw. The penalty keeps the surrogate the most accurate model at every noise level, and its second-order form centers the recommended input for saturation where a first-order penalty reads late (a bias of +0.32 s with 94% coverage, against +0.44 s and 64% for the matched unconstrained GP). Because the recommended input lies where the curve is nearly flat, no method can pin it down precisely, and we report it as a cost-indexed quantile with the probability of falling short.

♻ ☆ Dynamic response phenotypes and model discrimination in systems and synthetic biology
Eduardo D. Sontag
Biological systems encode function not primarily in steady states, but in the structure of transient responses elicited by time-varying stimuli. Overshoots, biphasic dynamics, adaptation kinetics, fold-change detection, entrainment, and cumulative exposure effects often determine phenotypic outcomes, yet are poorly captured by classical steady-state or dose-response analyses. This paper develops an input-output perspective on such "dynamic phenotypes," emphasizing how qualitative features of transient behavior constrain underlying network architectures independently of detailed parameter values. A central theme is the role of sign structure and interconnection logic, particularly the contrast between monotone systems and architectures containing antagonistic pathways. We show how incoherent feedforward (IFF) motifs provide a simple and recurrent mechanism for generating non-monotonic and adaptive responses across multiple levels of biological organization, from molecular signaling to immune regulation and population dynamics. Conversely, monotonicity imposes sharp impossibility results that can be used to falsify entire classes of models from transient data alone. Beyond step inputs, we highlight how periodic forcing, ramps, and integral-type readouts such as cumulative dose responses offer powerful experimental probes that reveal otherwise hidden structure, separate competing motifs, and expose invariances such as fold-change detection. Throughout, we illustrate how control-theoretic concepts, including monotonicity, equivariance, and input-output analysis, can be used not as engineering metaphors, but as precise mathematical tools for biological model discrimination. Thus we argue for a shift in emphasis from asymptotic behavior to transient and input-driven dynamics as a primary lens for understanding, testing, and reverse-engineering biological networks.
♻ ☆ Establishing the Minimal Clinically Important Difference (MCID) for Smartphone-Derived Gait Measures in Multiple Sclerosis
Mike D Rinderknecht, Bernhard Fehlmann, Dimitar Stanev, Cedric Simillion, Ernst Bos, Letizia Leocani, Agne Kazlauskaite, Gary Cutter, Helmut Butzkueven, Licinio Craveiro
Background: Digital health technologies allow for frequent, remote gait monitoring in people with multiple sclerosis (MS). However, to differentiate daily variability from actual disease progression in longitudinal data, established minimal clinically important differences (MCID) are required. Currently, there is limited literature defining these thresholds for digital gait metrics. Objective: To establish MCIDs for digital gait measures reflecting progression in MS. Methods: Digital gait measures were captured via daily, remote, smartphone-based Two-Minute Walk Tests in CONSONANCE (NCT03523858), a phase 3b study of ocrelizumab in progressive MS. Using an anchor-based approach, median changes from baseline at Week 96 on digital gait measures were computed for patients showing clinically meaningful worsening on either Timed 25-Foot Walk, Ambulation Score, Expanded Disability Status Scale, or 12-item Multiple Sclerosis Walking Scale. These changes were subsequently triangulated to derive the MCID estimates. Results: 243 patients with progressive MS (female: n=125 (51%); mean [SD] age: 49.3 [9.3]; mean [SD] EDSS: 4.8 [1.4]) had digital gait data available at baseline and Week 96. Median changes were generally consistent across anchors. Triangulated MCIDs are: Step Velocity = -0.16 m/s, Step Velocity Scaled to Walking Time = -0.18 m/s, Step Duration = 0.06 s, Step Length = -0.07 m, Total Number of Steps = -28, and Total Distance Walked = -24 m. Conclusion: These MCIDs provide a framework for interpreting meaningful gait changes and integrating digital measures into MS outcome evaluation. Beyond facilitating novel clinical trial endpoints to evaluate treatment efficacy, they enable objective, real-world monitoring to advance personalized patient care.

☆ Evolutionary Informed Translation from Protein to Nucleic Acid Sequence
Anna Hart, Mohammed El-Kebir, Huimin Zhao, Heng Ji
The choice of nucleic acid sequence for a given protein strongly impacts function and is species-specific. Current protein-to-nucleic-acid translation models treat sequences in isolation, often over-favoring frequently used codons. To generate more natural nucleic acid sequences, we introduce PINATA (Phylogenetically Informed protein to Nucleic Acid Translation with sequence Alignment), a model that leverages sequences from related species encoded in multiple sequence alignments (MSAs) to guide prediction. Our architecture leverages evolutionary context and learns species-and protein specific context to make biologically-informed predictions. Evaluated across 61,934 gene trees and 882 species, PINATA generates sequences that mirror the naturally-occurring sequence substantially better than existing language models. PINATA leverages MSA data to improve exact native codon recovery by 17.95 percentage points over existing language models, effectively preserves natural rare-codon nuances, and more closely matches native sequence properties. PINATA maintains strong predictive performance even with limited evolutionary data. While previous methods largely treated sequences from different species in isolation, our method leverages the evolutionary relationships between nucleic acids and proteins across species to create a more biologically-informed translation. We demonstrate that leveraging rich evolutionary data through an biologically-designed method strongly aids in translating protein sequences to nucleic acid sequences across species. We will make data and code publicly available upon publication.
♻ ☆ StabilityArc: Decoding Protein Sequence Embeddings into Generalizable Stability Landscapes NeurIPS 2026
Aaron L. Feller, Andrew D. Ellington, Claus O. Wilke
Every protein has a unique stability landscape, but the physical consequences of mutation are governed by recurring biochemical constraints. We test whether a shared decoder, trained on measurements from diverse proteins, can interpret these constraints in an unseen target, enabling cross-protein transfer for initial experimental round prescreening. We present StabilityArc , which maps frozen ESMC-600M residue representations through a shared RoPE transformer to an Lx20 matrix of substitution effects; a symmetric, contact-aware residual aids in predicting epistasis in simultaneous substitutions. In 66 strict leave-one-protein-out evaluations covering 134,794 ProteinGym variants, StabilityArc achieves 0.7134 Spearman correlation, exceeding the strongest zero-shot baseline, ProSST-2048 (0.6526), by 0.0608. We further explore the utility of this method by providing the score as a prior for Kermut, achieving Spearman correlation of 0.8280 across three supervised split schemes, improving on Kermut's reported 0.8167.

♻ ☆ Generative molecule evolution using 3D pharmacophore for efficient Structure-Based Drug Design
Yi He, Ailun Wang, Zhi Wang, Yu Liu, Xingyuan Xu, Wen Yan
Recent breakthroughs in generative models have shown great promise for drug discovery, yet Structure-Based Drug Design (SBDD) faces two critical challenges: the scarcity of high-quality protein-ligand complex data and the practical synthesizability of generated molecules. While ligand-based approaches benefit from billion-scale small molecule datasets, they often lack the structural context of the target protein. To address these dual challenges of data scarcity and synthetic inaccessibility, we propose MEVO, a generative molecular evolutionary framework that operates within a synthesizable chemical space. MEVO introduces a reaction-aware generative model trained on reaction routes constructed by applying predefined reaction templates to Enamine Building Blocks. The model jointly generates molecules and corresponding synthesis sketches consistent with these templates. By integrating this generative capability into a pocket-aware evolutionary strategy, MEVO iteratively refines molecular structures within the synthesizable chemical space, guided by a hierarchical scoring function that combines fast approximation with rigorous Absolute Binding Free Energy Perturbation (ABFEP) calculations. This framework efficiently generates high-affinity, synthetically feasible binders for diverse protein targets. We validate MEVO's performance through rigorous ABFEP calculations, demonstrating its ability to design potent inhibitors for challenging targets such as KRAS$^{\textrm{G12D}}$, achieving affinities comparable to known active inhibitors. By integrating the data richness of ligand-based learning with the precision of structure-based optimization, MEVO offers a holistic solution that ensures both high binding affinity and synthetic accessibility, paving the way for more efficient and practical structure-based drug design.

☆ Documenting Trofinetide Treatment Phenotypes in Rett Syndrome with a Dual-LLM Consensus Pipeline
Daniel Palacios, Guantong Qi, Mikael Guzman Karlsson, Shawntel K. David, Mohammed Sameeruddin, Bernhard Suter, Zhandong Liu, Davut Pehlivan, Hu Chen
Objective: Trofinetide, approved in 2023 as the first Rett syndrome treatment, lacks scalable real-world monitoring because chart review is labor-intensive. We developed a dual large language model (LLM) consensus pipeline to extract 18 treatment-response phenotypes, comprising 4 adverse effects, 13 Rett clinical features, and medication refusal, from clinical notes. Materials and Methods: We analyzed 26,763 notes from 108 patients with confirmed sustained trofinetide exposure at Texas Children's Hospital. The pipeline filters notes by keyword, classifies each through two LLMs requiring consensus, and aggregates a patient-by-timepoint matrix. Architecture and prompt were selected on a diarrhea development set, then frozen and applied to the other 17 phenotypes with the phenotype definition substituted, as an out-of-sample test. The reported prompt comparison scores 183 expert-labeled cells. Results: A prompt requiring diarrhea to be active and severe performed best (84.2 $\pm$ 0.0% accuracy; F1 = 0.674), 47 percentage points above regular-expression matching ($p < 0.0001$). Expert reviewers judged six of the pipeline's calls per phenotype, three positive and three negative, and agreed with 90.2% (92/102). The 13 Rett clinical features are manifestations of the disorder itself rather than adverse effects of trofinetide. Gross and fine motor impairment, language/communication, and stereotypy were documented present in 92-100% of patients at every visit. Discussion: In-context LLM extraction captured multi-domain treatment responses. The outcome is documented presence at each visit, not a direction-of-change judgment. Conclusion: Coupling in-context LLM reasoning with a consensus architecture offers a scalable alternative to keyword and regular-expression extraction for real-world evidence, adapting to new phenotypes and medications through configuration alone.

☆ Poisoning the corpora of protein design systems during pre-training and retrieval
Charalampos Koilakos, Kimon-Antonios Provatas, Michail Patsakis, Candace SY Chan, Georgios A. Pavlopoulos, Ilias Georgakopoulos-Soares
Protein foundation models increasingly support the generation of novel biological sequences, yet their reliance on large public datasets creates opportunities for adversarial manipulation of the material they learn from and retrieve. We investigate two data-only poisoning attacks against protein design systems, targeting the pre-training corpus of autoregressive protein language models and the retrieval corpus of a text-guided protein generator. For the first, we pre-trained ProtGPT2 and ProGen2 variants of 300 million parameters, introducing poisoned protein sequences containing one of two triggers followed by one of three payloads. Across all poisoned runs, both architectures acquired highly reliable trigger-dependent generation under uniform and ramped poisoning schedules. Under ramped exposure, the earliest majority activation occurred after only 200 contaminated examples, while for the uniform schedule earliest activation occurred around 5000 poisoned samples. For the second attack, we inserted records into a retrieval corpus system without modifying its weights or decoding procedure. A single query-aware record captured a top-16 retrieval position for 58\% of templated requests and 97\% of free-text requests. Conditional on successful poisoned retrieval, 62\% of requests produced altered sequences, while 19-21% acquired the inserted domain by profile-HMM homology. A universal query-unaware attack using 1,500 records also achieved substantial retrieval capture and domain transfer. Together, these findings demonstrate that adversarial protein sequence contributions can establish persistent, sequence-specific behaviour during pre-training or redirect protein generation at inference, while leaving ordinary model behaviour largely preserved. Our work establishes the integrity and biological provenance of protein sequence data as critical safeguards for generative protein design systems.

☆ OmicsBench: a long-horizon multi-turn benchmark of AI agents on research-study-scale bioinformatics
Gideon Shaked, Siddharth Nagisetty
Computational biologists increasingly delegate substantial analyses to AI agents in long-horizon, multi-turn sessions, yet most benchmarks of agents' bioinformatics capabilities pose a single short task with a single-shot prompt. We present OmicsBench, a long-horizon, multi-turn bioinformatics benchmark for AI agents. Each of its 97 tasks links dependent subtasks into a directed acyclic graph, for 662 underlying subtasks in all. Each subtask is posed as one user turn and graded deterministically against an answer key, with optional in-context feedback on incorrect answers. Across 12 models on three harnesses, the slowest agent averages 3.6 hours per task, and no combination comes near saturating the benchmark, with mean scores of 0.43 to 0.57 out of a possible 1 without in-context feedback and 0.54 to 0.67 with it. Multi-turn evaluation also uncovers behavior that single-shot prompts cannot, as agents score lower on subtasks that depend on earlier subtasks, and in-context feedback helps agents recover some of that loss. Because deterministic scoring gives no qualitative account of how an agent failed, we also introduce an unsupervised failure mode discovery pipeline that describes, embeds, and clusters agents' failed attempts to surface emergent failure modes. Applied to OmicsBench, this pipeline exposes qualitative shortcomings in how agents carry out bioinformatics analyses, ranging from agents applying their own statistical filters in place of the specified ones to agents writing whole answers from memory.

☆ Alert-List Concordance for Evaluating Imputation Strategies in ICU Sepsis Prediction
Priyanka Siddappa, Muntaqim Ahmed Raju, Shifat Haider Al Amin, Ruizhe Ma
Preprocessing is an under-measured reliability risk in clinical prediction: a model can keep a similar AUROC and still change who receives an alert. Imputation quality is commonly evaluated using reconstruction error or aggregate discrimination, neither of which directly measures agreement on which patients are flagged. At a matched alert rate, pipelines with similar aggregate performance can produce different patient lists. This question is especially relevant under the frequent, structured missingness of real ICU records. We therefore introduce alert-list concordance, which asks whether two pipelines identify the same high-risk patients, measured by the Jaccard similarity of their top-k% alert sets with patient-level bootstrap confidence intervals. On PhysioNet/CinC 2019 (40,336 patients, 71.9% missingness), we compare nine imputation strategies and a native-missingness baseline using a fixed XGBoost configuration, fitted and Platt-calibrated separately for each strategy. After Bonferroni correction, 22 of the 28 reconstruction-method pairs showed no statistically significant difference in patient-level AUROC, yet their alert lists overlap at Jaccard 0.518 to 0.750. Similar aggregate discrimination can therefore accompany different patient selections. ForwardFill is a separate outlier, pairing higher discrimination with the lowest overlap in the matrix, 0.40 to 0.45. The broad pattern holds within each hospital system and under six patient-level aggregation rules. At the row level, eight of ten methods fall within an AUROC range of 0.011. In this row-level pipeline, deep imputers did not outperform mean imputation on discrimination, and the sequential implementations cost 5 to 12 times mean imputation in total pipeline wall-clock time on our hardware.

☆ Harness-Driven Agentic Operation of Optical Pooled Screen Analysis NeurIPS 2026
Matteo Di Bernardo, Claire Peterson, Ege Topkoc, Matt Might, Iain M. Cheeseman
Optical pooled screens connect genetic perturbations to changes in cell morphology and behavior, but turning the microscopy data into interpretable results takes complex, multi-day analyses. Established pipelines process this data, yet researchers still select parameters, inspect outputs, and resolve failures for each experiment. Here, we present Brieflow-auto, a pipeline-specific harness implemented as a Claude Code plugin that lets a coding large language model (LLM) operate Brieflow, an open-source end-to-end pipeline for optical pooled screening data. A researcher supplies experimental facts and approves the launch. An agent tunes parameters in an interactive notebook, and the harness validates the configuration, monitors execution, recovers from common failures, and checks output quality. For pipeline defects, the agent opens upstream issues proposing backward-compatible fixes for the broader community. In five previously analyzed screens, harness runs yielded more cells with a confident single-gene assignment than the original hand analyses. On a screen the pipeline was not designed for, the harness turned each failure into a reviewed upstream fix. More broadly, we propose that wrapping an established pipeline in a harness can substantially accelerate its use and the long-term reliability of its codebase.

☆ Learning the Shared Latent Structure of Cryo-EM Inverse Problems
Nithin Chalapathi, Theo Sternlieb, Ethan Han, Sonya M. Hanson, Pilar Cossio, Aditi S. Krishnapriyan, Ritwik Gupta
Understanding biomolecular function requires characterizing three-dimensional structure and conformational variability, for which cryogenic electron microscopy (cryo-EM) is a powerful tool. Cryo-EM produces noisy two-dimensional particle images in which molecular structure, particle pose, and imaging conditions are jointly encoded. Recovering biological information from these observations spans inverse problems including pose estimation, heterogeneity analysis, and reconstruction. These tasks are usually addressed independently despite shared protein structure and image formation, motivating a common representation across inverse problems. Since biologically meaningful differences may be spatially localized, we study self-distillation with complementary global and masked patch objectives. Because experimental dataset discrimination can reflect acquisition-specific cues, we develop controlled synthetic and experimental evaluations and find that structural discrimination benefits from dense spatial features. We introduce the EMber family of vision transformers, pre-trained on >50 million experimental particle images from 214 EMPIAR datasets together with simulated projections from 268,000 molecular assemblies. EMber improves upon existing methods at conformational and compositional discrimination by 14.85 and 14.26 percentage points, estimates conformational populations to within 1.59pp error with a linear probe, and produces particle assignments yielding reference-quality reconstructions in fewer epochs. We demonstrate how to learn representations that capture biological information for use across multiple cryo-EM inverse problems.
☆ Causal Integrated Gradients: Scalable Feature Attribution Along Causal Paths
Joran Michiels, Anis Ismail, Maarten De Vos, Alejandro Sifrim
Existing feature attribution methods that account for feature dependencies do not scale to high dimensions. We propose causal integrated gradients (CIG) and causal expected gradients (CEG), which extend integrated gradients to incorporate prior causal structure among input features while retaining its computational efficiency and, under conditions on the causal graph, its axiomatic guarantees. In many settings, some causal structure between features is already known, for example, that a transcription factor causally regulates a target gene's expression. Yet existing gradient-based methods cannot exploit this structure, instead crediting a feature's downstream effects over its true upstream cause, such as ranking the target gene above the transcription factor that regulates it. Our key idea is to embed this causal structure by routing the integration path along the causal graph. The method thereby decomposes each feature's attribution into a direct effect and effects mediated through its causal descendants. We evaluate CIG in three settings of increasing realism. First, on a synthetic benchmark with a known causal model, CIG's attributions best match the ground-truth causal effect from Pearl's do-calculus, matching the accuracy of causal Shapley values at a fraction of the computational cost. Second, on simulated single-cell gene expression data with a known gene regulatory network (Dyngen), CIG most accurately recovers the transcription factors driving the simulation. Third, on real interferon-$β$ stimulation data, where only an approximate causal graph is available, CIG better prioritizes the transcription factors driving the measured response than DeepSHAP and integrated gradients. Together, these results show that incorporating causal structure into gradient-based attributions recovers upstream drivers that existing methods miss, without sacrificing scalability.

♻ ☆ Imaging-anchored multiomics in cardiovascular disease: integrating cardiac imaging, bulk, single-cell, and spatial transcriptomics
Minh H. N. Le, Thanh-Huy Nguyen, Tao Li, Bao Quang Gia Le, Han H. Huynh, Monika Raj, Carl Yang, Min Xu, Tuan Vinh, Nguyen Quoc Khanh Le
Cardiovascular disease arises from interactions between inherited risk, molecular programmes, and tissue-scale remodelling that are observed clinically through imaging. Cardiac MRI (CMR), computed tomography (CT), and echocardiography are integral to routine cardiovascular care, while bulk RNA sequencing, single-cell RNA sequencing, and spatial transcriptomics are providing increasingly detailed molecular characterization of cardiac tissue. Yet, these imaging and molecular data are still analysed in largely separate pipelines. This review examines joint representations that link cardiac imaging phenotypes to transcriptomic and spatially resolved molecular states. An imaging-anchored perspective is adopted in which echocardiography, CMR, and CT define a spatial phenotype of the heart, and bulk, single-cell and spatial transcriptomics provide cell-type- and location-specific molecular context. We define the representation requirements of each modality, compare multimodal fusion strategies, and synthesize integrative pipelines for radiogenomics, spatial alignment, and image-based gene-expression prediction, together with their validation requirements, limitations, and failure modes. Spatial multiomic maps of human myocardium and atherosclerotic plaque, together with single-cell, spatial, and multimodal medical foundation models, are advancing imaging-anchored multiomics; however, cost, scalability, and tissue availability remain substantial barriers to large-scale cardiovascular translation.

♻ ☆ RetroTAC: Learning Route-Derived Synthetic Accessibility for PROTACs
Stefano Ribes, Jia Xin Zhu Zhu, Tingting Mo, Lukáš Hudec, Kostas Papadopoulos, Atanas Patronov, Rocío Mercado
Proteolysis-targeting chimeras (PROTACs) enable targeted protein degradation, expanding therapeutic opportunities beyond conventional inhibition. However, assembling their large, multifunctional structures creates synthetic challenges that hinder their discovery. Traditional synthetic-accessibility scores can overestimate this difficulty by penalizing PROTACs' molecular complexity while overlooking modular assembly from accessible building blocks. In contrast, retrosynthesis planning tools provide explicit routes but are computationally expensive, particularly for large molecules. Here, we introduce a human-interpretable, PROTAC-oriented route-derived score that rewards short synthetic depth and balanced joining of molecular fragments, and RetroTAC, a machine-learning surrogate that predicts this score directly from molecular structures. RetroTAC combines complementary models in an ensemble, achieving a root mean squared error of 0.13 and an $R^2$ of 0.65 on held-out molecules. We further evaluate its utility on PROTACs enumerated by recombining warheads, linkers, and E3-ligase ligands, followed by retrosynthetic planning and route analysis. Candidates selected for high predicted scores achieve a mean route-derived synthesizability of 0.73, compared with 0.31 for low-score candidates; 98.75% versus 51.00% are fully resolved under the tested search settings. These results support RetroTAC as a fast prioritization tool for virtual PROTAC libraries, connecting molecular screening with interpretable route characteristics while reserving explicit planning for selected candidates.

♻ ☆ TeraAgent: A Distributed Agent-Based Simulation Engine for Simulating Half a Trillion Agents
Lukas Breitwieser, Ahmad Hesam, Abdullah Giray Yağlıkçı, Mohammad Sadrosadati, Fons Rademakers, Onur Mutlu
Agent-based simulation is a method for studying highly complex systems in disciplines as diverse as biology, medicine, sociology, economics, finance, and more. In this method, an agent represents a small part of the system, such as a human cell, and, through interactions among many agents, the system's behavior emerges, like the growth of cancer in the human body. Real-world complex systems, such as a human organ, comprise billions of agents. However, the state-of-the-art agent-based simulation platform, BioDynaMo, does not support the distributed processing of a simulation across servers. This limitation restricts the scalability of agent counts to the computational resources available within a single server, hindering users from conducting larger and more meaningful simulations. In this paper, we introduce TeraAgent, a new distributed agent-based simulation engine. To overcome BioDynaMo's scalability limitation, TeraAgent enables efficient exchange of agent information across servers by implementing two solutions: 1) a tailored serialization mechanism that allows agents to be accessed and mutated directly at the receive buffer, and 2) delta encoding that significantly reduces data transfer across servers by leveraging the iterative nature of agent-based simulations. Built on our solutions, TeraAgent 1) enables extreme-scale simulations with half a trillion agents (292x that of BioDynaMo) on 84,096 CPU cores, 2) reduces time-to-result leveraging parallelism across compute nodes, 3) improves interoperability with third-party tools, and 4) provides users with more hardware flexibility. TeraAgent serializes agents with a median speedup of 110x (37x for deserialization) over the state-of-the-art ROOT I/O baseline, delivers 8x the agent update rate per CPU core of Biocellion, and improves in-situ visualization performance by 39x.
♻ ☆ MorphoBranch: A Fine-Structure-Preserving Workbench for Morphometric Analysis of Branched Cellular Structures
Song Zhiying, Ling Hanyi, Wu Junyi, Jiang Yangbo
Background and Objectives: Fluorescence-labeled cellular arbors provide readouts of neuronal and microglial morphology, but fine and weakly labeled processes are prone to fragmentation and false connections that bias skeleton-based measurements. ♻ ☆ Covariance Eigenspace Provides Latent Attribution of Longitudinal Effects in Brain Age Gap
Jason Scheffel, Saurabh Sihag
Brain age gap is a promising machine learning (ML)-driven bio\-marker of accelerated biological aging and is derived from neuroimaging data. Recent works on coVariance neural networks (VNN) for brain age gap prediction have provided an explainable and anatomically verifiable framework for brain age gap prediction. Notably, VNN outcomes are derived by filtering inputs along covariance eigenvectors, providing a mechanism for explaining their decisions in terms of latent (orthogonal) vectors. In this paper, we investigate the longitudinal brain age gap effects in a Mild Cognitive Impairment (MCI) cohort using a VNN-driven brain age gap prediction framework. Specifically, using Integrated Gradients (IG) as the attribution mechanism, our results demonstrate that the eigenspectrum of the anatomical covariance matrix explains the different longitudinal effects observed in brain age gap in amyloid positive vs amyloid negative subcohorts more prominently than individual brain regions ($83.2\%$ with the leading eigenvector versus a maximal value of $7.7\%$ for any brain region). Therefore, the covariance-based latent explanation identifies a key coordinated input pattern that carries most of the observed longitudinal effect in brain age gap.

☆ Predicting Unseen Scientific Interactions: Conditional Flow Models Hallucinate Non-Additive Effects NeurIPS 2026
Sam Gijsen, Moritz Seiler, Kerstin Ritter
Conditional generative models are increasingly used for scientific prediction beyond the direct training support. Yet scientific questions often concern whether one intervention changes the effect of another. Such interactions can be of primary interest while explaining little of the total variation, allowing models to appear accurate overall without recovering the relevant scientific effect. It remains unclear how these models learn interactions and when these successfully extrapolate to unseen conditions. We therefore study interaction recovery in flow-matching models using a physiological time-series simulator, drug dose-response data, combinatorial cell perturbations, and molecular conformer generations, where we separate recovery from hallucinated interaction structure. In the controlled setting, individual effects are learned much earlier than interactions and models hallucinate interactions where none exist. Across settings, we find qualitatively different generalization problems, with successful prediction depending on whether condition structure continues from observed to held-out targets. When it does, interactions are recoverable. If not, models can generate plausible responses while missing the interaction. We furthermore find that hallucinations increase as targets become less identifiable, and that successful interaction recovery in output space can fail under nonlinear readouts. Finally, we show that aggregate fidelity metrics can conceal whether models learn and extrapolate interactions.

☆ Feasibility of a Patient-Specific Digital Twin for Ventriculoperitoneal Shunt Trajectory Planning
Kritsanavis Chongsrid, Vich Yindeedej, Supaporn Konmun, Xulei Yang, Si Yong Yeo
Catheter obstruction is the commonest cause of shunt failure; freehand insertion at Keen's point leaves the tip free in cerebrospinal fluid in only one case in ten. We built a digital twin that reconstructs skull, scalp, ear and ventricles from preoperative CT by deterministic geometry, searches every entry around Keen's point for the perpendicular trajectory with the longest intraventricular length under safety constraints, and returns the plan as two ruler measurements from the ear and two depths. Executed freehand in 11 consecutive adults (IDEAL Stage 2a), it achieved Hayhurst Grade 1 placement in 10 of 11 against 22 of 213 historically (p = 9 x 10^-9), median entry and angular deviations 2.5 mm and 5.9 degrees, 1.4 cm more intraventricular catheter than Keen's point, and no intraoperative complication. The technique was unchanged; the gain came from choosing entry and depth per patient. Whether it lowers revision is the next stage's hypothesis.

☆ Maximum likelihood, neural networks and posterior mean for inferring fractional Brownian motion parameters
Daniel S. Han, Vaishnavi Ananthanarayanan, Matthew J. Simpson
Fractional Brownian motion (fBM) is a model of anomalous diffusion parameterised by a Hurst exponent $H \in (0,1)$ and a generalised diffusion coefficient $D_H > 0$. We jointly estimate $H$ and $D_H$ using maximum likelihood (MLE) from density measurements with Poisson counting noise and single-particle trajectory with Gaussian localisation error. We show that one density measurement in time cannot infer both $H$ and $D_H$ and that several time points are needed, which matters most for inference precision. For trajectories, we show that a neural network trained with a squared error loss approximates the posterior mean of $H$, which can be computed exactly at negligible cost. Paired with the profile-likelihood estimate of $D_H$, the posterior mean has the lowest root mean squared error in both $H$ and $D_H$, except near $H=0$ and $H=1$ where MLE estimates $H$ better. In vesicle trajectories from two cell types, $H$ and $D_H$ form a continuum rather than distinct modes of motion. The posterior probability that each window within a track is subdiffusive or superdiffusive shows that $26\%$ of MRC5 fibroblast and $21\%$ of HeLa vesicle trajectories contain both subdiffusive and superdiffusive windows, against $2\%$ and $7\%$ expected from estimation uncertainty alone. Intracellular cargoes therefore switch between subdiffusion and superdiffusion over time rather than forming populations with fixed $H$.
☆ ProtModernBERT: Compute-Efficient Protein Language Modeling
Alberto Martínez, Natalia Flechas Manrique, Llorenç Espinosa-Portalés, Luc Andrea
Large protein language models provide a powerful framework for learning sequence-function relationships and guiding experimental design. However, architectural improvements are underexplored, especially in low-compute regimes of relevance for resource-constrained research laboratories. We show that the training cost for encoder-only transformer architectures can be reduced by leveraging modern architectural refinements, offering an encouraging performance / compute-efficiency trade-off. We introduce ProtModernBERT, a modernized BERT-style model pre-trained on amino acid sequences, and compare it with ProtBert, Amplify, and ESM2 across a set of supervised structural tasks -encompassing secondary structure prediction, disorder prediction, and subcellular localization- and ProteinGym's zero-shot functional benchmark. We find that ProtModernBERT matches or closely tracks the performance of Amplify-120M and ProtBert with up to 80-90\% reduction in pre-training FLOPs, and narrows the gap to ESM2 when controlling for pre-training. Our analysis using a cluster-based validation protocol for hyperparameter selection and randomly initialized models disentangles the contributions of architecture and data, showing that modern components provide stronger inductive biases that are more consequential than parameter count in low-compute regimes.

☆ Structure Before Intelligence: Gating What Agentic Scientific Workflows Release
Ashish Acharya, Aman Al Amin, Hong Qin
An agentic scientific workflow can run to completion and still release wrong results: a silent numerical error, a stale input, or a defect in a shared upstream step can contaminate several outputs while a sibling branch stays correct. We treat trust in such results as a release decision. A harness around the workflow's directed acyclic graph (DAG) decides which outputs may be used: it enforces mandatory checks, contains failures to the affected branch, recomputes from tracked versions, adds validators, and releases a result only if a risk model estimates its probability of being invalid as low. Language-model agents work inside the harness, choosing corrective actions and reviewing releases, but cannot override it. On 60 held-out paired instances of two workflows with injected faults, we switched components on and off. Each supplied a different property. Containment and recovery raised the number of correct results. Validation supplied safety: fixed rules without validation released 37 invalid results, while validating every candidate released none. Risk estimation supplied efficiency, matching that safety with 31% fewer validators at somewhat lower yield, although better targeting than random validation was not shown. Lineage evidence helped on one workflow. The language model added no measurable benefit: whether invalid results escaped depended on the harness, not the model. This proof of concept points to a practical path: placing agents inside explicit release-control structure can keep released scientific results trustworthy, and it gives more capable agents a measurable baseline to improve on.

☆ Separating mixtures of single-fiber orientation distributions for increased sensitivity to crossing white matter fibers
Adam M. Saunders, Gaurav Rudravaram, Michael E. Kim, Elyssa M. McMaster, Yifan Shuai, Warren Boschen, Sophie Lin, Suzana Herculano-Houzel, Daniel C. Colvin, Yurui Gao, Kurt G. Schilling, Adam W. Anderson, Bennett Landman
Intro: Diffusion MRI allows for in vivo probing of brain tissue architecture via micron-level diffusion aggregated at the millimeter scale, but crossing white matter fibers decrease the sensitivity of downstream tasks such as microstructural modeling and tractography. Current fixel-based methods for identifying the number, orientation, and spread of fibers rely on finding the peaks of fiber orientation distributions from constrained spherical deconvolution. However, this method is biased by the spreads of nearby fibers and fails at angular separations lower than approximately 40-50 degrees. Methods: In this work, we perform a constrained nonlinear optimization called fiber segmentation by signal splitting and iterative least squares estimation (FISSILE), modeling each voxel as a mixture of single-fiber distributions. We use FISSILE to fit axially symmetric fibers in simulations and on three in vivo human subjects from the Human Connectome Project, an ex vivo raccoon brain, as well as the same human subject scanned twice at two different signal-to-noise ratios (SNRs). Results: On simulated data, FISSILE successfully identifies fibers at an angular separation of 40° and a volume fraction of 0.1 at an SNR as low as 30, while MRtrix3's fod2fixel is limited to 60° separation and 0.1 volume fraction at the same SNR. On acquired data, FISSILE identified 85.8% of white matter voxels on average in human brains and 79.4% in the raccoon with at least one fiber crossing, whereas fod2fixel identified 30.1% and 44.5%. The number of white matter fiber crossings was mildly sensitive to different noise levels in the human subject with two acquisitions. Conclusion: A method that models the crossing fiber phenomenon as an unmixing problem increases sensitivity to individual fibers for more precise characterization of anatomy.

☆ How large an effect would we have seen? Positive controls, detection floors and dataset pitfalls in scalp-EEG speech decoding
Blessing Isoyiza Adeika, Onyema Osuagwu
Objective. Studies that decode imagined speech from scalp electroencephalography (EEG) commonly validate their pipelines with a positive control, usually overt speech, and report null results without stating what effect size the pipeline could have detected. Approach. Using ds003626 ("Thinking out loud"), KaraOne and Track 3 of the 2020 International Brain-Computer Interface Competition, we audited decoding of overt speech, articulator preparation and imagined speech with artifact-channel subsets, band-limited decoding and trial selection, under a protocol that enforces split integrity by assertion. We injected class-dependent effects of known amplitude into the real recordings to measure the detection floor of five decoder families, and we checked the ds003626 labels against the dataset's acquisition protocol. Main results. On ds003626, two lip electrodes decoded overt speech at 0.7430 and the eight external channels at 0.7068, against 0.4231 for the 128-channel scalp montage, and no decodable signal survived in the 8-30 Hz band. On the other two corpora, within-subject accuracy rose monotonically with frequency into the range dominated by muscle activity. Band-power logistic regression detected an injected 8-30 Hz desynchronization of 0.54 uV under a criterion fixed in advance, whereas a compact convolutional network (EEGNet) detected nothing within subject up to 4.31 uV. In ds003626, the condition codes are the reverse of the intuitive reading, the class encoding changed between public releases, and condition is a deterministic function of time within each session. Significance. In these corpora the overt-speech positive control is dominated by myogenic signal and cannot validate a decoding pipeline. We recommend that speech-decoding studies report band-limited and artifact-channel accuracy, the direction of accuracy across frequency, and a measured detection floor.

☆ napari-TurboBox: Interactive Multi-Viewer Bounding Box Annotation for AI-Guided Volumetric Bioimaging
Nils Friederich, Philip Kohle, Maximilian Beichter, Mona Wellhäusser, Marcel Stahl, Pascal Zwick, Angelo Jovin Yamachui Sitcheu, Thomas Lautenschlager, David Exler, Linus Schilpp, Stephan Allgeier, Andreas Specker, Lennart Hilbert, Ralf Mikut
Promptable segmentation models turn one 3D bounding box into an instance mask. Box placement therefore limits how fast microscopy volumes with thousands of objects are annotated. Each box must be checked in several synchronized viewers, but napari-bbox shares no state between viewers. An edit must update every viewer and slows down with more boxes. napari-TurboBox instead holds one shared copy of the boxes for all viewers. In a four-viewer layout, napari-TurboBox needs a few milliseconds per mouse move to update the three slice viewers up to $10^4$ boxes. The same update in napari-bbox leaves the 33 ms budget beyond about $10^3$ boxes and takes 159 ms at $10^4$. Boxes stay axis-aligned and export to COCO and YOLO as per-slice 2D boxes. napari-TurboBox is a pure-Python plugin for napari 0.6.6 to 0.9. The code is available at https://github.com/N-Friederich/napari-turbobox under the Apache 2.0 licence, and version 0.1.0 is archived at https://doi.org/10.5281/zenodo.23090828 on Zenodo.

☆ Duration-Aware World Models for Sleep Staging with Dwell-Time Constraints
Junhui Gao
Closed-loop neuromodulation requires sleep staging that respects stage-specific minimum dwells: stimulation policies switch by sleep stage, and a policy change is permitted only after the current stage has persisted for its minimum duration. Discriminative classifiers fragment stage trajectories---for example, a 30 s Wake segment inside a 10 min N2 run---and even hidden semi-Markov smoothing leaves 11.3% of segments below the minimum dwell threshold. This paper presents a world model in which a recurrent state-space model (RSSM) learns dwell structure endogenously from EEG, via duration-aware latent sampling, a survival-curve dwell prior, and transition-matrix calibration, and a trajectory-level hard-constraint layer guarantees the minimum dwell at decoding. On SHHS-1 (200 subjects, 160/20/20, three repeated splits), the model reaches a test accuracy of 0.690 +/- 0.053 (95% CI over 3 folds) versus 0.641 +/- 0.042 for an identically split discriminative baseline, with macro-F1 +17.5 pp and better accuracy on all three folds. On Sleep-EDF the constraint layer cuts below-threshold segments from 71.8% to 0.1% with no accuracy loss (0.734 to 0.737), and an online locked scheduler reduces RL policy switches by 87% per night while keeping every policy run at least 90 s. To the best of the author's knowledge, this is the first system to couple learned dwell statistics with a decoding-time hard guarantee. Code and pretrained weights will be released.
