♻ ☆ Stochastic partial differential equation model for environmental DNA dynamics in river environments
Hidekazu Yoshioka
Environmental DNA (eDNA) has emerged as a novel tool for quantifying the seasonal abundance of aquatic species in water bodies; however, its mathematical modeling is still at a germinating stage because of its mechanistic uncertainties. We propose a first-step mathematical and computational framework for the eDNA dynamics of migratory fish based on a novel stochastic partial differential equation model with a delayed source input. The model governs spatiotemporal eDNA concentration in rivers where the source comes from a stochastic differential equation for the migration dynamics of the fish. The affine nature of the model facilitates its theoretical analysis, including the guarantee of well-posedness and the closed-form derivation of the Laplace functional despite the proportionality coefficient of the multiplicative noise term being non-Lipschitz. We also propose a discretization scheme for the model that theoretically generates nonnegative numerical solutions. We finally apply the proposed model to eDNA concentration data sampled from midstream reaches of a river system and perform sensitivity analysis.

♻ ☆ Scan-Coil Delay Causes Anisotropic Signal Loss in Fast 4D-STEM
Vishal Kumar, Andreas Jehle, Tizian Lorenzen, Julika Radecke, Knut Müller-Caspary, Massimo Kube, Henning Stahlberg
Fast pixelated detectors are driving 4D-STEM toward microsecond dwell times, a regime in which the finite response of the scan deflection coils becomes comparable to the dwell time itself. Using direct probe imaging and sub-frame diffraction analysis, we document a significant intra-dwell scan-coil delay that systematically smears the recorded signal anisotropically along the fast scan direction, with a settling timescale of several tens of microseconds. We present a phase-correlation-based sub-frame alignment procedure that measures and corrects this smearing, and we assess its impact on focused and defocused 4D-STEM reconstructions over a range of scan step sizes. The correction restores signal across a broad range of spatial frequencies, with the largest gains at the large step sizes required for low-dose biological imaging. Because it operates on existing data with no modification to the microscope, the method offers a practical route to recovering signal that would otherwise be lost to scan-coil delay.

♻ ☆ Sparse Contextual Coupling Reshapes Diffusion Geometry in Multilayer Hypergraphs
Hao Ding, Sanjukta Krishnagopal
Many complex systems combine dense background structure with sparse contextual information. We introduce a diffusion-based framework for analyzing how sparse condition-specific layers reshape diffusion geometry in multilayer hypergraphs. Each layer is represented as a weighted hypergraph, layers are coupled through shared entities, and random walks on the coupled system induce multiscale diffusion distances between nodes. We apply the framework to disease-conditioned gene networks by coupling a dense MSigDB functional gene-set layer to sparse disease-specific DGIdb drug-gene hypergraphs, with disease-associated drugs selected from DDDB and HumanNet-GSP used to define external gene weights. Community-level comparisons further reveal disease similarities not reducible to direct DGIdb gene overlap, including a Breast Cancer-Schizophrenia relationship consistent with recent biomedical evidence. These results show that sparse contextual layers can induce interpretable nonlocal changes in higher-order network geometry.
♻ ☆ AI Scientists for Building Virtual-Cell Models
Xiangru Tang, Jiapeng Chen, Daniel Shao, Zhuoyun Yu, Yan Cui, Weixu Wang, Fang Wu, Yuchen Zhuang, Wenqi Shi, Zhi Huang, Arman Cohan, Xihong Lin, Fabian Theis, Smita Krishnaswamy, Mark Gerstein

Whether they can go further and convert real, noisy and complex biological data into executable model hypotheses and architectures that encode testable assumptions remains unclear. Predicting how cells respond to genetic and chemical perturbations is a natural test of this ability: it is central to decoding disease mechanisms and therapeutic discovery, yet it demands cross-disciplinary expertise rarely concentrated in a single research group. Here, we introduce CellForge, an organized team of autonomous agents for single-cell perturbation modeling. Given a dataset and a task, it surveys the literature, parses the data, identifies relevant baselines, and formulates a model hypothesis through critic-mediated discussion among agents specialized in data modeling, single-cell biology, and deep learning. It then writes the training code, runs the experiments, and returns a trained predictor including architecture, code, and validation results. The architecture itself is the hypothesis: CellForge produces it end-to-end, with no human specifying the design, loss, or training protocol. Across six perturbation datasets spanning transcriptomic, epigenomic, and proteomic contexts, CellForge yields models that are competitive with hand-crafted state-of-the-art results on established tasks and extends the same workflow to paired multi-omic readouts. Blinded human evaluators rate CellForge's research plans as more scientifically sound and innovative than those from single LLMs or deep-research systems. In summary, we show autonomous agents can complete the full hypothesis-to-validation cycle in single-cell biology, with an AI effectively building another AI, in a complex setting that currently requires a team of human experts.

☆ MFG: Multi-turn Feedback Guided Molecular Optimization
Trieu Nguyen, Frazier N. Baker, Ruoxi Gao, Daniel Adu-Ampratwum, Xia Ning

☆ STAMP as a topology-aware molecular language for fragment-level learning and editing
Yuxuan Ren, Jiatong Li, Ziqiao Meng, Yatao Bian

☆ An efficient and flexible generalization of transit compartment models for delayed biological processes
Giulia Belluccini, Christiaan H. van Dorp, Linjie Huang, Jodie A. Cochrane, Sara Hamis, Tyler Cassidy
Transit compartment models are widely used in pharmacokinetics/pharmacodynamics to represent delayed biological processes such as absorption, maturation, cellular proliferation, and treatment-induced toxicity. This transit compartment formulation imposes an artificial relationship between the mean and variance of the delay distribution, as the number of transit compartment must be integer-valued number of transit compartments. Consequently, estimating the number of compartments from experimental data leads to mixed-integer optimization problems that artificially constrain model dynamics and bias parameter inference. However, these transit compartment models are mathematically equivalent to distributed delay differential equations with Erlang delay kernels. We leverage a hypoexponential generalization of these distributed delay differential equations that preserves the familiar ordinary differential equation structure while allowing the mean and variance of the delay distribution to be estimated directly as real-valued parameters. This approach does not increase the number of inferred parameters and can be immediately implemented as a straightforward modification of existing transit compartment models. We demonstrate the approach using models of viral eclipse phases, lymphocyte proliferation, and chemotherapy-induced neutropenia. Across these examples, the hypoexponential formulation provides comparable or improved fits relative to Erlang transit models and substantially reduces computational cost by avoiding grid search over compartment numbers. This framework provides a practical approach to estimate biologically meaningful delay distributions in quantitative systems pharmacology (QSP) models.
☆ ECG-RSI: Recursive Self-Improvement through Targeted Electrocardiogram Waveform Evolution
Xiaoliang Wang, Zhuo Chen, Chuanyang Zheng, Jiangwei Lao, Quanbin Wang, Honglin Lin, Jiajia Liu, Zhihong Lu, Hao Jin, Jian Wang, Lijun Wu
Electrocardiogram (ECG) models need supervision that targets evolving weaknesses and remains faithful to waveform evidence. We introduce ECG-RSI, a recursive self-improvement framework combining verified waveform synthesis with feedback-guided intervention search. Signal remeasurement verifies waveform edits; construction errors guide generator repair, while learner errors motivate interventions compared on a separate selection set. Across seven benchmarks, three rounds raise Qwen3.5-35B-A3B's mean question-answering accuracy from 34.57% to 56.04% and mean classification F1 from 24.27 to 34.03. Across the retained trajectory, the preferred supervision shifts from direct reinforcement to confusion-focused contrasts. Continued feedback correction yields diminishing external gains. These results motivate separating error diagnosis from intervention selection and checking whether gains on the feedback set transfer to independent benchmarks.

☆ MedEvo: Medical Reasoning Evolution through Knowledge Navigation
Xiaoliang Wang, Zhuo Chen, Chuanyang Zheng, Quanbin Wang, Jiangwei Lao, Yingao Liu, Jiajia Liu, Jinming Cao, Honglin Lin, Zhihong Lu, Hao Jin, Jian Wang, Lijun Wu
Medical multimodal reasoning models are typically trained on teacher-generated rationales, discarding the valid reasoning in students' own failed trajectories, while general-domain correction methods offer little guidance for errors in image reading, anatomical context, or differential diagnosis. We propose MedEvo, which uses medical knowledge, namely a tag taxonomy and error-type diagnosis, to navigate both trajectory repair and data selection. For each failed rollout, a gold-blind auditor locates the first consequential error and provides a minimal, answer-free hint; the same student retains the valid prefix and regenerates the erroneous suffix. After each round, MedEvo identifies capability gaps across medical tags on a held-out feedback set and prioritizes related questions for new rollouts and repairs. With supervised fine-tuning (SFT) alone, MedEvo raises Qwen3.5-9B mean accuracy across seven benchmarks from 65.57% to 69.12% (47.40% to 54.50% on the multimodal track of MedXpertQA (MedXpertQA-MM)), surpassing teacher-distillation SFT (66.93%). It also raises Qwen3.5-35B and Qwen3.5-397B mean accuracy from 67.91% to 73.04% and from 71.83% to 74.95%, respectively. Our analyses show that failed trajectories contain reasoning worth preserving, that repair success does not imply learning value, and that independent feedback is key to sustained improvement.

☆ An Open-Source Web-Based Viewer for Disseminating Image-Derived Models
Christian Herz, Kyle Sunderland, Matthew Daemer, Julia Iacovella, Sehdev Pressley, Ashley Koluda, Csaba Pinter, Kevin Zuber, John Daniels, Allen Ladd, Mali Hetmaniuk, Kevin K. Whitehead, Andras Lasso, Matthew A. Jolley
Patient-specific 3-dimensional (3D) models are increasingly central to planning surgery and catheter-based intervention in children with congenital heart disease (CHD), whose anatomy is often atypical and variable. These models inform care only when the surgeons and interventional cardiologists who make treatment decisions can rotate, crop, and measure them directly. Desktop modeling applications require local installation, capable hardware, and training. Commercial web-based viewers are portable but closed, and, in our experience, cannot display volume rendering, time-resolved (e.g., 4-dimensional) data, or the custom workflows centers build for CHD. Because CHD populations are small and heterogeneous, such workflows are unlikely to be prioritized commercially, and end users have no means to add them. To address these limitations, we developed the SlicerHeart Viewer, an open-source module that presents a simplified interface to a complete 3D Slicer/SlicerHeart session running on a server. Clinicians access models created by clinical modeling services from any device through a web browser. Because 3D Slicer runs underneath, volume rendering, time-resolved data, and any SlicerHeart workflow, including those developed for CHD, are available without modification. The module is independent of the hosting environment; we chose internal servers behind institutional single sign-on, and a cloud-hosted option is in development. Over one year at a single pediatric center, nearly all of 82 modeled cases were shared through the Viewer with clinicians from five specialties, with cases loading in 5 to 10 seconds. The approach is not specific to CHD and is deployable at other centers.

☆ PMO-Dock: Benchmarking Docking, Specificity, and Generalization in Molecular Optimization
Gor Simonyan, Tatevik Abrahamyan, Narek Abelyan, Tigran Fahradyan, Hrant Khachatrian
The Practical Molecular Optimization (PMO) benchmark standardized evaluation in molecular optimization, but it is built on simple property-based oracles that do not capture structure-based drug design. As the field has moved to docking-based objectives, evaluation practice has become unstandardized, and different methods report results on different docking tasks, often without a shared protocol for fair comparison or sample-efficiency constraints. To address this, we introduce Practical Molecular Optimization for Docking (PMO-Dock), a benchmark and protocol for docking-based optimization consisting of 25 tasks covering hit generation, lead optimization, and a new specificity task requiring strong on-target binding while penalizing off-target interactions. The protocol separates model development from final evaluation: validation tasks are used for hyperparameter tuning, and test tasks are held out for final evaluation and are not used during development. Evaluation on the test tasks is done under a strict oracle budget to reflect realistic drug-discovery settings. We benchmark four diverse high-performing methods spanning different optimization paradigms, Saturn (reinforcement learning), GenMol (discrete diffusion), Genetic-guided GFlowNet, and ChemLlama (LLM-based evolutionary search). Our results show no universally best method across tasks, additional analyses of hyperparameter transfer (cross-task and cross-category) and sensitivity show that transfer is often limited and varies by method, motivating method-dependent selection strategies. This benchmark provides a concrete standard for evaluating generalization to unseen tasks and sample efficiency under a limited test-time oracle budget.
♻ ☆ Forecasting Without Full Identification: Reliability and Computational Cost in Clot-Growth Simulation
Lennon J. Shikhman, Lily DiPaulo, Ying Qian, He Li
We forecast clot growth from sparse early measurements with incomplete biochemical information. Using 1,000 simulated trajectories, we compare inverse search, which weights candidate inputs by their agreement with early observations, with a direct multilayer perceptron (MLP) predicting future clot size. Inverse search is more accurate when tissue factor alone, or tissue factor and fibrinogen, are unknown. When all five inputs are unknown, direct prediction achieves 2.16% pooled relative $L_2$ forecast error, outperforming search with 8,192 candidates using a forward MLP (3.25%) or latent neural ordinary differential equation model (3.36%). A conditional error bound separates candidate disagreement, weight on poor early fits, surrogate error, and candidate coverage. Increasing randomized quasi-Monte Carlo candidate budgets from 512 to 32,768 reduces numerical variation. A covariance score ranks additional assays by expected forecast value while accounting for measurement noise. At 10% range-scaled assay noise, incorporating assay uncertainty reduces forecast error from about 5% to below 3% for both surrogates. An independent reaction cascade verifies candidate integration against tensor quadrature, with mean normalized forecast-integration error below $10^{-5}$ at 32,768 candidates. These results demonstrate accurate clot-growth forecasting without full biochemical identification and support selecting prediction strategies and additional measurements according to accuracy, reliability, and computational cost.

♻ ☆ Estimating marginal association between paired outcomes in clustered data with an application to analyzing caries and periodontal disease data from the National Health and Nutrition Examination Survey
Owen Visser, Steven Levy, Bruce Dye, Shaoping Zhang, Victor Agboli, Somnath Datta
The within-cluster subgroup composition of units can inform on the marginal distribution of those units outcomes, denoted informative subgroup size (ISS). Moreover, within-cluster subgroup composition of paired outcomes can allow one outcome to inform upon the marginal distribution of the other. If this type of ISS ignored, it can bias marginal association estimates by allowing subgroup composition to influence how clusters contribute to the analysis. We develop two weighting approaches for estimating marginal association between paired outcomes in this setting. The weights are derived using within-cluster resampling arguments and extend conventional cluster weighting to paired response subgroups. We adapt an existing test of marginal dependence to assess ISS by examining this subgroup structure in both directions. In simulated data exhibiting this bidirectional ISS, pair-based weighting reduced bias when association was primarily carried through the paired observations. These weights also allow scientifically or clinically meaningful dichotomizations to enter directly into the weighting scheme, with the choice of subgroup definition further improving estimation in several simulated settings. Application to NHANES oral-health data showed strong evidence of ISS for filled-surface outcomes across subgroups defined by clinical attachment loss and pocket depth, with the corresponding periodontal--caries associations generally small and positive. These methods provide a practical approach for estimating marginal association when paired subgroup composition is informative.

☆ Calibrating AlphaFold confidence scores for interface quality
Sagyndyk Sagadinov, Vsevolod Viliuga, Arne Elofsson
AlphaFold confidence scores are widely used to rank protein complex predictions and to select subsets of models for downstream applications. Although these scores provide strong discriminatory power for distinguishing prediction quality, their numerical values were never intended to represent the probability that a prediction satisfies a given structural quality criterion. In this work, we calibrate commonly used confidence scores to estimate the probability that a predicted complex exceeds a chosen threshold of DockQ, an established measure of interface quality. On antibody-antigen and general multimer datasets, post-hoc isotonic calibration consistently improves agreement between predicted probabilities and observed DockQ success rates while preserving the underlying score ordering. The calibrated probabilities also provide more accurate estimates of the fraction of selected models expected to pass a given structural quality threshold. Although the mapping is fitted within a given prediction distribution and therefore transfers imperfectly across prediction domains, we show that reliable estimates are recovered from a small number of labeled target-domain samples. Because the calibration is entirely post hoc and requires no underlying AlphaFold model fine-tuning, it provides a straightforward and customizable way to convert existing model quality rankings into calibrated probabilities of passing a chosen structural quality threshold.
♻ ☆ QSAR-Guided Generative Framework for the Discovery of Synthetically Viable Odorants

The discovery of novel odorant molecules is key for the fragrance and flavor industries, yet efficiently navigating the vast chemical space to identify structures with desirable olfactory properties remains a significant challenge. Generative artificial intelligence offers a promising approach for de novo molecular design but typically requires large sets of molecules to learn from. ☆ Probability generating functions for epidemiological time series
Mariah C. Boudreau, Guillaume St-Onge
Probability generating functions (PGFs) are a fundamental tool in mathematical epidemiology to characterize the stochastic evolution of epidemics via branching process theory. However, they are rarely used as operational tools in real-time during an outbreak, the main barrier being a lack of a systematic, computational methodology to describe epidemiological time series, such as cases or hospital admissions over time. Here, we introduce a general and computationally efficient framework for outbreak analytics based on PGFs. We first develop a method to encode joint distributions of incidences and their noisy measurements by realistic surveillance systems. Then, by transforming PGFs to cumulant generating functions (CGFs), we enable efficient evaluation of these joint distributions. We demonstrate the practicality of our approach for Bayesian inference and short-term forecasts, offering a significant computational advantage over simulation-based methods. Altogether, our framework operationalizes generating functions and paves the way for their broader adoption in computational epidemiology.

☆ From Simulated Fluids to Living Tissue: Zero-Shot Foundation Model Transfer to Perturbed Embryos
Louisa Cornelis, Haewon Jeong, Irina Espejo, Payel Mukhopadhyay
Morphogenesis is driven by the interplay of genetic regulation and tissue mechanics. In vivo video data is limited, particularly under genetic mutations and environmental perturbations, making generalization beyond the most representative conditions in the dataset challenging. However, at tissue scales, the dynamics of Drosophila can be described as a continuum flow, motivating the usage of representations learned across other physical systems as priors for developmental dynamics. We investigate finetuning Walrus, a Continuum Dynamics Foundation Model pretrained across a broad set of simulated continuous systems, on tissue velocity fields from only 12 wild-type Drosophila blastoderm videos. We evaluate autoregressive prediction both in-distribution and zero-shot under unseen temperature and genetic perturbations. Zero-shot refers to evaluation on perturbation conditions never seen during wild-type finetuning, without any additional training. In-distribution, a Fourier Neural Operator model trained from scratch achieves best performance on VRMSE. However, Walrus (CRPS) is the strongest model under the most severe distribution shift of unseen samples of mutated embryos and temperature conditions. Interestingly, predictions capture the shape of the mean surface tissue velocity trajectory and its separation between 17°C and 27°C, including the timing of the peak at Germ Band Extension onset, a biologically meaningful milestone of embryo development. Our results provide preliminary support that representations learned from different dynamical systems can transfer to experimental morphogenesis in developmental regimes, provided they admit a continuum description. More broadly, our results motivate further study of physics foundation models as a new complementary tool for the simulation of morphogenesis in low-data regimes.

☆ AutoNovo: Agentic Program Search Discovers Transferable Nucleic Acid Design Algorithms NeurIPS 2026
Joel Shor, Subhashini Venugopalan
In-silico optimization of nucleic acids relies on predictive oracles that imperfectly capture biological function, so sequences optimized against one model may exploit model-specific artifacts and fail to generalize to independent models. Recent studies have shown that not all designers generalize equally well to independent models. In this work, we use agentic program search to iteratively propose, execute, evaluate, and revise code for nucleic-acid designers. We describe the successful strategies used to prompt the search. AutoNovo designers achieved the best observed mean among the evaluated methods on 3/3 DNA-design tasks in terms of in-silico performance, and 6/6 transfer tasks in terms of independent models. The designers come from a family of discovered nucleic acid designers rather than a single universal best designer. We plan to release the resulting designers. These results are the first steps in creating nucleic acid designers that maximize cross-model generalization, and thus improving the odds that in-silico designed sequences reflect real biology.

☆ Physiology-Informed Sparse Identification of Dynamic Crop Models
Corné Verburg, Alexander Heinlein, Fred van Eeuwijk, Neil Budko
Developing resilient crops requires models that accurately capture how plant growth dynamics emerge from the continuous interplay between genetic and environmental factors. While high-throughput phenotyping platforms generate data for dynamic growth models, current modelling approaches show limitations: process-based crop growth models (CGMs) capture environmental dynamics but are difficult to scale across many genotypes because they require calibration of many parameters, while quantitative genetic models allow modelling many genotypes but often summarise time-varying environments using static covariates. To address this gap, we propose a data-driven framework to identify reduced systems of ordinary differential equations (ODEs) describing crop growth dynamics as functions of environmental variables and a controllable number of genotype-specific coefficients (GSCs). Using the Sparse Identification of Nonlinear Dynamics (SINDy) method, we infer model structure directly from data via sparse regression on a physiologically informed library of candidate functions. In this framework, GSCs parameterise cultivar-specific differences in response to environmental conditions. The resulting models are linear in these GSCs, making them compatible with mixed-model and genomic prediction approaches. We evaluate the approach on an in silico dataset generated with WOFOST for five potato cultivars across twelve growing seasons. Our identified ODEs generalise to held-out years and achieve $R^2 > 0.9$ for end-of-season yield prediction across held-out years when compared to full-scale WOFOST simulations. These results provide a proof of concept for SINDy-based identification of parsimonious dynamic crop models from longitudinal crop-growth data.
♻ ☆ Characterizing Fungal Infections in the All of Us Research Program
Md Fantacher Islam, Emeral Norzagaray, Corneliu Antonescu, Vignesh Subbian
Fungal infections, such as coccidioidomycosis, aspergillosis, and histoplasmosis, represent a growing public health concern in the United States. However, the actual burden of these infections is often underestimated by traditional surveillance methods. Therefore, this study characterizes these infections within the All of Us Research Program and evaluates the quality of related clinical data. We constructed three cohorts, coccidioidomycosis (n = 1,171), aspergillosis (n = 687), and histoplasmosis (n = 347), among 393,601 participants with electronic health record data. We analyzed geographic and sociodemographic distributions, disease reporting, and host factors, and performed a data quality assessment on 10 key laboratory biomarkers and organism-specific tests within 90 days of diagnosis. Our analysis confirmed known epidemiological patterns, including the geographic distributions of coccidioidomycosis in the Southwest and histoplasmosis in the Midwest. Aspergillosis is not routinely reportable, and 49.3% of histoplasmosis cases occurred in states without reporting mandates. Fungal infections disproportionately affected older adults and males relative to baseline, and 44.7% of aspergillosis cases were in immunocompromised patients. The data quality assessment showed high completeness for general hematology markers (e.g., hemoglobin > 70% in coccidioidomycosis and aspergillosis) but limited availability of organism-specific tests, such as Coccidioides serology (4.6% in coccidioidomycosis). Most coccidioidomycosis hemoglobin values (97.9%) were reported in g/L despite a g/dL label. In conclusion, the All of Us dataset is a valuable resource for characterizing fungal infections, but data quality issues related to completeness and conformance must be addressed to support robust clinical research.
♻ ☆ iBitter-Stack: A Multi-Representation Ensemble Learning Model for Accurate Bitter Peptide Identification
Sarfraz Ahmad, Momina Ahsan, Muhammad Nabeel Asim, Andreas Dengel, Muhammad Imran Malik
The identification of bitter peptides is crucial in various domains, including food science, drug discovery, and biochemical research. These peptides not only contribute to the undesirable taste of hydrolyzed proteins but also play key roles in physiological and pharmacological processes. However, experimental methods for identifying bitter peptides are time-consuming and expensive. With the rapid expansion of peptide sequence databases in the post-genomic era, the demand for efficient computational approaches to distinguish bitter from non-bitter peptides has become increasingly significant. In this study, we propose a novel stacking-based ensemble learning framework aimed at enhancing the accuracy and reliability of bitter peptide classification. Our method integrates diverse sequence-based feature representations and leverages a broad set of machine learning classifiers. The first stacking layer comprises multiple base classifiers, each trained on distinct feature encoding schemes, while the second layer employs logistic regression to refine predictions using an eight-dimensional probability vector. Extensive evaluations on a carefully curated dataset demonstrate that our model significantly outperforms existing predictive methods, providing a robust and reliable computational tool for bitter peptide identification. Our approach achieves an accuracy of 96.09\% and a Matthews Correlation Coefficient (MCC) of 0.9220 on the independent test set, underscoring its effectiveness and generalizability. To facilitate real-time usage and broader accessibility, we have also developed a user-friendly web server based on the proposed method, which is freely accessible at https://ibitter-stack-webserver.streamlit.app/. This tool enables researchers and practitioners to conveniently screen peptide sequences for bitterness in real-time applications.

♻ ☆ Learning When Visual Context Matters for Mouse Behavior Analysis
Weihan Li, Jingyang Ke, Qinheng Pu, Yule Wang, Chengrui Li, Anqi Wu
Understanding mouse behavior at scale is a foundational problem in neuroscience, with two core tasks: classifying frames into known behaviors and segmenting recordings into recurring patterns without labels. Pose-based methods are the standard approach but face fundamental limitations: pose coordinates capture only skeletal information, discard environmental context, and degrade under frequent occlusions in social scenarios. Mouse videos can provide the visual context that pose lacks, yet not all frames benefit equally, and adding visual features uniformly to every frame is suboptimal. We find that a pose-based motion encoder, trained with auxiliary supervision from videos, can identify where it struggles: with a discrete representation, the encoder learns a codebook of recurring patterns, and frames whose motion does not fit this codebook produce large quantization residuals. We show that these high-residual frames are where visual context tends to help most: residual-guided frame selection consistently outperforms uniform selection across budgets, and matches or exceeds dense visual processing in most settings. We therefore propose ResiFuse, a framework that pretrains a motion encoder on pose with an auxiliary cross-modal loss and integrates vision context only on high-residual frames at fine-tuning time. We evaluate on three mouse behavior datasets and show improvements over pose- and video-based baselines on most tasks, with the largest gains on the most socially complex dataset.

♻ ☆ Audit Before You Update: Recording Artifacts and the Limits of Bounded Model Updates in MIMIC-IV ICU Prediction
Fatema Ferdous Tamanna, K. M. Merajul Arefin, Md. Abdul Masud
Clinical prediction models in intensive care degrade as data shift, and regulators such as the FDA ask developers to specify bounded update protocols in advance. Evaluations of such protocols assume that apparent drift reflects clinical practice rather than changes in how the database records care. We test whether update strategies can be evaluated on unaudited ICU data, and whether a parameter-bounded update gives up accuracy or attribution stability relative to full fine-tuning once the data are audited. We audit MIMIC-IV 3.1 (2008-2022) for recording artifacts and define a cleaning protocol. On the cleaned cohort, a label-free classifier two-sample test gates updates across three eras. We compare a bounded update, which retrains only treatment-context and fusion parameters, with full fine-tuning for two model families and two targets under a pre-specified non-inferiority margin of 0.015 AUROC, and measure attribution stability against the source model. In 2020-2022 about half of ICU stays have no input or procedure-event records, and a vasopressor ItemID migration makes the 2017-2019 label rate appear to fall when it actually rises. On the cleaned cohort, the gate detects drift in every era, including in physiology-only features, with no false alarms on null data. Bounded updating is non-inferior or superior in 10 of 12 primary cells, but this depends on the target, the model family and learning-rate tuning, and adaptation gains over never updating are small. At a shared learning rate, bounded updates keep physiological attributions more stable in neural models, clearly for vasopressor and less clearly for intubation; with separately tuned rates, full updates are more stable. Audits should precede update evaluation, and the update boundary should be chosen per target.

♻ ☆ Unraveling phenotypic variation in multiscale behavioral dynamics
Gautam Sridhar, Claire Wyart, Antonio Carlos Costa
Behavioral variability reflects differences in internal and external parameters and provides a substrate for natural selection. However, uncovering its structure is challenging because behavior evolves nonlinearly across multiple timescales. Our goal is to reconstruct phenotypic variation from finite behavioral observations without prescribing the scales at which individuals should be compared. To achieve this, we introduce a multiscale dissimilarity that compares dynamics across all statistically resolvable resolutions by constructing reduced transfer operators at progressively finer scales and weighting their differences by finite-sampling uncertainty. This dissimilarity defines a geometry of dynamical phenotypes, which we reconstruct using diffusion maps. We show that the resulting phenotypic coordinates locally follow the parameter directions to which dynamics are most sensitive. We validate our approach in a stochastic model and apply it to bacterial and larval zebrafish behavior. In bacteria, we identify run speed as a major component of inter-individual variability, associated with the chemotaxis protein CheB. In zebrafish, we find that prior prey experience reshapes behavioral dynamics and phenotypic variability, with paramecia experience concentrating fish in a prey-capture-associated region of phenotypic space. Together, these results provide a framework for identifying internal and environmental variables that structure dynamical phenotypes across scales.

♻ ☆ RNAGenScape: Property-Guided, Optimized Generation of mRNA Sequences with Manifold Langevin Dynamics NeurIPS 2026
Danqi Liao, Chen Liu, Xingzhi Sun, Dié Tang, Haochen Wang, Scott Youlten, Srikar Krishna Gopinath, Haejeong Lee, Ethan C. Strayer, Antonio J. Giraldez, Smita Krishnaswamy
Generating property-optimized mRNA sequences is central to applications such as vaccine design and protein replacement therapy, but remains challenging due to limited data, complex sequence-function relationships, and the narrow space of biologically viable sequences. Generative methods that drift away from the data manifold can yield sequences that fail to fold, translate poorly, or are otherwise nonfunctional. We present RNAGenScape, a property-guided manifold Langevin dynamics framework for mRNA sequence generation that operates on a learned manifold of real data. By performing iterative local optimization constrained to this manifold, RNAGenScape preserves favorable computational viability proxies, accesses reliable guidance, and discourages excursions into regions poorly supported by the training data. The framework integrates three components: (1) an autoencoder jointly trained with a property predictor to learn a property-organized latent manifold, (2) a denoising autoencoder that projects updates back onto the manifold, and (3) a property-guided Langevin dynamics procedure that performs optimization along the manifold. Across three real-world mRNA datasets spanning two orders of magnitude in size, RNAGenScape increases median oracle-predicted property gain by up to 267% and success rate by up to 31%, while maintaining good biological viability proxy measures and achieving competitive inference efficiency compared to existing generative approaches.

☆ Data-Driven Discovery of a Hyperelastic Strain-Energy Function for Veins Using Constitutive Artificial Neural Networks
Marisa Bazzi, Divya Adil, Gavin Mays, Ellen Kuhl, Christopher K. Breuer, Jay D. Humphrey, Alison L. Marsden
Veins are commonly subjected to altered mechanical loading in clinical settings such as vein grafting, arteriovenous fistula creation, and tissue-engineered vascular graft implantation, making accurate constitutive models of venous tissue clinically important. Venous mechanical behavior differs characteristically from that of arteries, and existing constitutive models struggle to simultaneously fit the circumferential and axial stress-stretch responses across vein types. Here, we present a constitutive artificial neural network (CANN) framework that discovers a compact, interpretable hyperelastic constitutive model from biaxial mechanical data across three types of veins (saphenous, jugular, and inferior vena cava). The framework incorporates an expanded candidate function library with structurally motivated terms representing progressive fiber recruitment and fiber-family interactions. Sobol sensitivity analysis, correlation-based pruning, and response-level family collapse were then used to reduce the discovered basis to a compact strain-energy density function (SEF). The resulting eight-term SEF achieved significantly higher fitting accuracy than established venous constitutive models across all three types of veins $(R^2>0.9, p<0.01)$. These findings establish a framework for constitutive model discovery in veins and propose a candidate constitutive form for further validation across larger, independent cohorts.

☆ Homogeneity and heterogeneity of functional element composition of genome units across human and seven model organisms
Yanying Wu
Is a chromosome a miniature of its genome, with the same mix of functional elements as the whole? We ask this of eight annotated genomes: human, mouse, zebrafish, Caenorhabditis elegans, Drosophila melanogaster, Saccharomyces cerevisiae, Arabidopsis thaliana and maize. Elements are classified in a four-level hierarchy, from five major classes (protein-coding genes, ncRNA genes, transposable elements (TEs), pseudogenes, IG/TR gene segments) down to finer classes. Units are chromosomes or arms, cytogenetic bands, equal cuts of each chromosome, and random segments of matching size. We compare each unit with the genome and with other units, call compositions homogeneous when they differ by less than 5%, and score each against a random sample of the same size. The first finding is a surprising homogeneity. The level-1 compositions of the major chromosomes match each other and the genome in maize, zebrafish, yeast and C. elegans, and in fly down to the major arms. Finer classes can be homogeneous too: protein function in Arabidopsis, and the Wicker TE orders in maize. Enzyme reaction classes are interchangeable between chromosomes in all eight genomes. The second finding is a structured heterogeneity. Where a unit departs from its genome, it does so in one of three ways: clusters of named elements, such as rRNA genes or immune receptor loci; megabase-scale compartments, such as TE-rich pericentromeric heterochromatin; and lineage-specific gene families, visible only at the finest levels. Corrected for chance, the genomes fall into groups. For protein function and gene families, composition is more uniform in plants and fly than in vertebrates, and least uniform in C. elegans. For the major classes and TE orders, uniformity follows where each genome places its repeats, along the arms (maize, zebrafish) or around the centromeres (Arabidopsis, fly), not its lineage.

☆ Bayesian adaptive design for neural recruitment curves
Vishweshwar Tyagi, Saihari S. Dukkipati, Daniel Twili, Jonas M. Mikhaeil, Andrew Gelman, Jason B. Carmel, James R. McIntosh
Nonlinear response models remain ubiquitous across disciplines with recruitment curves describing muscle response to neural stimulation. Conventional acquisition methods typically rely on static uniform sampling, requiring many stimuli, while adaptive threshold-hunting designs such as ML-PEST forfeit accurate characterization of the overall curve. We present a Bayesian adaptive design that uses posterior uncertainty over curve parameters to propose stimuli that maximize information gain across the full recruitment curve. We additionally implement a focused acquisition method that can target arbitrary subset of curve parameters, which we use to evaluate and interpret our design. In simulations, our design outperforms uniform sampling in recovering threshold while matching ML-PEST, and outperforms ML-PEST in predictive curve accuracy while matching uniform. It likewise matches our threshold-focused acquisition, suggesting our design doesn't trade off threshold estimation for recovering the curve. We further show data collected using our design yields greater power when detecting a change in threshold in intervention studies. Our design proposes the next stimulus within 5 seconds, making it feasible for transcranial magnetic stimulation (TMS) experiments. Finally, we validate our design in TMS experiments and provide a library for Python, called onMEP, for online acquisition of neural recruitment curves.
☆ Ensemble-Based Modelling of Optogenetic Bioprocesses under Phototoxicity
Yufan Liu, Sam Ferrer, Sebastián Espinel-Ríos
Optogenetics enables methanol-free protein production in the yeast $\textit{Komagataella phaffii}$ using light as a control input, facilitated by the blue-light-responsive transcription factor EL222. This opens the door to more programmable protein production in the food and biopharmaceutical industries. However, excessive illumination can inhibit growth and protein accumulation in some of these engineered cell factories. Previous hybrid modelling used black-box regression across sparsely sampled conditions to infer light-dependent kinetic parameters, without explicitly representing intracellular regulation. Here, we develop a multiscale mechanistic model linking EL222 activation, transcription, and translation to protein, biomass, and substrate dynamics. A latent light-associated stress state represents photoinhibitory effects on growth, transcription, and translation. Parameter variability was propagated to time-course predictions, providing an empirical assessment of parameter-related uncertainty. Wider prediction bands coincided with less complete experimental coverage of the growth phases. Overall, the framework provides a mechanistic basis for future model-based optimisation and control of optogenetic bioprocesses under phototoxicity.
☆ Amortised Global Sensitivity Analysis of Pharmacokinetic models for Simulated Clinical Trials
Daniele Pessina, Roberto A. Abbiati, Maria M. Papathanasiou
Pharmacokinetic and pharmacokinetic-pharmacodynamic models are used in simulated clinical trials to compare dosing protocols and patient groups before a trial is run. Distinct virtual patient cohorts, representing renal impairment, obesity and overdosage, can impose different input distributions on the same model. Global sensitivity analysis (GSA) identifies which uncertain inputs drive the cohort's predictions, but a direct analysis incurs its full computational cost for every cohort. An alternative accounting is proposed here: a single surrogate is trained on an input envelope wide enough to contain every cohort, each cohort is then analysed using that surrogate, and the reported quantity is the number of cohorts at which the surrogate route has cost fewer mechanistic evaluations than the direct route. The study tests three GSA methods: variance-based GSA, Morris screening and optimal transport. It covers four PK and PK/PD models with seven to twenty-three free inputs, 21 cohorts and five surrogates. Every estimate is scored against a converged mechanistic solution obtained with its own method, and all costs are expressed in mechanistic model evaluations. The parameter ranking is recovered at low cost: gradient-boosted trees reconstruct it on 20 of the 21 cohorts and cost fewer evaluations to train than one direct cohort analysis, on all four models, at break-even counts between 0.02 and 0.14 cohorts. Amortisation is fastest at the highest dimensions, where each direct variance-based sample of a $D$-input model requires $D+2$ evaluations while the training budget does not scale accordingly. Recovering absolute index values is harder, and fewer surrogates manage it as inputs grow: two of the five surrogates fail at fifteen inputs, where the gradient-boosted trees and the Gaussian process still succeed, and at twenty-three inputs no surrogate succeeds on a majority of cohorts.

☆ Bayesian Uncertainty Quantification of Breast-Cancer Pathology AI Agreement under Reader- and Annotation-Condition-Dependent Reference Labels
Hammed A. Olayinka
Human reference labels used to evaluate medical AI can vary across readers and annotation workflows. ☆ Association of Sex Hormone-Binding Globulin with Glycated Hemoglobin and Multidimensional Biomarker Structure in Reproductive-Age Women: An Analysis Based on NHANES 2021--2023
Ruilin Miao
Background: Sex hormone-binding globulin (SHBG) is linked to adiposity, insulin resistance and type 2 diabetes, but nationally representative evidence on the shape of the continuous SHBG-HbA1c association in reproductive-age women remains limited. Methods: We analyzed NHANES August 2021-August 2023 data for nonpregnant women aged 20-44 years (n=843). Survey-weighted linear regression related log-transformed SHBG to continuous HbA1c, with quadratic and spline tests for nonlinearity and tests for age modification. Forty biomarkers in hormone, biochemical, hematologic and inflammation/lipid blocks were analyzed by multiple factor analysis, sparse PLS and LASSO with bootstrap stability selection, with nested cross-validation. Results: After adjustment for age, race/ethnicity, poverty-income ratio and BMI, ln(SHBG) was inversely associated with HbA1c (beta=-0.1454, P=0.00083). Quadratic (P=0.00386) and spline (P=0.00236) tests supported nonlinearity, with a steeper slope at lower SHBG. The ln(SHBG)-by-age interaction was significant (P=0.0169). Adjustment for BMI, waist circumference and HOMA-IR attenuated the coefficient by 43.7%, 48.1% and about 55%. SHBG remained associated with HbA1c after conditioning on leading biomarker axes (beta=-0.1062, P=0.00342) and was selected in 98.8% of 500 LASSO bootstrap resamples. Nested cross-validation gave out-of-fold R^2 of 0.1298, 0.2136 and 0.2392 for clinical, biomarker and combined models. Conclusions: Higher SHBG was associated with lower HbA1c in a nonlinear pattern among reproductive-age women. The association overlapped with adiposity and insulin resistance but persisted after adjustment. The cross-sectional design and limited fasting subsample preclude causal or clinical interpretation.
♻ ☆ RNAGenScape: Property-Guided, Optimized Generation of mRNA Sequences with Manifold Langevin Dynamics NeurIPS 2026
Danqi Liao, Chen Liu, Xingzhi Sun, Dié Tang, Haochen Wang, Scott Youlten, Srikar Krishna Gopinath, Haejeong Lee, Ethan C. Strayer, Antonio J. Giraldez, Smita Krishnaswamy
Generating property-optimized mRNA sequences is central to applications such as vaccine design and protein replacement therapy, but remains challenging due to limited data, complex sequence-function relationships, and the narrow space of biologically viable sequences. Generative methods that drift away from the data manifold can yield sequences that fail to fold, translate poorly, or are otherwise nonfunctional. We present RNAGenScape, a property-guided manifold Langevin dynamics framework for mRNA sequence generation that operates on a learned manifold of real data. By performing iterative local optimization constrained to this manifold, RNAGenScape preserves favorable computational viability proxies, accesses reliable guidance, and discourages excursions into regions poorly supported by the training data. The framework integrates three components: (1) an autoencoder jointly trained with a property predictor to learn a property-organized latent manifold, (2) a denoising autoencoder that projects updates back onto the manifold, and (3) a property-guided Langevin dynamics procedure that performs optimization along the manifold. Across three real-world mRNA datasets spanning two orders of magnitude in size, RNAGenScape increases median oracle-predicted property gain by up to 267% and success rate by up to 31%, while maintaining good biological viability proxy measures and achieving competitive inference efficiency compared to existing generative approaches.

☆ Designing Protein Structures from Natural Language
Joseph Lee, Yanjing Li, Bo Qiang, Gregory Lau, Allyson Ettinger, Noah A. Smith, Rory Donovan-Maiye, David Baker, Hanna Hajishirzi, Pang Wei Koh
A central challenge in de novo protein design is generating proteins with desired structural and functional properties. Existing design models typically require a dedicated input representation for each property, limiting the design objectives they support. We develop Prompteina, the first all-atom structure generation model that supports new and broader design objectives specified in natural language, analogous to text-to-image generation. ☆ MORAD: Reinforcement Learning with Multi-Objective Rewards for RNA Inverse Design
Jixin Tang, Ji Guo, Jun Zhao
We introduce MORAD, an online reinforcement learning framework for backbone-conditioned RNA diffusion models that trains a single shared policy across diverse target backbones. Structure-conditioned RNA inverse design seeks nucleotide sequences compatible with a target three-dimensional backbone, yet existing reinforcement learning approaches either optimize a separate policy for each target backbone or rely on offline preference optimization, leaving online post-training of a reusable backbone-conditioned model largely unexplored. MORAD combines backbone-conditioned group sampling, group-relative reward normalization, and a forward-process diffusion objective. Beyond existing RL rewards that primarily target tertiary structural quality, MORAD jointly optimizes six measurements spanning tertiary geometry, base pairing, ensemble behavior, and sequence composition. To aggregate these heterogeneous objectives despite differences in scale and optimization direction, MORAD maps each measurement to a bounded desirability score and combines them with a weighted geometric mean. Compared with a tertiary-structure-only reward, this multi-objective formulation improves 10 of the 11 evaluation metrics that have a preferred direction. Applied to pretrained RIDE, MORAD improves GDT-TS by 16.0% (from 0.3300 to 0.3827) and pairing MCC by 19.4% (from 0.6110 to 0.7295), while reducing normalized ensemble defect by 27.5% (from 0.3906 to 0.2830). On the test set, MORAD outperforms RiboDiffusion, gRNAde, and RDesign across tertiary structural accuracy, secondary-structure pairing, and thermodynamic quality, establishing a new state of the art in structure-conditioned RNA inverse design.

☆ TopoLIA: Topology-Inspired Latent Interaction Attention for Drug Discovery
Xiang Liu, Yiming Ren, Mustafa Hajij, Pietro Liò, Guo-Wei Wei
The growing sophistication of drug discovery calls for new advances in artificial intelligence (AI). Transformers have underpinned recent breakthroughs in AI, yet their conventional source-target attention mechanism can become a bottleneck for modeling complex interactions. Here, we introduce TopoLIA, a topology-inspired latent interaction attention mechanism that learns not only how much information should be exchanged between interaction partners, but also how that information should be transformed. Source-target-dependent attention is mediated through a compact latent interaction space, providing a structured representation of complex interactions. We further incorporate topological spectral representations to capture multiscale data geometry and extract algebraic relationships. TopoLIA thus incorporates topological principles into both data representation and neural network architecture. We demonstrate TopoLIA on protein-ligand binding, where persistent spectra encode multiscale topological and geometric patterns in 3D protein-ligand complexes, while latent interaction attention (LIA) enables structured, source-target-dependent information exchange. We pretrain TopoLIA by reconstructing masked multiscale spectral tokens from unlabeled molecular structures, yielding transferable structural representations without task-specific annotations. Across multiple scoring and docking benchmarks, TopoLIA achieves state-of-the-art performance and consistently outperforms its standard-attention counterpart. Rank analyses show that compact latent dimensions yield the best performance, supporting compact interaction spaces as an effective inductive bias for biomolecular learning. More broadly, LIA provides a general framework for structured, source-target-dependent information exchange that is applicable to diverse scientific and AI problems involving complex heterogeneous interactions.

☆ PhySeqNet: physics-informed fluorescent foci detection for BARseq spatial transcriptomics
Neha Goswami, John Hover, Renae Galluccio, Yichen Wu, Huiqing Zhan, Xiaoyin Chen, Anthony M. Zador
BARseq enables spatially resolved gene-expression profiling across intact tissue, but its sensitivity depends on accurate detection and decoding of dense fluorescent rolonies. Here we present PhySeqNet, a physics-informed deep learning framework for annotation-free fluorescent foci detection in BARseq. These results show that physics-informed simulation can train practical, annotation-free detectors that improve BARseq sensitivity without disrupting downstream biological interpretation.

☆ RetroTAC: Learning Route-Derived Synthetic Accessibility for PROTACs
Stefano Ribes, Jia Xin, Zhu Zhu, Tingting Mo, Lukáš Hudec, Kostas Papadopoulos, Atanas Patronov, Rocío Mercado
Proteolysis-targeting chimeras (PROTACs) enable targeted protein degradation, expanding therapeutic opportunities beyond conventional inhibition. However, assembling their large, multifunctional structures creates synthetic challenges that hinder their discovery. Traditional synthetic-accessibility scores can overestimate this difficulty by penalizing PROTACs' molecular complexity while overlooking modular assembly from accessible building blocks. In contrast, retrosynthesis planning tools provide explicit routes but are computationally expensive, particularly for large molecules. Here, we introduce a human-interpretable, PROTAC-oriented route-derived score that rewards short synthetic depth and balanced joining of molecular fragments, and RetroTAC, a machine-learning surrogate that predicts this score directly from molecular structures. These results support RetroTAC as a fast prioritization tool for virtual PROTAC libraries, connecting molecular screening with interpretable route characteristics while reserving explicit planning for selected candidates.

☆ Double-Take: Improving Capacity, Binding, and Selectivity In Long-Context Within Bidirectional Memory
Vakul Raghavan, Utkarsh Grover, Mingyang Mao, Chengwen Wang, Xiaomin Lin
Processing a long sequence does not guarantee that distant information remains usable. As context grows, a fixed-size memory must retain many associations, connect information across positions, and preserve relevant structure without being overwhelmed by everything it observes. These are distinct requirements: a memory may store more information without representing the relationships needed for retrieval, and it may transport those relationships without preserving the ones that matter. Thus, the challenge is not simply extending context length, but constructing a memory that remains usable as context grows. We study this problem in large-chunk test-time training (TTT) and identify two basic failures: single-step writes accumulate interference, limiting capacity, while self-associative writes cannot form cross-position bindings. We introduce Double-Take (DT), which repairs these failures with multi-step unpinned updates and cross-position mixing, and adds bidirectional change-sensitive writes for selective retention. Capacity alone leaves end-to-end recall at the $0.10$ chance floor; adding binding raises it to $0.92$. Trained only on $32\,\mathrm{kb}$ genomic contexts, DT reaches $0.927$ AUROC at $131\,\mathrm{kb}$ and $0.856$ near $1\,\mathrm{Mb}$, while transferring zero-shot from human to mouse. Together, these results identify capacity, binding, and selectivity as three requirements for usable long-range memory.

☆ Beyond Discrimination: Evaluating Probability Calibration in Radiomics-Image Fusion for Renal Cell Carcinoma Classification
Yuan Liang, Morgan May, Abraham Campbell, Sourav Bhattacharjee, Simon Caton, Pierpaolo Dondio

☆ DISCERN: Identifiable Disease Evidence for Generalizable Medical Multimodal Learning
Xiaoguang Zhu, Pengyi Jiang, Juncen Guo, Jingyi Wu, Lianlong Sun, Yang Liu
Clinical multimodal models can learn enrollment shortcuts shared across modalities and fail on new cohorts. We model these data through a latent structural causal model: disease state and background factors generate physiology through shared mechanisms, while cohorts differ in selection and measurement. Holding acquisition fixed, we identify conditional disease evidence without observing disease-state labels or recovering the full causal model. In common Gaussian coordinates, cross-cohort state alignment and natural-parameter changes identify a minimal selection statistic; conditioning on it removes enrollment-dependent evidence and yields an identifiable reference posterior within the specified shift family. State-dependent interactions can carry evidence invisible to individual-modality posteriors, motivating joint, information-preserving representations. DISCERN combines an information-lattice encoder, a latent-state model, and an outcome head through joint training. Together, SONIC establishes a shared language for integrating molecular maps across studies and technologies.

☆ La-Ribo: RNA Co-Design via Geometry-Latent Flow Matching

RNA function arises from the coupling of nucleotide sequence and three-dimensional structure, motivating their joint design. We introduce La-Ribo, a generative framework for RNA sequence-structure co-design via geometry-latent flow matching. Kartik Ayyer, Tej Varma Yenupuri, Johan Bielecki, Lutz A. Eichacker, J. Domingo Meza-Aguilar, Louise J. Persson, Lena Worbs, Patrick Adams, Roberto Alvarez, Adil Ansari, Alfredo Bellisario, Rebecca Boll, Carl Caleman, Sebastian Cardoch, Andrea Castoldi, Joe P. J. Chen, Cyril Danilevski, Merle Dechering, Simon Dold, Armando D. Estillore, Alberto De Fanis, Lukas V. Haas, Patrick Konold, David Lomitze, Abhishek Mall, Dimitra Manatou, Iñaki de Diego Martinez, Parichita Mazumder, Andrew J. Morgan, Anna Munke, Yevheniy Ovcharenko, Matteo Porro, Safi Rafie-Zinedine, Amit K. Samanta, Christina Schmidt, Arezu Sehati, Jonas A. Sellberg, Björn Senfftleben, Carolin Seuring, Zhou Shen, Nicusor Timneanu, Sergey Usenko, Daniel Westphal, August Wollter, Tamme Wollweber, Paul Lourdu Xavier, Henry N. Chapman, Janos Hajdu, Richard A. Kirian, Jochen Küpper, Erik G. Marklund, Petra Fromme, Michael Meyer, Filipe R. N. C. Maia
The prospect of imaging single biomolecules, viruses and cells with intense, ultrashort X-ray pulses has driven the development of X-ray free-electron lasers (XFELs). However, the weak scattering from small particles is easily swamped by background from residual gas, which has so far limited applications to strongly scattering targets such as viruses, cell organelles and cells. Here we report a three-dimensional (3D) reconstruction of an isolated 1-MDa membrane-protein complex, photosystem I (PS I), from single-particle diffraction data. PS I trimers were aerosolised by charge-reduction electrospray ionisation and injected into the European XFEL beam, with partial helium gas exchange reducing background scattering by 80%. These results show that membrane-protein complexes can be imaged in vacuo with X-ray lasers, an important step towards ultrafast diffractive imaging of single macromolecules.
♻ ☆ Leveraging secondary-structure information for accurate nucleic acid structure prediction with OFoldNA
Valhalla Team
Recent advances in biomolecular structure prediction have enabled accurate modelling of increasingly complex molecular systems. Secondary structure (SS) provides a more readily available layer of structural information that captures base-pairing relationships and folding topology. Here we present OFoldNA, an all-atom diffusion model that incorporates SS information into nucleic acid folding and protein--nucleic acid co-folding. ♻ ☆ Non-Markovain Quantum State Diffusion for the Tunneling in SARS-COVID-19 virus
Muhammad Waqas Haseeb, Mohamad Toutounji
In the context of biology, unlike the comprehensively established Standard Model in physics, many biological processes lack a complete theoretical framework and are often described phenomenologically. A pertinent example is olfaction -- the process through which humans and animals distinguish various odors. The conventional biological explanation for olfaction relies on the lock and key model, which, while useful, does not fully account for all observed phenomena. As an alternative or complement to this model, vibration-assisted electron tunneling has been proposed. Drawing inspiration from the vibration-assisted electron tunneling model for olfaction, we have developed a theoretical model for electron tunneling in SARS-CoV-2 virus infection within a non-Markovian framework. We approach this by solving the non-Markovian quantum stochastic Schrodinger equation. In our model, the spike protein and the GPCR receptor are conceptualized as a dimer, utilizing the spin-Boson model to facilitate the description of electron tunneling. Our analysis demonstrates that electron tunneling in this context exhibits inherently non-Markovian characteristics, extending into the intermediate and strong coupling regimes between the dimer components. This behavior stands in stark contrast to predictions from Markovian models, which fail to accurately describe electron tunneling in the strong coupling limit. Notably, Markovian approximations often lead to unphysical negative probabilities in this regime, underscoring their limitations and highlighting the necessity of incorporating non-Markovian dynamics for a more realistic description of biological quantum processes. This approach not only broadens our understanding of viral infection mechanisms but also enhances the biological accuracy and relevance of our theoretical framework in describing complex biological interactions.

♻ ☆ Bayesian Sampling of Structural Ensembles: The Role of Ensemble-Counting Measures
Ivan Gilardoni, Giovanni Bussi
Structural ensemble refinement is widely used to integrate molecular simulations with experimental measurements. While most applications focus on the maximum-a-posteriori (MAP) ensemble, Bayesian sampling of the posterior distribution can provide uncertainty estimates and posterior averages for arbitrary observables. A notable step in this direction was introduced by the Bayesian Energy Landscape Tilting (BELT) framework, where sampling is performed on a family of maximum-entropy ensembles parametrized by Lagrange multipliers. Here, we show that Bayesian sampling in this setting requires an explicit choice of ensemble-counting measure. In particular, the flat measure in Lagrange-multiplier space used in the original BELT formulation leads to a posterior distribution that is formally non-normalizable for finite reference trajectories. We propose the Jeffreys measure as an invariant ensemble-counting prescription, restoring normalizability in the finite-sample situations considered here, and providing a consistent definition of posterior averages. Using both an analytically tractable Gaussian model and maximum-entropy refinement of RNA oligomer simulations, we compare different ensemble-counting measures and show that they can significantly affect Bayesian estimates. The resulting methodology has been implemented in the \texttt{MDRefine} software package.

☆ Elucidating the Space of Enzymatic Reaction: A Unified Benchmark and Pretrained Model
Yutong Hu, Tianming Huang, Yanbo Zhao, Qiongyu Zhang, Shixiang Tang, Lei Bai, Ziyi Zhou, Liang Hong, Pan Tan
Existing reaction models primarily learn molecular transformations, whereas enzy- matic reactions depend jointly on molecular structure and catalytic function. Across benchmark tasks and challenging generalization splits, VenusRX achieves the best or competitive performance on most evaluated settings over representative chem- ical and enzymatic baselines. Together, VenusRX-Bench and VenusRX provide a unified framework for elucidating and modeling enzymatic reaction space
☆ Certified Scalable Enclosures for Uncertain Underdetermined Systems
Rudra Prakash, Shaunak Sen
The central challenge in underdetermined models with bounded uncertainty, such as in nonlinear design and estimation, is certifying the solution sets. Conventional solution methodologies, such as those based on Newton's method or on sampling-based uncertainty quantification, are either not applicable due to the underdetermined nature or do not give guarantees that all solutions have been found, assuming they converge. We addressed this issue for a problem that arises in nonlinear design, where a target steady-state box is prescribed and the parameters have to be found. We developed a trapezoidal linearisation method that rigorously encloses all solutions and combined it with a tractable linear programming method to compute the component-wise bounds. We showed that the resulting families of linear programs can iteratively contract an initial parameter region. A subdivision of the target state and the initial parameter region can improve the contraction and provide tighter enclosures. The trapezoidal relaxation gives a family of linear programs that can also be used for finding solutions for interval linear systems via the Oettli-Prager characterisation. We demonstrate the generality of the method through different applications, including nonlinear design in biomolecular circuits, a sensitivity analysis method, and in a compressed sensing context, and discuss the trade-off between enclosure tightness and computational cost.

☆ Cross-species representation learning aligns mouse and human neural dynamics and tracks clinical drug efficacy
Marko Tvrdic, Justin Richmond Domingo, Jae Ann Buenaluz, Jydell Ashley Palomo Penollar, Edmayelle Villavicencio Alforja, Jobi Fallaeria Subosa, Gabriel Ocana-Santero
Preclinical models poorly predict human drug efficacy, particularly in neurological disorders. Neural activity offers a uniquely rich source of translational information because it captures high-dimensional variation in nervous-system function that can be measured in both animals and humans. However, its high dimensionality makes it difficult to distinguish conserved disease-related features from variation arising from species, recording modality and experimental context. Here, we test whether shared neural dynamics can be identified directly from electrophysiology data by learning representations organized by biological state rather than species. We develop a dual-rule contrastive learning framework that aligns corresponding mouse and human states while preserving separation between distinct phenotypes. This framework recovered conserved sensory-response structure across species and, in epilepsy, resolved distinct relationships between three mouse models and heterogeneous human patient populations. When treated animals were projected into a frozen cross-species representation, drug-induced movement towards the human-aligned healthy state retrospectively tracked known clinical efficacy across ten model-drug combinations including a disease-specific detrimental effect. The framework also identified shared disease-associated neural dynamics between Fmr1-knockout mice and human 16p11.2 copy-number variant carriers despite differences in genetic aetiology and recording modality. Together, these findings show the potential of cross-species neural representation learning to map heterogeneous human disease onto experimentally tractable preclinical states and assess whether interventions restore human-relevant circuit function.

☆ Cohort Effects, Not Treatment Response, Dominate Public Immunotherapy Cohorts: Evidence from Microbiome and Tumor Genomic Data
Amey Garg
Researchers often pool public gut microbiome datasets from immunotherapy studies to get around small sample sizes, assuming that adding cohorts adds statistical power faster than it adds differences between studies. We test that assumption. We processed four public shotgun metagenomic cohorts of patients receiving immune checkpoint blockade through one pipeline and pooled them step by step, from 39 to 283 patients. Because the PERMANOVA effect size R^2 is above zero even when there is no real effect, we compared every result with its exact chance expectation. Measured as raw variance explained, cohort membership accounted for 8 to 27 times more variance than treatment response, although much of that ratio comes from sample size alone. At every pooling step, treatment response explained no more variance than chance (within 0.2 percentage points) and was never significant, while cohort membership explained 6.0 to 9.5 percentage points beyond chance (p = 0.001 each time). Classification stayed at chance across three model types, none of six leak-free batch-correction strategies recovered signal, and no genus reached significance in a random-effects meta-analysis. The largest single cohort (n = 165) was also non-significant, although simulation shows it had at least 90% power to detect an effect at its own 95% upper bound; within it, recruiting site alone explained 7.0 percentage points beyond chance. In three tumor genomic cohorts (n = 223), a small but real response effect sat next to a cohort effect about eighteen times larger, so the problem is not specific to microbiome data. We release batch_detector, an open tool that reports chance-corrected cohort and response effects before pooling, and test it on an independent colorectal cancer meta-analysis.

♻ ☆ Reliability assessment and multicenter clinical application of magnetic resonance methods for knee cartilage quantification

♻ ☆ Parameter Identifiability beyond the Fisher Information Approximation in Data-Driven Modeling
Shun Wang, Wenrui Hao
Data-driven mechanistic modeling of high-dimensional biological data requires rigorous parameter identifiability. Profile likelihood entails costly repeated optimization, while Fisher-information criteria discard the model's Hessian and may misjudge weakly constrained directions. From a well-posedness definition of identifiability we characterize the stability of the estimated parameters under data perturbations while retaining the full Hessian. It yields Hölder-$\tfrac12$ stability for a zero residual when the Fisher information is degenerate and corrects the Fisher matrix otherwise; residual and perturbation sizes decide the order of stability, Lipschitz below a crossover scale proportional to the residual norm and Hölder-$\tfrac12$ above. In a local quadratic approximation we derive coordinate identifiability metrics allowing singular Jacobians and nuisance Hessian blocks, and spectral bounds link the corrections to parameter and prediction uncertainty. Applications to JAK--STAT signaling, HIV dynamics, and amyloid-$β$ accumulation support the theory. Requiring only local derivatives, the framework offers a tractable basis for identifiability analysis of large-scale models.

☆ De novo design of monoclonal and bispecific antibodies with OFAntibody
Valhalla Team
Recent advances in generative protein design have enabled de novo antibody generation with explicit target and epitope conditioning. However, most existing approaches remain formulated around a single antigen-antibody interface, whereas bispecific antibody design requires modeling multi-component complexes in which multiple target-recognition interfaces must coexist and interact within a shared antibody structure. Here we present OFAntibody, an all-atom generative framework for de novo design of monoclonal and bispecific antibodies. OFAntibody further enables the same target combination to be explored across different antibody formats, while joint multi-interface generation reduces geometric incompatibilities arising from independent design and post hoc assembly. Together, these results extend de novo antibody design from binary antigen-antibody complexes to programmable multi-component complexes, providing a foundation for designing single molecules that combine recognition of distinct targets and their associated biological functions.
☆ MD-LLM-2: A Transferable Language Model of Molecular Dynamics with Physical Conditioning and Explicit Path Probabilities
Mhd Hussein Murtada, Z. Faidon Brotzakis, Michele Vendruscolo
Molecular dynamics (MD) simulations model the motion of proteins between conformational states, but crossing free energy barriers and sampling multiple thermodynamic conditions remain computationally costly. We recently introduced MD-LLM-1, which showed that language models can generate conformational trajectories at lower cost, but required a separate model for each protein and did not explicitly model thermodynamics or kinetics. Here we introduce MD-LLM-2, a transferable language model that generates trajectories conditioned on sequence, temperature and molecular history. Because each step defines a normalized distribution over structural tokens, the model assigns an explicit likelihood to every generated or simulated trajectory. Trained across mdCATH domains, MD-LLM-2 reproduces the median radius of gyration of MD within 1 Angstrom in 10 of 12 unseen domains, predicts reduced Trp-cage compaction at higher temperature, and generates L99A T4 lysozyme trajectories that reach published transition-region backbone structures. In alanine dipeptide simulations, conditioning on a torsional scaling parameter $λ$ predicts basin populations at unseen couplings with a mean absolute error of 0.09 versus 0.35 for MBAR, and first-passage probabilities with an error of 0.08 versus 0.16 for a reversible Markov state model. Frame-isoentropic sampling further limits the accumulation of errors during autoregressive trajectory generation. Together, these results extend MD-LLM from protein-specific trajectory generation to transferable, physically conditioned modelling with explicit path likelihoods, and show that the framework can predict equilibrium populations for force-field parameters not seen during training, with an initial indication that first-passage kinetics can also be captured.
♻ ☆ UNAAGI: Atom-Level Diffusion for Generating Non-Canonical Amino Acid Substitutions
Han Tang, Alexander Tong, Wouter Boomsma
A central challenge in structural biology is identifying beneficial amino acid substitutions, a problem that underpins both mutational effect prediction and protein engineering. ♻ ☆ Frame-invariant topological representations of trabecular bone microarchitecture for strength prediction
John Rick Manzanares, Richard Leslie Abel, Paweł Dłotko

Twenty-four human trabecular cores were analyzed using persistence images, directional Betti tensors, and nested ridge regression over 13 validation groups. The directional construction combines cone occupancy, directional covariance, and principal axis degeneracy. Equal bone removal experiments and a pair closely matched in morphometry were used to examine whether topological differences tracked changes in simulated elastic stiffness. ☆ Information-Dense Synthesis for Molecular Discovery
Kasper K. Jakobsen, Eli N. Weinstein
Machine learning can accelerate molecular discovery by designing molecules and planning experiments. However, many scientific challenges demand molecules with very rare properties, and in this sparse setting, existing algorithms offer little gain over random guessing. We propose a method to efficiently search large regions of molecular space using algorithmically controlled stochastic synthesis. Rather than design, make and test individual molecules, we design and make complex mixtures, test them as a pool, then deconvolute the molecule-activity map. We optimize synthesis to encode maximal information. Theoretically, this approach can reduce the number of experiments required to find the optimal molecule among $d$ candidates from $\mathcal{O}(d)$ to $\mathcal{O}(\log d)$ or $\mathcal{O}(1)$. In simulation, on estimated protein fitness landscapes, it finds active molecules with an order of magnitude fewer experiments than existing Bayesian optimization methods.
☆ Mathematical Invariant-Enabled Topological Neural Networks for Molecular and Materials Property Prediction
Yiming Ren, Xiang Liu, Mustafa Hajij, Pietro Liò, Guo-Wei Wei
Existing molecular and materials learning approaches often rely on a limited set of structural representations, which may capture only selected aspects of complex three-dimensional structure.
